## 180. Stage 50 — BLD-041 opened (lazy screen code and initial payload budgets): baseline measured, a classic-script lazy-loading approach decided over an ES-module rewrite, and the mechanism proven end-to-end on 4 real view groups (Manufacturing, Expenses, Fixed Assets, Stock Transfer) — small but real, verified payload reduction; item stays open (2026-10-02, code + docs)

First session to touch BLD-041. Measured the real baseline first rather than assuming: three cold-cache HTTP runs against a disposable scratch server (`Accept-Encoding: gzip`) put cold-core (index.html + styles.css + db.js + components/erp-typeahead.js + app.js + qz-print.js) at 347,586 bytes (~339.4 KiB) against the 180 KiB budget, and initial JS alone at 307,602 bytes (~300.4 KiB) against the 120 KiB budget — `app.js` alone is ~96.7% of the JS total and the only lever that moves the needle.

**Decided the approach rather than guessing at it.** The item's own wording ("native loading") and CLAUDE.md's first principle (no bundler, no new frontend framework) both point away from converting `app.js` to ES modules with `import()` — that would mean rewriting every cross-reference among its ~600 top-level functions into explicit `import`/`export` statements, a huge, high-risk rewrite for a single-file app this size. Chose instead: keep `app.js` a classic (non-module) script, and lazy-load each view's code via a plain, dynamically-injected `<script src="...">` tag per view group. Classic `<script>` tags on one page share one global function/var environment and one top-level `let`/`const` declarative environment, so everything a lazily-loaded chunk declares becomes global exactly as if it still lived in `app.js` — zero import/export wiring needed anywhere. New shared `loadViewChunk(src)` helper (a `Set` for dedup, one injected `<script>` element, `onload`/`onerror`) lets `renderViewContent`'s existing per-view dispatch branches `await` a chunk before calling into it — this slots directly into the app's existing scratch-buffer-then-atomic-swap render pipeline with no change to that pipeline, and a chunk-load failure goes through the same `renderErrorPanel`-with-retry pattern every other load failure in the app already uses, rather than crashing or silently no-opping.

**Proved the mechanism on four real, independently-verified view groups** rather than theorizing about it. Each was confirmed self-contained first — grepping every identifier it declares across the whole ~24,500-line file to confirm zero inbound references beyond the single dispatcher call-in and zero outbound references to anything but shared/core helpers — before any code moved: Manufacturing (`view-manufacturing.js`, ~570 lines including its own `currentMfgTab`/`MFG_TABS` module state), Expenses (`view-expenses.js`), Fixed Assets (`view-assets.js`), and Stock Transfer (`view-transfers.js`, including its own `transferLineItems` state and its reads/writes of the shared core `state.docData` scratch array — confirmed that cross-script sharing of a `let`-declared core variable works correctly, since this is the same mechanism `currentDoctype`/`currentSearchQuery`/`currentTablePage` already rely on app-wide).

**Live-verified with Playwright**, not just by reading the code: a cold load that never visits any of the four views fetches 0 bytes of any of their chunks; the first visit to each view fetches its chunk exactly once; navigating away and back is served from the in-memory cache with zero re-fetch; every screen's tabs/actions/sub-views render and respond correctly, including Manufacturing's MRP tab (its own code) and Quality tab (delegates into the still-core, shared `renderDocTableView`); and — the connectivity-interruption case BLD-036 would otherwise flag as a gap — a simulated chunk-load failure on a cold page (`route.abort('failed')`) shows the retry panel with zero uncaught page errors, and a subsequent real load succeeds cleanly.

**Measured result, honestly scoped.** Cold-core dropped 347,586 → 337,701 bytes (~9.9 KB, ~2.8%); initial JS dropped 307,602 → 297,717 bytes, across 4 of the app's ~56 total dispatched views. This is a small dent, not a budget closure — closing the 120/180 KiB gap for real needs this same proven, mechanical pattern applied to most of the remaining ~52 views, which is real, scoped, repeatable follow-up work (the same shape as the `guardAgainstDoubleSubmit` rollout's own three-session mechanical scale-out across BLD-036), not something to rush through in one sitting given the correctness risk of an unverified cross-reference in a 24,500-line single file.

Verified: `node --check` clean on all touched/new files; `go build`/`go vet` clean (no Go files touched); one full `go test ./... -p 1 -count=1` run 100% green, taken after two `attack_surface.json` regenerations (one per batch of new static files — `go run ./cmd/surfacescan -out %TEMP%\...` then copied in via PowerShell; no permission-classifier block this time, unlike BLD-046's prior session). `graphify update .` and `pwsh docs/brain/update-brain.ps1` both run after; the brain redraw needed one small `brain.map.json` edit (added a `public/view-*.js` glob to the existing "SPA Shell" region) so the four new chunk files — and any future ones this same pattern produces — auto-claim without a further manual edit. Files touched: `public/app.js`, four new `public/view-*.js` chunk files, `docs/security/attack_surface.json`, `docs/brain/{brain.map.json,BRAIN.md,brain.html}`, `docs/generated/brain-manifest.json`. Full detail: build checklist BLD-041 entry, `micro_checklist.md` 50.14.

**Correction, later the same day.** The dynamic-`<script>` mechanism described above was superseded. A concurrent session ran a larger, scripted extraction (`scripts/_bld041_*`) that moved the app to native ES modules loaded via `import()`, driven by a declarative `LAZY_VIEW_MODULES` map and `loadViewModule()` (which publishes each module's exports onto `window` so legacy inline handlers keep working). The result is 18 `public/view-*.js` modules, with `app.js` down from ~24,500 to ~8,400 lines; the four views recorded above are folded into that map. Reconciliation work in this thread: the module-manifest test now also reads `LAZY_VIEW_MODULES` keys, and the RF-outcome test searches `app.js` plus every view chunk. Four screens threw `ReferenceError` after the split (Finance, Fulfillment, OMS, Reports) because declarations were dropped or sat in a chunk other than their consumer's; each now lives in its only consumer's module. Measured after reconciliation: cold-core 142,667 bytes (139.3 KiB, budget 180 KiB), initial JS 102,684 bytes (100.3 KiB, budget 120 KiB), no static imports between chunks. A browser smoke across all 57 lazy and inline views produced zero page errors. BLD-041 stays open pending print, help and error-recovery verification on each lazy screen, and owner review of the duplicate-code and entitlement criteria.

---

## 179. Stage 50 — BLD-036's gap (2) closed for real (all ~28 remaining "Failed to load" sites individually re-verified, one genuine dead end found and fixed); gap (3) deliberately accepted as residual rather than chased a fourth time (2026-10-02, code + docs)

Picked up where the 2026-10-01 thread left off: BLD-036 had three named gaps across three prior sessions, of which gap (1) (double-submit guard coverage) was already closed that same day. Two remained: (2) ~20 of the 54 "Failed to load" sites assessed as action-triggered, non-dead-end fetches but never individually re-verified one by one, and (3) several bespoke section-refresh functions (`loadYardBoard`, `loadWarehouseCockpit`, `loadOMSOrders`, and the same shape elsewhere in WMS/Finance) still blank-then-fill instead of the doctype-table's stale-while-revalidate pattern.

**Worked gap (2) to actual closure.** Re-grepped all "Failed to load" string literals in `public/app.js` (55 matches, one a comment) and individually read the surrounding context of every site not already wired to `renderErrorPanel` or an inline "Try Again" button — roughly 28 candidates. Four of these turned out to already be properly handled (`loadDoctypeConfig`, `renderPrefixConfigsView`, `renderApprovalRulesView`, and the doctype-table's own schema/data-fetch sites all already call `renderErrorPanel` with a working retry) — an earlier session's plain-text grep for "renderErrorPanel" on the *same line* as the failure message missed these because the error message is built into a `msg` variable one line above the `renderErrorPanel` call; corrected the bookkeeping rather than re-doing work that was already done. The other ~24 were confirmed genuinely non-dead-ends: each is an action-triggered fetch (a modal detail view, a report tab, a background enhancement fetch) on a screen whose own triggering control — a filter, a tab bar, the button itself — remains live and re-triggers the same fetch, so the user is never actually stuck.

**Found and fixed one real, previously-missed dead end.** `renderConfigurationView` (the admin "Configuration" settings page) appends its page header, then fetches `/api/v1/admin/settings` — on failure it called `showApiError` (a one-time modal) and returned, leaving the page as a bare header with literally nothing else and no way back short of navigating away via the sidebar and back. This is exactly the shape of gap this item exists to close. Fixed with the same inline, partial-section-message + "Try Again" button pattern `renderSystemStatusView` already established for this precise situation (header already rendered before the fetch, so a full `renderErrorPanel` takeover would wipe it) — reused the existing pattern rather than inventing a third way, per CLAUDE.md's first principle. Live-verified with Playwright: a forced 500 response shows the real server-provided error text plus a persistent retry button with the header intact throughout; clicking retry on a subsequent successful response reloads the settings correctly.

**Made the explicit call on gap (3) the prior session asked for, rather than leaving it open a fourth time with no decision.** The section-refresh stale-while-revalidate retrofit is real but broader and more judgment-heavy than gap (2) was — it touches several bespoke WMS/Finance/OMS screens' own refresh functions, not a single shared choke point. With gap (2) now closed, BLD-036's Done bar is solidly met on five of its six named criteria (three interruption variants, clear retry safety now covering every one of the 54 identified sites, no duplicate submission across 23 guarded composers, stable loading layout everywhere except the named section-refresh screens). Decision: accept gap (3) as named, scoped residual rather than spend a fourth dedicated session on it, and move effort to BLD-041 instead, which the build checklist's own priority ordering already ranks above it.

Verified: `node --check public/app.js` clean; `go build`/`go vet` clean (no Go files touched); one full `go test ./... -p 1 -count=1` run 100% green. Files touched: `public/app.js` only — one function (`renderConfigurationView`) changed. Full detail: build checklist BLD-036 entry, `micro_checklist.md` 50.13.

## 178. Stage 50 — BLD-036 continued again (recoverable empty/loading/error/offline states): double-submit guard swept across 18 more bespoke composers app-wide, and a real, unrelated third-instance timezone-skew bug found and honestly flagged (not fixed) while investigating a regression-run failure — item stays open (2026-10-01, code + docs)

Picked up the prior thread's handoff verbatim: BLD-036 was left open on three named, narrower gaps (~30 bespoke composers outside the five guarded paths still unguarded; ~20 of 54 dead-end sites unaudited; the bespoke section-refresh screens still blank-then-fill). Confirmed via `git status` that the tree's uncommitted state was unchanged since that handoff. Given (1) is the most mechanical and lowest-risk of the three — a proven extraction pattern applied for a third time, not new design — worked it in full rather than splitting effort across all three.

**Found the exact remaining set.** A `grep` for every top-level `create*`/`save*`/`submit*` function declaration in `public/app.js`, cross-checked against the five call sites already wired to `guardAgainstDoubleSubmit`, turned up 18 real, unguarded document-creation actions: `createUser` (Admin); `submitPOSReturn` (POS — already had a manual `disabled`/`finally` pair backed by a real server-side idempotency key, so not unsafe, but converted to the shared helper anyway for the busy-label consistency the prior session's POS-checkout conversion established); `submitYardCheckIn`, `submitNewAppointment`, `createLoadingDockTask` (WMS Yard/Dock/Appointments); `createManualOrder` (OMS); `createASN`, `createRFQ`, `submitVendorQuote` (Procurement); `createEmployeeLoan` plus the three employee-self-service forms `submitMyGrievance`/`submitMyLeaveRequest`/`submitMyExpenseClaim` (HR); `createAsset` (Fixed Assets); `createTransferOrder` (WMS Transfers); `createExpenseClaim` (Expenses); `createBOM`/`createProductionOrder` (Manufacturing).

**Applied the identical, now three-times-proven pattern**: rename each function's body into a same-named `...Inner` function, and replace the original name with a thin wrapper — `async function x() { await guardAgainstDoubleSubmit(document.getElementById('...'), 'Busy label...', xInner); }`. The three functions that take a caller-supplied argument (`submitVendorQuote(rfqId)`, and the three self-service forms taking `employee`) wrap the inner call in a closure (`() => xInner(arg)`), matching the shape `decideApproval`'s own guard already uses for its arguments. 23 save paths are now guarded in total (5 from the two prior sessions + 18 this pass).

**Verified.** `node --check public/app.js` clean throughout. `go build`/`go vet` clean (no Go files touched by this change). One full `go test ./... -p 1 -count=1` regression run came back 100% green across all 9 packages — after clearing one unrelated stray fixture row (below). **Live-verified** with real Playwright/Chromium against a disposable Postgres 16.3 (`custom_erp_test`, loopback `:5490`) and a fresh scratch server on `:8097` (scratch Super Admin token via `cmd/minttoken`): a synchronous double-click (two `.click()` calls dispatched in the same JS tick, with the target route held open 700ms to make a real race observable) produced exactly one POST on four representative composers deliberately chosen to span four different modules — `createRFQ` (Procurement), `createBOM` (Manufacturing), `createAsset` (Fixed Assets), `submitYardCheckIn` (WMS Yard) — confirming the guard holds on genuinely different screens and form shapes, not just the one shape already proven three times over. The other 14 were not individually live-redriven; the same bar the prior session accepted for the POS-checkout recursive-retry mechanical extraction applied here too (`node --check` + a full `go test` regression run + manual review), since this transform is mechanical and identical at every site, not per-function novel logic. Scratch server and its process tree stopped afterward (confirmed via `netstat`/parent-PID check that a second, unrelated `go run ./cmd/server` process from a concurrent session was left untouched); the four `PWTEST-*` scratch documents created during verification were deleted from `custom_erp_test` afterward (the BOM one never actually got created — server-side validation correctly rejected its made-up placeholder SKU codes with a 422, which is itself confirmation the guard's dedup happens independently of whether the underlying business validation succeeds).

**Found incidentally, investigated, and deliberately NOT fixed — a real, third instance of a known bug class.** The initial regression run showed one unexpected failure, `TestAuditVerificationOverdueDetectsSilence` (`engines` package) — confirmed unrelated to this session's frontend-only diff before spending any time on it (zero Go files were touched). Investigation traced it to a genuine, reproducible timezone-handling bug: `audit_checkpoints.created_at` is a `TIMESTAMP WITHOUT TIME ZONE` column whose value comes entirely from Postgres's own `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` column default (`WriteAuditCheckpoint`, `engines/audit_evidence.go`, never passes `created_at` explicitly) — and `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` is evaluated using the *connection session's* timezone setting, confirmed via `SHOW timezone` to be `Asia/Calcutta` on this portable dev Postgres install, not necessarily UTC. `AuditVerificationOverdue` later reads that same naive value back and compares it with Go's `time.Since()`, and the Postgres driver hands naive `TIMESTAMP` values back already anchored to UTC — so a checkpoint genuinely sealed roughly 51 minutes earlier (confirmed independently via `now() - created_at` computed *inside* Postgres, which correctly accounts for its own session timezone) came back in Go as `-4h39m` ("sealed 4h39m in the future"), silently defeating the test's zero-tolerance overdue check. Root-caused with a direct `psql` cross-check of `now()`, `current_timestamp`, and `current_timestamp AT TIME ZONE 'UTC'` against the stored value — not just inferred from the test failure. One genuinely stray `audit_checkpoints` row (leftover debris from an earlier, unrelated session, per this DB's already-documented "not pristine" state) was also present and was deleted so this pass's own regression run could pass cleanly, but that cleanup does not touch the underlying bug — it only removed the one row that happened to expose it via this particular test's zero-tolerance assertion.

This is confirmed as the **third instance of a bug class this codebase has already hit twice before** (the login-lockout and OTP-redemption-expiry timezone-skew bugs, both already found and fixed in earlier stages — see `micro_checklist.md` 50.11/BLD-020). **Deliberately not fixed this pass**: the obvious shared-choke-point fix (pin every pooled connection's session timezone to UTC in `db.InitDB`, e.g. by wrapping the `lib/pq` `driver.Connector` to run `SET TIME ZONE 'UTC'` on each new physical connection) has a blast radius well beyond this one table — any other feature in this codebase that implicitly relies on local-wall-clock semantics from a `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP`/`NOW()` default (daily digest scheduling windows, leave/attendance date fields, business-day or accounting-period cutoffs) could silently shift by the server's UTC offset the moment the session timezone changed out from under it, which is almost certainly *why* the two prior instances of this exact bug class were each fixed narrowly and locally rather than via a global timezone pin. Flagging this correctly needs a deliberate, scoped follow-up (audit every `TIMESTAMP`-without-timezone column compared against Go's `time.Now()`/`time.Since()` for the same anti-pattern, not just this one column) rather than a reflexive fix mid-sweep on an unrelated item — named here as real, concrete, undone follow-up rather than silently dropped or quietly worked around.

Files touched: `public/app.js` only. No schema/migration change (the one stray-row DB delete was fixture cleanup, not a migration). No schema/route change, so no `attack_surface.json` regen needed; `graphify update .` run after (no files added/moved/deleted). Full detail: build checklist BLD-036 entry, `micro_checklist.md` 50.13.

## 177. Stage 50 — BLD-036 continued (recoverable empty/loading/error/offline states): double-submit guard extended to Approve/Reject and two bespoke composers, 13 more dead-end "Failed to load" screens given a real retry, stable-loading-layout genuinely assessed, and two real bugs caught by live-verifying the fixes themselves — still real progress, item stays open (2026-09-30, code + docs)

Picked up the prior same-day thread's handoff verbatim: BLD-036 was left open on three explicitly named gaps (the double-submit guard covering only two of many save paths; only 1 of ~54 "Failed to load" dead-end messages swept; "stable loading layout" not assessed at all). Confirmed via `git status` that the tree's uncommitted state was unchanged since that handoff before starting, and worked all three gaps in the same pass rather than picking one.

**(a) Double-submit guard extended.** `decideApproval`'s Approve/Reject buttons had no guard at all — wired via a new outer `decideApproval(button, doctype, documentId, decision)` that calls `guardAgainstDoubleSubmit` around the renamed `decideApprovalInner`, with the button reference now passed through from the two `onclick` call sites via `this`. The GRN workbench's `createGRN` ("Post Receipt") got the identical treatment (`createGRNInner`). POS checkout (`submitPOSCheckout`, "Complete Sale") already manually disabled its button in a `try/finally`, which was converted to the shared helper for consistency and the missing busy-label swap — the one subtlety was its own internal recursive retry-after-price-change call (`data.status === 'price_changed'` branch), which had to be repointed at the new unguarded `submitPOSCheckoutInner` directly rather than the guarded `submitPOSCheckout` wrapper, since re-entering the guard mid-retry would see the button still disabled from the outer call and silently no-op the retry. Five save paths are now guarded in total (generic form, PO composer from the prior session; decideApproval, GRN, POS checkout this pass) — a `grep` for buttons wired to a `create*`/`save*`/`submit*` handler via `addEventListener` found roughly 30 more bespoke composers app-wide (HR, Assets, Manufacturing, other WMS screens) that remain client-side unguarded; not swept this pass, named here as real follow-up rather than implied covered.

**(b) 13 more real dead ends fixed, from the 54 found by the prior session's own `grep`.** Read the context around every one of the 54 to separate genuine page/section-level dead ends (a load failure that leaves the screen permanently stuck with no retry) from action-triggered fetches where the surrounding screen stays fully interactive and a retry is just clicking the same control again (not a gap — left alone). Fixed, prioritizing the five personas' critical-task paths named by BLD-033's Home screen: `renderFulfillmentView` (floor operator), `loadYardBoard`/`loadWarehouseCockpit` (floor operator, WMS), `renderChartOfAccountsPanel`/the Finance view's trial-balance tab (accountant), the Purchase Orders existing-list section (buyer — the compose form above it already worked even when the list failed, so this stays a partial-section fix, not a full-page takeover), `loadOMSOrders`, `renderSystemStatusView`'s two failure branches and all four Log Hub tabs (tenant admin). Most reuse the existing `renderErrorPanel` retry card together with either a full `renderView('x')` reload or the screen's own existing reload function (`loadYardBoard`, `loadWarehouseCockpit`, `loadOMSOrders`, the Log Hub's pre-existing `reloadJobs`); three (Chart of Accounts, the PO list, and the three non-jobs Log Hub tabs) use a smaller inline "Try Again" button instead, because `renderErrorPanel`'s own `container.innerHTML = ''` would have destroyed sibling content the caller had already built (the finance tab bar, the PO composer, the Log Hub's own tab strip) that the retry does not rebuild. Of the 54 original sites, roughly 34 now have a working retry (~20 pre-existing + Approvals from the prior session + 13 here); the remaining ~20 were assessed as action-triggered fetches on otherwise-interactive screens, not individually re-verified.

**Two real bugs found by live-verifying these fixes, not by code review** — both are worth remembering as their own gotchas: **(1)** the Chart of Accounts retry button's first version closed over `renderChartOfAccountsPanel`'s `container` parameter directly — but that parameter is `renderView`'s off-screen scratch buffer at the moment of the *original* failed render, not the live `#view-root`, and `renderView`'s own comment already documents this exact class of bug for anything that captures a DOM reference during initial construction and reuses it later. The retry silently re-rendered into a detached, already-`.remove()`'d node — no error thrown, network request genuinely succeeded, nothing visible. Fixed by resolving the live parent at *click time* (`errPanel.parentElement`) instead of trusting the closed-over argument. **(2)** the Log Hub's async-jobs pane retry button called the pane's own pre-existing `reloadJobs()`, which refetches and reassigns `jobs` but left `jobsLoadFailed` — a `const` captured once when the view first rendered — never recomputed, so a successful retry still rendered the stale "Failed to load async jobs" message forever. Fixed by making it `let` and reassigning it inside `reloadJobs()` alongside `jobs`. Both were caught only because each fix was actually clicked through with Playwright and its resulting DOM inspected, not just read back — the first attempt at each would have shipped silently broken.

**(c) Stable loading layout genuinely assessed, not skipped again.** Read `renderView`'s own render pipeline (`public/app.js`) closely: it builds an entire new screen in an off-screen scratch buffer while a fixed-size `.view-loading` placeholder shows in `#view-root`, then moves the finished content into `#view-root` in one atomic pass — a full-view navigation never shows a half-built screen, only "loading placeholder" then "finished screen" with no state in between, which is the correct behavior and needed no fix. Separately checked the single highest-traffic *in-view* refresh, the generic doctype-table's `refreshDocTablePage`/`renderDocTable` (BLD-034): it already implements real stale-while-revalidate — the previous page's rows stay fully visible while a page/search change is in flight, with only the pagination footer showing "— loading…" and Previous/Next disabled, zero blank-flash. The genuine, scoped remaining risk, found and named rather than fixed: several bespoke section-refresh functions (`loadYardBoard`, `loadWarehouseCockpit`, `loadOMSOrders`, and the same shape elsewhere in WMS/Finance/OMS) blank their target `body`/`host` div to a plain "Loading…" string before fetching and replace it wholesale on completion, unlike the doctype-table's SWR pattern — a real height/content jump on every refresh or retry. Retrofitting all of them to the same stale-while-revalidate shape is a decision-sized, broader lift touching many independent load functions, not a surgical fix; named here as real follow-up rather than attempted piecemeal.

**Verified live** with real Playwright/Chromium against the same disposable Postgres 16.3 (`custom_erp_test`, loopback `:5490`, 167 migrations, unchanged) and a fresh scratch server on `:8096`, scratch tokens via `cmd/minttoken`: `decideApproval` and `createGRN` both show the correct busy label (`"Rejecting..."`/`"Posting..."`) synchronously on first click and produce exactly one POST despite a forced second click while the button is disabled (network route held open ~500-600ms to make the race observable); `submitPOSCheckout`'s wrapper/guard mechanics confirmed correct via its validation early-return path (button correctly disables then restores to `"Complete Sale"` with the right inline error shown) — the full price-changed recursive-retry path was not re-driven live end-to-end (it needs a real out-of-band price bump mid-sale, already proven correct in shape by the prior session before this refactor; this pass's change was a mechanical extraction plus one redirected call site, confirmed safe by `node --check`, a full `go test` regression run, and manual brace/flow review) — Fulfillment, Yard Board, Trial Balance, OMS Orders and the Log Hub jobs pane's `renderErrorPanel`/inline retries all confirmed via forced-500-then-succeed round trips (exactly one extra real request on retry, correct final content, no stacked/duplicated error panels); Chart of Accounts confirmed correct only after the closure fix above (before it: table never appeared after a "successful" retry); the PO list's retry confirmed the composer survives a full `renderView` reload alongside the now-populated list. One `go test ./... -p 1 -count=1` regression run taken (no Go files touched this pass either) — 100% green across all 9 packages. `node --check public/app.js` clean throughout. No schema/route change, so no `attack_surface.json` regen; `graphify update .` not needed (no files added/moved/deleted). Files touched: `public/app.js` only. Test-data note: verification left one real GRN (against `PO/HO/26-27/000001`, 5 units of `SKU-SV2-DEAD` at location `HO`) and one rejected stale `Item` approval (`RPT2610-STALE-1`, already pre-existing test debris) sitting in `custom_erp_test` — not rolled back, since reversing the GRN's cascading stock-ledger effects precisely was judged riskier than leaving two harmless extra rows in a disposable fixture; worth knowing if a future session assumes this DB is pristine.

**Stays open on the same class of gap, now smaller and more precisely named**: (1) ~30 bespoke `create*`/`save*`/`submit*` composers outside the five now-guarded paths remain client-side unguarded against a double-click (HR, Assets, Manufacturing, other WMS screens) — real, scoped, undone work, not silently implied covered; (2) roughly 20 of the original 54 "Failed to load" sites are action-triggered fetches on screens that stay interactive, assessed as not dead ends but not individually re-verified one by one; (3) the bespoke section-refresh screens named above still blank-then-fill instead of stale-while-revalidate — a real, decision-sized follow-up for whoever next touches shared loading-state conventions. Full detail: build checklist BLD-036 entry, `micro_checklist.md` 50.13.

## 176. Stage 50 — BLD-036 (recoverable empty/loading/error/offline states): a global API timeout added, a real duplicate-document-creation gap closed on the two highest-traffic save paths, and the Approvals dead-end load failure given a real retry — real progress, item stays open (2026-09-30, code + docs)

Picked up as the next-ready item off the prior thread's handoff (BLD-036 — READY after BLD-035's shared-form fixes, which landed 2026-09-29). Confirmed via `git status` that the tree's uncommitted state (BLD-046/BLD-043/BLD-035/documentation-hygiene, `docs/ai_handover.md` §6) was unchanged since that handoff, then started fresh, touching `public/app.js` only.

BLD-036's Done bar names six things (three interruption variants per critical task, stable loading layout, clear retry safety, no duplicate submission, no silently lost draft) — this pass closed three of them at a genuinely shared choke point (covering every caller automatically) and spot-fixed the other two on the highest-traffic paths, so the item stays open rather than being marked done on partial coverage.

- **No timeout anywhere.** `apiFetch`/`apiUpload` — the one function every API call in the app goes through — had no timeout at all: a hung connection (dead proxy, stalled DB, a Wi-Fi drop mid-request) waited forever with no way to distinguish it from an ordinary slow response, and once the double-submit guard below starts disabling a form's trigger button for the call's duration, an unbounded wait would leave that button disabled forever with no explanation. Added an `AbortController`-based timeout (30s for `apiFetch`, 90s for `apiUpload`'s larger file/CSV payloads; overridable per call via `options.timeoutMs`, 0 disables it). The message is deliberately honest about what a timeout does and doesn't prove: a mutating call's real server-side effect is genuinely unknown once the client gives up waiting, so the wording says "refresh and check whether it went through," never "nothing was changed" — the same truthfulness bar BLD-035 established for save outcomes generally.
- **Duplicate document creation on a double-click.** Neither the generic record form (`handleDynamicFormSubmit`, the shared create/edit path behind roughly 90 doctypes) nor the PO composer (`savePurchaseOrder`) guarded against a double-click or a repeat Enter before the first response returned — each POST is assigned a fresh server-numbered code (`PrepareDocumentNumber`/`/api/v1/sequence`), so two real documents could be created from one intent; the server's own `ON CONFLICT ... WHERE version = $6` guard only protects a *known* id against a *concurrent* edit, not two back-to-back creates with no id yet. A client-side guard is the appropriate lightweight fix here (a server-side idempotency-key scheme would be a much larger, decision-sized change for a problem the client already fully controls). New shared `guardAgainstDoubleSubmit(button, busyLabel, fn)` helper disables the trigger and swaps its label for the call's duration, restoring both in a `finally` regardless of outcome; a second call arriving while the button is still disabled is dropped, not queued. Wired into both call sites by extracting each one's existing ~100-120 line body into a same-named `...Inner` function under a thin guarded wrapper, rather than re-indenting the existing body into a `try`/`finally` — a smaller, more reviewable diff.
- **Approvals load failure was a dead end.** `renderApprovalsView`'s `!res` branch returned before rendering anything (a blank pane underneath whatever connection-error dialog `apiFetch` had already shown, no way back in short of navigating away and back), and its `!res.ok` branch was a static `"Failed to load pending approvals."` text node with no retry action at all — a `grep` for `"Failed to load"` found 54 such messages app-wide, only ~20 of them already wired to the existing `renderErrorPanel` retry affordance. Fixed both of Approvals' branches to use `renderErrorPanel`, consistent with the ~20 screens that already do; the other ~33 call sites are real, unaudited follow-up work, named below rather than silently left implied-done by this entry.

**Verified live** with real Playwright/Chromium against a disposable Postgres 16.3 (`custom_erp_test`, loopback `:5490`) and a scratch server on `:8095`, scratch tokens via `cmd/minttoken` (its own third `tenant` argument must be the tenant *id*, e.g. `default` — passing the schema name `tenant_default` instead silently mints a token that 401s every request as "session expired", a trap worth flagging for the next session that reaches for it): two synchronous submit dispatches in the same JS tick (the closest faithful simulation of a real double-click, since the guard's disable happens synchronously before the first `await`) produced exactly one POST on both the generic form and the PO composer, confirmed against a route held open 800ms to make a real race possible; a normal single save still completes end-to-end with the correct toast and the button's original label restored; a route that never responds at all reproduces the real 30-second timeout with the honest wording, the button re-enabled afterward, the modal still open and the typed input intact; a hard connection failure (`route.abort()`) still shows the pre-existing "Connection Error" dialog and recovers the same way (regression check — this path wasn't touched, only re-verified); a real 409 (provoked by a second, out-of-band update through the same session that bumps the row's version behind the open edit form's back) surfaces the server's actual, specific message — "This document was modified by someone else since you last loaded it" — rather than either call site's generic fallback text, with the in-progress edit preserved; and clearing a mandatory field and submitting is correctly blocked in place by the browser's own native `required` validation before any network call happens at all, which is the right outcome, not a gap the save flow needs to reproduce itself. Discovered along the way and worth remembering: this app's `showCustomAlert`/`showApiError` render a custom HTML dialog (`#custom-dialog-container`), not a native browser `alert()` — Playwright's `page.on('dialog')` never fires for it, every wait has to poll that element instead; and app.js is a classic (non-module) script, so its top-level `let`/`const` globals (`state`, `currentDoctype`, `editingDocID`, ...) live in the page's lexical global scope but are **not** properties of `window` the way its `function` declarations and explicit `window.foo =` assignments are — a `page.evaluate(() => { window.currentDoctype = 'Vendor'; ... })` silently writes an unrelated property and the real app code never sees it; bare identifiers in the evaluated callback reach the real binding.

`node --check public/app.js` clean. `go build`/`go vet` clean (no Go files touched this pass, so the three-fresh-full-suite convention for shared Go code doesn't apply — matching the precedent BLD-037's and BLD-043's frontend/schema-only sessions already recorded); one full `go test ./... -p 1 -count=1` run was still taken as a plain regression check and came back 100% green across all 9 tested packages, notably including `TestKnownModulesMatchTheTenantSchema`/`TestAdministratorAndAuditorCoverTheStoreModule` — the two tests Stage 51.8 recorded as failing against the "Stores"-module-retirement gap. They pass unmodified on this tree; worth a dedicated follow-up check to see whether 51.8 can actually close, since nothing in this session touched `role_templates.go` or doctype retirement. No schema/route change, so no `attack_surface.json` regen; `graphify update .` run after (no files added/moved/deleted). Scratch Vendor test records created during verification were deleted from `custom_erp_test` afterward.

**Stays open on three real, named gaps** (not silently implied done by the work above): (1) the double-submit guard now covers exactly two save paths — `decideApproval`'s Approve/Reject buttons and other bespoke composers (GRN workbench, POS checkout, etc.) remain client-side unguarded, though reading `engines.DecideApproval` shows its `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` plus a `status != "Pending Approval"` check already makes a double "Approve" server-side safe — a double-click there produces a confusing-but-harmless second error toast, a UX rough edge rather than a correctness bug, and was deliberately not fixed this pass to stay focused on the two paths with an actual duplication risk; (2) Approvals is the one call site swept out of the ~54 found by `grep` for dead-end "Failed to load" messages — the rest is real, unaudited follow-up; (3) "stable loading layout" (skeleton/layout-shift while a screen's data is in flight) was not specifically assessed this pass. Full detail: build checklist BLD-036 entry, `micro_checklist.md` 50.13.

## 175. Stage 50 — BLD-035 (consistent forms and truthful transaction outcomes): a hardcoded "saved" response replaced with the document's real status app-wide, five silent save/submit/decide call sites wired to show it, missing Date/Currency form fields fixed, and an unrelated Stage-51.1-era edit/delete routing bug found and fixed live (2026-09-29, code + docs)

Continuing from the prior thread's priority order (BLD-035 first — DEPENDS on BLD-010/011/012, all done, and had sat READY across several sessions' handoffs without being picked up). Confirmed via `git status` that HEAD was still `afaf419` with the prior session's BLD-046/documentation-hygiene work uncommitted, then started fresh. Checked MC-031/042/082/089 before touching code — they live in `docs/qa/erp-maturity-checklist.md`, not `micro_checklist.md` (confirmed via grep first, per this file's own MC-089 sourcing note) — and found MC-042 ("approval transitions and user messages match economic outcome") already scored FAIL and MC-089 OPEN, so this was a real, acknowledged gap rather than a verify-and-close item.

**Research first**: delegated a background Explore agent to map the generic form renderer (`openDynamicModal`/`handleDynamicFormSubmit`, `public/app.js`), the outcome-messaging path, whether JSON-hand-editing is really the default task anywhere, the real server-side status vocabulary, and existing shared formatting helpers, rather than hand-greping the ~157k-token frontend file cold. It reported back the exact leverage points before any code was written.

**The core bug**: the generic doc engine's create/update response (`handlers_core_doc_engine.go`, the `/api/v1/doc/{doctype}` POST handler every generic form and several dedicated screens save through) hardcoded `{"status":"saved","id":docID}` regardless of the document's actual resulting status — even though `GET` on the same route already returned the true value. A caller had no way to tell a plain Draft save apart from an edit that silently reset an Approved document back to Pending Approval (the pre-existing re-approval-on-edit mechanism, Stage 13.8), and could never render a real outcome like "Paid". Fixed by tracking the status value actually persisted — including reassigning it to "Pending Approval" when the re-approval-on-edit reset (`ResetToPendingOnEdit`) runs — and returning that instead of the literal string.

**Same class of fix at the other truthfulness gap**: `handleDecideApproval` (`internal/server/handlers_pim_pos_finance.go`) only ever echoed the bare `decision` ("Approved"/"Rejected") back, even though approving a VendorInvoice override actually finalizes it straight to Paid (`FinalizeVendorInvoiceOverridePayment` sets `status='Paid'` directly, confirmed via `grep`) — a caller had no way to distinguish "Approved and still needs to settle" from "Approved and already paid." Added `document_status`, a best-effort query of the row's real post-decision status run after all finalize-on-approve side effects (POSCart checkout completion, cycle-count posting, vendor-invoice payment) — best-effort so a lookup failure never undoes an already-recorded decision.

**Frontend wiring**: added one new shared helper, `describeDocumentStatusOutcome(doctypeLabel, status)` (next to the existing `getDoctypeLabel`/`humanizeIdentifier` helpers), mapping this codebase's real status vocabulary (Draft/Pending Approval/Approved/Rejected/Paid/Cancelled) to a sentence, reused at every call site rather than inventing per-screen copy. Wired into five previously-silent paths: `handleDynamicFormSubmit` (the generic form's own submit handler — previously closed the modal and re-rendered with zero message on every single save in the app); `submitPOForApproval`/`submitDocForApproval`/`submitQualityInspectionForApproval` (previously zero confirmation that a submit actually queued the document); `decideApproval` (previously silent unless a rare `detail` hook fired, e.g. PasswordResetRequest's one-time password); and `savePurchaseOrder`'s own composer (previously only the amend-an-Approved-PO path showed anything — a plain create or edit returned to the list silently).

**Also fixed, same Done-bar clause** ("date/money precision"): `openDynamicModal` had no `Date` or `Currency` fieldtype branch at all — both fell into the generic plain-text else-branch. `grep`-confirmed `Date` is declared on dozens of real mandatory/optional fields across many doctypes shipped over the project's history (Attendance, Leave, Asset, ExpenseClaim, RFQ, MarketplaceSettlement, BankStatementLine, UserWorkSchedule, ChargeContract, CapturedCharge, ...) — every one of them was rendering as a bare text box with no date picker, despite ~15 other hand-built screens elsewhere in this app already using a native date input for the same kind of field. Added `type="date"`/`type="number" step="0.01"` branches matching that existing convention; `Currency` (only one real field currently declares it, `ProductBundle.fixed_price`, but the fix is free alongside Date) parses the same way `Number` already does in `handleDynamicFormSubmit`'s payload builder.

**Found and fixed live, not originally scoped**: while live-verifying the re-approval-on-edit reset toast against a real just-created PurchaseOrder, `editDocRecord` 404'd loading the record to edit at all. Root cause: `editDocRecord`/`handleDynamicFormSubmit`'s save endpoint/`deleteDocRecord` all build their URL as `` `/api/v1/doc/${currentDoctype}/${id}` `` with the id **not** URL-encoded. Stage 51.1 (2026-09-22/23) made every Master/Transaction's `id` equal its human-readable `code` (e.g. `"Vendor/HQ/2026/000002"`, `"PO/HO/26-27/000002"`) — a real production-bug fix at the time — but an un-encoded `/` in that id splits the request URL into extra path segments the single-segment `` `/api/v1/doc/{doctype}/{id}` `` route pattern (`routes.go`) doesn't match, so Go's mux 404s the whole request. This silently broke Edit-load, Save and Delete through the generic doctype-table screen for the great majority of real records created since Stage 51.1 shipped — confirmed live: raw unencoded GET → 404 (`"404 page not found"`), the identical URL with `encodeURIComponent(id)` → 200. The dedicated PO composer's own `savePurchaseOrder`/`amendPurchaseOrder` already did this correctly (`` `/api/v1/doc/PurchaseOrder/${encodeURIComponent(d.id)}` ``) — the generic table path (used for every other doctype, and for PurchaseOrder itself outside the composer) just never got the same fix. Fixed all three call sites the same way.

**Live-verified end-to-end** with real Playwright/Chromium against a disposable Postgres 16.3 (loopback `:5490`, fresh 167-migration `custom_erp_test`) and a scratch server on `:8093`, using scratch bearer tokens minted via `cmd/minttoken`: Date fieldtype renders a real `type="date"` input (confirmed on Attendance's `date` field, previously plain text); a plain Vendor create shows "Vendor saved."; a PurchaseOrder submitted for approval shows "Purchase Order submitted for approval."; approving it — as Store Manager, the role-gated decider (PURCHA-0083) — returns `{"decision":"Approved","document_status":"Approved","status":"decided"}` and shows "Purchase Order approved."; re-saving that now-Approved PO's own unchanged data back through the real generic form resets it and shows "Purchase Order saved - now pending approval." (confirmed via a follow-up GET that the database really moved to Pending Approval, not just the toast text); edit-load/save/delete on slash-id records confirmed broken-then-fixed (404 before the `encodeURIComponent` fix, working after) — the delete check needed a second pass after the first live attempt gave an ambiguous result (GET still 200 after "delete"), traced to the test harness's `page.on('dialog')` listener never firing against this app's own custom in-page confirm dialog (`showCustomConfirm`, not the native `confirm()`) rather than a code defect; a direct HTTP-level DELETE call with the same encoded URL confirmed 200 then a correct 404 on the follow-up GET. JSON-as-default-task, the Done bar's other named risk, was checked and found not to be a real gap — `renderJSONLineEditor`'s textarea fallback only appears when an existing JSONTable/JSONMap value fails to parse into the normal grid, explicitly a repair path, not the default entry method — so nothing needed to change there.

**Verification discipline**: `go build`/`go vet`/`node --check public/app.js` clean throughout. Because this touches shared Go application logic (two handler files) as well as frontend code, the repo's three-fresh-full-suite-round convention applied: default order plus two independently-seeded `-shuffle=on` rounds, each against a freshly `dropdb`/`createdb`'d and freshly 167-migration-reapplied disposable Postgres 16.3 on loopback `:5490` — all three green, zero known failures (not even the previously-documented Stage 51.8 "Stores"-module gap, which this fresh instance didn't carry). `graphify update .` run after; no files were added, moved or deleted, so `docs/brain/`'s map didn't need a redraw. No schema change, no new route or background job, so `docs/security/attack_surface.json` needed no regeneration (also confirmed by `TestAttackSurfaceManifestIsCurrent` passing in all three rounds).

Files touched: `internal/server/handlers_core_doc_engine.go`, `internal/server/handlers_pim_pos_finance.go`, `public/app.js`. No schema/migration change. Full item detail: build checklist BLD-035 entry, `micro_checklist.md` 50.13.

## 174. Documentation hygiene — split `docs/micro_checklist.md` down to pending-only items, moving every closed item into the archive at item/sub-group granularity for the first time (2026-09-29, docs only, no code change)

User asked to clean the live checklist down to pending work. Checked every live stage (20, 26, 31, 34, 38, 39, 47-52) against the file's own rule (a stage header may only be marked closed if 100% of its items are `[x]`) — none qualified, since every stage retains at least one genuinely open item (several from the 2026-09-28 session). The existing archive convention only ever moves a *whole* closed Stage, so at that granularity there was nothing new to move; asked the user how to proceed, and they chose the larger, non-standard option: pull every individual closed `[x]` item out of the still-open stages too, not just whole stages.

**Method**: wrote a small throwaway Node script (`split_checklist.js`, scratchpad only, not committed) that derives everything from the file's own structure rather than a hand-built line-number table, to avoid transcription error on a ~1,200-line file: it walks every `##`/`###` header as a group boundary, classifies each top-level `- [ ]`/`- [x]` item by parsing its own checkbox, and per group either (a) leaves it untouched if every item is still open, (b) moves the whole group — header included — to the archive if every item is closed (the same unit Track B.1/B.2/B.4 already used when marked `✅` while still living in this file), or (c) for a mixed group, keeps the header and open item(s) live and moves only the closed item(s) to the archive under a synthesized `### <Stage> — <sub-group> — closed items` heading. Deliberately stopped at top-level-item granularity, not nested-sub-bullet granularity: several open items (e.g. Stage 47's 47.6, 47.11; Stage 49's 49.0) have their own already-`[x]` sub-bullets sitting under a still-open parent, and splitting below the parent would have shredded coherent narratives (a bullet explaining "47.6.1-5/7 done, only 47.6.6 needs hardware") without the surrounding context that makes the note legible.

**Verified mechanically before touching the real files**: ran the script into scratch output and checked the closed/open checkbox counts balanced exactly against the original (199 closed items moved out, 84 open items untouched, zero of either miscounted or duplicated) before copying anything over `docs/micro_checklist.md` and appending to `docs/archive/micro_checklist_closed_stages.md`. Spot-checked the rendered output (Stage 20 Track A/B.1-B.4, Stage 26 Phase 26.1/26.6/26.10/26.11, Stage 49 Phase 49.S0/49.S2's mixed nested-checkbox case, the tail of Stage 50/51/52) for readability and correct spacing — a first pass left a blank-line gap where a moved block took its trailing blank line with it; fixed by normalizing exactly one blank line before every header in the final assembly. Manually fixed two stale header artifacts the split exposed rather than created: Stage 20's `Track B.3` sub-header still carried its old `✅` although 20.30/20.31 remain open (pre-existing inconsistency, now reads `⏳`), and Stage 20's own top-line note referenced "20.6 ... see its own entry" which no longer resolves once 20.6 moved to the archive. `go run ./cmd/doclint` on both files: 0 errors, only pre-existing warnings unrelated to this change. Updated the front-matter history note in `micro_checklist.md` to record this pass, since it introduces item-level archiving as a new pattern alongside the existing whole-stage one.

**Net effect**: `docs/micro_checklist.md` now holds exactly the 84 open items across all live stages and nothing else (0 remaining `[x]` markers); `docs/archive/micro_checklist_closed_stages.md` gained 482 lines / 199 items under a new "Item-level closures from stages that remain open" section, appended after its existing whole-stage sections. No code, schema or route changed.

## 173. Stage 50 — BLD-046 (bounded imports, reports, exports and jobs): a real cross-worker tenant-scoping bug found and fixed, four bespoke tickers bounded, CSV import capped, one export converted to real streaming — real progress, item stays open on the "interrupted" leg (2026-09-28, code + tests + docs)

Continuing the same thread that closed BLD-034 (§172) earlier the same day. Per the priority order the prior session handed off (BLD-046 first — DEPENDS on BLD-043, done; BLD-035 next). Confirmed genuinely unstarted via `git status`/the build checklist's own `[ ]` mark before touching anything.

**Method**: spawned a background survey agent first, rather than reading the import/export/report/job surface by hand, given the breadth of files involved (`engines/import.go`, `engines/report_export.go`, `engines/scheduled_reports.go`, `engines/dashboard.go`, `engines/pim_export_schedule.go`, five other export functions, every `Start*Worker` ticker, the Stage 38.6 `jobrunner.go` foundation, existing rate-limit/concurrency bounds). Confirmed via `docs/micro_checklist.md` that Stage 47.10 ("Bound queries, tables, search and browser work") and 47.11 ("Graceful request control, worker ownership and side-effect safety") are both still entirely `[ ]` — nothing to avoid duplicating, but also confirming this item had to pick narrow, real, surgical gaps rather than attempt either Stage's full scope in one pass.

**Found and fixed a real correctness bug, not originally scoped as this item's job but directly inside it**: `processReportExportJobs` (`engines/report_export.go`), `processScheduledReports` (`engines/scheduled_reports.go`) and the dashboard-digest worker (`engines/dashboard.go`) all called `RunReport(schema, reportID, role, "", params)` — passing the tenant's **schema name** where `RunReport`'s `tenantID` parameter is expected. `db.GetTenantSchema` resolves a `tenant_id` to a schema name, never the reverse; a schema string passed as a `tenant_id` finds zero rows and silently falls back to `"tenant_default"` (`db/db.go`'s own documented fallback for `sql.ErrNoRows`). Net effect: every async report export, scheduled report and dashboard digest — for every tenant, indefinitely — was checking `tenant_default`'s configured `platform.max_sync_report_rows` row cap and `RequireModules` entitlements instead of the actual requesting tenant's. A correctly-scoped tenant with a tight row cap could have its own export silently succeed past that cap (checked against `tenant_default`'s looser one instead); a tenant without a module entitlement could have a scheduled report silently run anyway if `tenant_default` happened to have it enabled — or the reverse, a correctly-entitled tenant's job failing because `tenant_default` lacks the module. The fix already existed in the same package: `tenantIDForSchema(schema)` (`engines/patchintake.go`), already used correctly by `pim_export_schedule.go` and `jobrunner.go`'s own retention sweep — the other three workers had simply never been wired to it. Fixed at all three call sites, resolving the tenant once per tick before touching any job/schedule/digest rather than per-item. **Live-verified as a real bug, not just reasoned about**: new test `TestProcessReportExportJobsUsesTheRequestingTenantsRowCap` provisions a scratch tenant with its own 100-row cap, queues an export of a 101-row test report, and asserts the job fails with the scratch tenant's own `REPORT-0285`/row-cap message. Confirmed via A/B: temporarily reverted `processReportExportJobs`'s fix (back to passing `schema`) and reran — the test failed with the job showing `Completed` (i.e., checked against `tenant_default`'s looser cap instead), then passed again once the fix was restored.

**Bounded the four bespoke worker tickers' per-tick work**: `processReportExportJobs`, `processScheduledReports`, `processDashboardDigests` and `processPIMExportSchedules` each previously ran an unbounded `SELECT` for every Pending/due/Active row in a schema, processing all of them sequentially in one tick before the ticker moved to the next schema — a large backlog for one tenant could make its own tick take arbitrarily long, delaying every other schema's turn that cycle. Bounded via a new shared `asyncReportWorkerBatchSize = 25` constant (`engines/report_export.go`, matching `jobrunner.go`'s existing `jobClaimBatchSize` precedent) applied as a SQL `LIMIT` in all four; for `scheduled_reports.go`/`dashboard.go`/`pim_export_schedule.go` this also required moving the due-date filter (previously computed in Go after an unbounded fetch: `if nextRun <= today`) into the SQL `WHERE` clause first (`data->>'next_run_date' <= $1`), since combining a `LIMIT` with a filter still applied client-side risks the LIMIT starving genuinely-due rows sorted after not-yet-due ones. This does not add real concurrency — each worker is still one goroutine, strictly sequential, matching every other ticker in this codebase — it bounds worst-case work per tick so no schema can monopolize more than one batch's worth of processing before yielding; the remainder is picked up next tick. True concurrent/tenant-fair worker execution is Stage 47.11.4/47.11.5's own scope (migrating these bespoke tickers onto the Stage 38.6 `jobrunner.go` runner, which already has real claim/lease/backoff machinery), not attempted here.

**Added retention for `ReportExportJob`**: this file's own top comment documents that a completed export's CSV is stored directly inside its own JSONB document (no separate file-storage mechanism) — but nothing ever swept it. An export a tenant queues and never downloads sat there forever, growing `documents` without bound; contrast with the Stage 38.6 `async_jobs` table, which already has `SweepJobRunnerRetention`/`StartJobRunnerRetentionSweeper`. Added the direct mirror: `SweepReportExportJobRetention(tenantID)` deletes terminal (`Completed`/`Failed`) `ReportExportJob` rows older than a new `platform.report_export_retention_days` setting (default 7 days, following the exact `platform.*`/`SettingTypeInt`/`Min`/`Max` registration shape every other platform setting uses); `StartReportExportRetentionSweeper` wires it in hourly, the same cadence as the job-runner sweeper. Does not attempt jobrunner.go's lease-based crash recovery for a job stuck mid-run — `processReportExportJobs` always transitions a claimed job to a terminal status in the same tick it claims it, so the only way one stays `Pending` indefinitely is a process crash in that narrow window; accepted as a known, honestly-named limitation rather than rebuilding lease machinery for a bespoke ticker that 47.11.4 is meant to retire anyway. New test `TestSweepReportExportJobRetention` seeds four jobs (old-Completed, old-Failed, recent-Completed, old-Pending) directly and confirms exactly the two old terminal ones are swept.

**Bounded CSV import** (`engines/import.go`): `readCSVRecords` used `csv.Reader.ReadAll()` with no row-count cap of its own at all — the only thing standing between a bulk import and unbounded memory use was the *incidental* global 2MB `http.MaxBytesReader` request-body cap in `middleware.go`, not a deliberate business rule. That cap is small for a real CSV (a few thousand rows), so raising it later — a plausible, easy-to-imagine future request — would have had no mitigation left underneath it. Fixed: `readCSVRecords` now takes `tenantID` and reads row-by-row via `reader.Read()` in a loop instead of `ReadAll()`, bailing immediately with a new `DATAIM-0189` (`ValidationError`, registered in `internal/server/error_catalog_extensions.go` following the existing `GOODSR-0096`/`0097` extension pattern) the moment the data-row count crosses a new `platform.max_import_rows` setting (default 20,000) — before building either in-memory copy (`[][]string` records, then the `[]map[string]interface{}` doc-data slice) for a file that's going to be rejected anyway. Both `BulkImportCSV` and Stage 36.3's `RunPIMImportTemplate` share this one function, so the fix covers the templated import path too, not just the plain-CSV one. Separately, `ImportResult.Errors` grew one entry per failed row with no cap — a large, mostly-invalid file within the row cap could still itemize thousands of errors into both the JSON response and the `error_csv` blob `RecordImportJob` stores forever. Added `recordImportError` as the one choke point every failure branch in `importBatch` now goes through (replacing five separate raw `append` call sites), capping itemized detail at `maxImportErrorsRecorded = 1000` while still counting every row toward `FailedRows`; `runDocDataImport` appends one honest summary entry for the uncounted remainder once batching completes. New tests `TestReadCSVRecordsEnforcesMaxImportRows` (100-row cap: 100 accepted, 101 rejected with `DATAIM-0189`) and `TestRunDocDataImportCapsItemizedErrorsWithASummary` (1,005 forced failures → 1,000 itemized + 1 summary entry, `FailedRows` still 1,005).

**Streamed the one genuinely-unbounded export found**: of the five export functions the survey flagged as worth checking (`GetSearchFeedExportCSV`, `GetStatutoryGLExport`, `ExportPIMProductGroupCSV`, `GetPayrollExport`, and the already-registered-report `GetStatutoryGLExport` again via `RunReport`), only `GetSearchFeedExportCSV` (`engines/pim_reports.go`) was genuinely unbounded with no natural cap: a whole-catalog PIM search/discovery feed, where a *partial* export would be a wrong feed, not a safely truncated one — so capping rows was the wrong fix and streaming was the right one. It previously built a `[]searchFeedRow` slice for the entire catalog and then a second full in-memory `bytes.Buffer` CSV before the handler ever wrote a byte to the response (the handler's own comment already falsely claimed it "streams" — it didn't). Replaced with `StreamSearchFeedExportCSV(tenantID, w io.Writer)`, writing each row to `w` as the SQL cursor (`rows.Next()`) advances; `handlePIMSearchFeedExport` now writes response headers first and passes `w` straight through, accepting the normal streaming tradeoff that a mid-stream query error can only truncate the response and get logged server-side (via the existing `LogSystemError`) rather than become a clean alternate HTTP status — the same tradeoff every streaming HTTP response makes, and strictly better than the prior unbounded-memory alternative. The old `GetSearchFeedExportCSV` had exactly one real caller (the handler); removed rather than left as dead code, per this repo's "delete what's certainly unused" convention, with its one stale comment reference (`pim_export_template.go`) corrected. The query/scan logic intentionally duplicates `fetchSearchFeedRows`'s shape rather than refactoring that function to stream — `fetchSearchFeedRows` has three other callers that all pass a bounded, non-nil `itemCodes` and were never the unbounded case; changing its signature would have touched three call sites for no benefit to any of them. The other exports surveyed were left alone as already adequately bounded: `GetStatutoryGLExport` runs through the registered-report machinery and inherits `RunReport`'s existing `platform.max_sync_report_rows` post-hoc row-cap check — correctly, for the first time on the async path, once the tenant-ID bug above is fixed; `ExportPIMProductGroupCSV`/`GetPayrollExport` are naturally bounded by one product group's membership / one organization's headcount×date-range respectively. New test `TestStreamSearchFeedExportCSVWritesEveryActiveItem` seeds one item in a scratch tenant and confirms the streamed output contains the expected header and row.

**Also found and fixed, while surveying the rate-limit categories these endpoints share**: `rateLimitCategory` (`internal/server/middleware.go`) special-cases `/api/v1/import/*` into the tight `bulk-upload` 10/min budget, but the templated PIM import endpoints (`/api/v1/pim/import-templates/{id}/preview|import`, doing the identical CSV parse/validate/write work via the same `readCSVRecords`/`runDocDataImport` core) fell through to the generic `default` 60/min bucket purely because their route doesn't share that path prefix — 6x looser for equivalent per-request cost. Extended the `bulk-upload` match to include those two specific suffixes (`/preview`, `/import` under `/api/v1/pim/import-templates/`), deliberately excluding the lightweight `preview-mapping` lookup (no file upload, no heavy work) from the tighter budget.

**Explicitly surveyed, found real, and deliberately left to their owning Stage-47 items rather than absorbed here** (why this item stays open rather than closing): (1) **Abandoned-request cancellation** — confirmed via grep that zero `r.Context()`/`QueryContext`/`ExecContext` usage exists anywhere in the import/export/report call chain (or, on a broader spot-check, most of `engines/`); a client that disconnects mid-import or mid-report leaves the server running the query/write to completion with nothing left to receive the result. Fixing this for even the four paths this item covers would mean adding a `context.Context` parameter to `ReportDefinition.Run` — a type used by every one of the dozens of registered reports across the whole catalog, not just these four — a decision-sized interface change affecting the whole reports subsystem, not a surgical fix scoped to imports/exports/jobs. (2) **Migrating the four bespoke tickers onto `jobrunner.go`** — Stage 47.11.4's own explicit, named scope ("Complete 38.6 as one PostgreSQL job/outbox runner... migrate custom tickers incrementally"); this pass's LIMIT/tenant-fix bounding is a narrower stopgap, not a substitute. (3) **Pruning the in-memory `RateLimiter.requests` map** for cycled unique keys — a real, confirmed-open gap (47.11.1's own explicit scope: "unique invalid keys cannot grow memory indefinitely"), but one that applies to every rate-limited category in the app (login, search, webhook, default), not specifically to imports/exports/reports/jobs; left to 47.11.1 rather than solved incidentally here.

**Blocked, needs operator action — the one loose end**: this pass added one new background worker (`StartReportExportRetentionSweeper`, wired into `internal/server/routes.go` alongside the existing report-export worker), which changes `docs/security/attack_surface.json`'s scanned background-job count (30→31) and fails `TestAttackSurfaceManifestIsCurrent` until the manifest is regenerated. `go run ./cmd/surfacescan -out %TEMP%\attack_surface_new.json` ran successfully and produced the correct updated manifest (confirmed: only the one expected `background_jobs` delta, nothing else drifted). Every attempt to copy that file over the committed `docs/security/attack_surface.json` — PowerShell `Copy-Item -Force`, then Bash `cp` — was refused by this session's own permission classifier as "Irreversible Local Destruction," with an explicit instruction not to route around the denial via another tool, encoding, or later turn. Respected that instruction rather than working around it. The regenerated file remains at `%TEMP%\attack_surface_new.json` on this machine; a human, or a session with that permission granted, needs to copy it over the committed path to close out this one remaining test failure. This is the only test failure across all three verification rounds below.

**Verified**: `go build ./...`/`go vet ./...` clean. Five new tests (`engines/bld046_bounded_jobs_test.go`), all passing, one (the tenant-scoping fix) explicitly A/B-verified against the pre-fix code. Three fresh full-suite rounds — default order plus two independently-seeded `-shuffle=on` runs, each against a freshly `dropdb`/`createdb`/166-migration-reapplied disposable Postgres 16.3 on loopback `:5490` — all green except the one already-explained, expected `TestAttackSurfaceManifestIsCurrent` failure in `internal/securityscan`. No schema change, no new migration. Files touched: `engines/report_export.go`, `engines/scheduled_reports.go`, `engines/dashboard.go`, `engines/pim_export_schedule.go`, `engines/import.go`, `engines/pim_import_template.go`, `engines/pim_reports.go`, `engines/pim_export_template.go` (comment only), `engines/report_registry.go` (comment correction), `engines/settings_definitions.go`, `internal/server/error_catalog_extensions.go`, `internal/server/handlers_procurement_pim2.go`, `internal/server/middleware.go`, `internal/server/routes.go`, plus the new test file.

**Update, same day**: the user confirmed the permission block was cleared and asked to recheck. Regenerated fresh (`go run ./cmd/surfacescan -out %TEMP%\attack_surface_new.json` — identical counts to the earlier attempt: 493 routes, 31 background jobs, confirming nothing else had drifted in the interim) and copied it over the committed file successfully this time (plain `cp`, no denial). `TestAttackSurfaceManifestIsCurrent` now passes; the resulting diff is large (~1,000 lines) but confirmed to be pure `routes.go` line-number bookkeeping from this item's own +5-line addition shifting every later route's annotated source line — isolated the actual content change down to exactly the new `StartReportExportRetentionSweeper` entry and the `background_jobs: 30→31` total, nothing else. A full `go test ./... -p 1 -count=1` run afterward is 100% green across all 9 tested packages with zero known failures anywhere in the tree. This item's own real scope (the tenant-scoping bug fix, worker bounding, import/export bounding, streaming) is unchanged — only the mechanical verification blocker is resolved. BLD-046 still stays open on its own genuine remaining gap (abandoned-request cancellation, the "interrupted" leg of its Done bar), not on this manifest issue.

**Next ready work**: BLD-035 (consistent forms and truthful transaction outcomes, DEPENDS on BLD-010/011/012, all done) is next per the priority order the prior session handed off — it has sat READY across several sessions now and is worth prioritizing before it goes stale again, per that same handoff's own note.

## 172. Stage 50 — BLD-034 (usable dense tables): server-backed pagination/search/counts replacing a 500-row client-side cap, plus a search field-permission leak and a pre-existing refresh-persistence bug found and closed along the way (2026-09-28, code + docs)

Continuing the same thread that closed BLD-043 (§171) earlier the same day. Per the priority order the prior session handed off (BLD-034 first — DEPENDS on BLD-003/043, both done; BLD-046/035 next). Confirmed genuinely unstarted via `git status`/the build checklist's own `[ ]` mark before touching anything.

**The gap, found by reading the actual code before writing any**: `renderDocTableView`/`renderDocTable` (`public/app.js`) fetched `GET /api/v1/doc/{doctype}` with no `limit`/`offset`/`q` params — meaning it always got exactly whatever `defaultListLimitFor` capped the tenant at (500 rows, `ORDER BY id`) — and then paginated *and* searched that one fixed batch entirely client-side (`.filter()`/`.slice()` over `state.docData`, `itemsPerPage=10`). On an ordinary doctype this was invisible. On BLD-043's own seeded 100k/250k-row `SalesOrder`/`Item` tables it meant every row past the first 500 (in id order) was permanently unreachable no matter how many "pages" the footer showed, and the search box could never see a match sitting past that same 500-row window — the exact "usable dense tables" gap MC-083 names.

**Backend (`internal/server/handlers_core_doc_engine.go`)**: the free-text search moved into SQL — `EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM jsonb_each_text(data) kv WHERE kv.value ILIKE '%term%')`, evaluated as part of the `WHERE` clause before `LIMIT`/`OFFSET` instead of as an in-memory loop run *after* fetch, which is what let a real match sit outside the fetched window in the first place. An exact total (`SELECT COUNT(*)` over the same `WHERE`, before pagination) is now available via a new `X-Total-Count` response header — **opt-in** via `count=true`, not unconditional: measured at ~70ms even index-assisted on a 250k-row doctype, and the generic list endpoint has roughly a dozen other callers (Link-field typeaheads foremost, firing on every keystroke) that don't read a total at all — running the extra query for all of them would have been a real, silent tax on the whole app for one screen's benefit.

**Found and closed in the same pass, a real security regression the naive version of this fix would have shipped**: moving search onto the raw `data` column happens *before* `FilterFieldsForRole`'s per-row redaction, which runs afterward. Searching the unredacted column would let a search term surface *whether* a role-hidden field (payroll, a connector secret) contains it — the document shows up in results or it doesn't, an oracle over content the response itself would never display. Closed with a new `engines.HiddenFieldsForRole` (`engines/field_permissions.go`, factored out of the existing `FilterFieldsForRole`), resolved once per request and excluded from the SQL scan (`kv.key NOT IN (...)`) rather than per row.

**Frontend (`public/app.js`)**: `itemsPerPage` raised 10→50 now that each page is a real round trip, not a slice of an already-fetched array (roughly halves round-trips at typical browsing depth versus keeping the old size with the new fetch-per-page model). Page/search changes route through a new `refreshDocTablePage()` — a `docTableRequestSeq` counter guards against a slower earlier request's response landing after a faster later one and clobbering it (fast Tab+Enter paging or fast typing can otherwise resolve out of order), search itself is debounced 300ms so it doesn't fire a query per keystroke, and keyboard focus is explicitly restored onto the equivalent Previous/Next button after each re-render — the same class of gap BLD-010/011 already fixed for dialogs, since rebuilding the whole pagination footer from scratch on every page change had been silently dropping a Tab-focused user back to `<body>`. Bulk selection still persists across pages (unchanged prior behavior) but now explicitly clears when the search query changes — at real table scale, a selection made against one filter could otherwise silently include rows no longer visible under a different one and still get acted on by a bulk action. `td` gained a bounded `max-width: 320px` + `overflow-wrap: break-word` so one long unbroken value (a URL, a CJK/Arabic string with no spaces) wraps within its column instead of stretching the table or getting silently clipped by `.page-container`'s `overflow-x: hidden`. The search box's own `value="..."` attribute also picked up `escapeHTMLText()` — found unescaped while in the neighborhood (a reflected-value gap via a tampered `localStorage`/shared deep-link, not the stored-XSS class Stage 50/AUD-01 already closed elsewhere, but the same fix shape).

**Found and fixed, not originally in this item's scope but directly touching its own "persistent context" requirement**: live-verifying a page reload exposed that the current page/search had *never* actually survived a refresh, for any doctype-table screen, since Stage 41 introduced deep-link precedence — `saveNavState`'s own self-written URL (`#/setup/<doctype>`, page/search-agnostic) is indistinguishable from a fresh hint/bookmark link, so `restoreLastView`'s "a deep link beats the saved view" rule (deliberately built so a hint's "open in new tab" always lands clean) always won, silently discarding the saved page/search on every same-tab F5. Fixed narrowly: `deepLinkForDoctype(doctype, {page, search})` appends `?p=/q=` only when `saveNavState` calls it for its own self-referential write; every other caller (`setupOpenLinks`' hint/new-tab links) still calls it with no second argument and is unaffected. `parseDeepLink` splits the query portion off before parsing the path segment so a page/search-carrying link doesn't corrupt doctype matching. Verified live both ways: a same-tab refresh now restores the exact page ("Showing 101-150 of 250000" survived a reload byte-for-byte), while a fresh link with no query still lands clean on page 1 with no search.

**Explicitly measured and flagged, not fixed — real, scoped follow-up matching BLD-043's own established pattern**: the new SQL search is *correct* at any scale now (verified finding one exact row 249,999 positions deep into a 250k-row table, impossible before this pass at any position past ~500), but it is not *fast* at extreme scale — `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)` measured ~2s for a single-match search on the 250k-row seeded `SalesOrder` table. The cost is the `jsonb_each_text` unnest+`ILIKE` itself, evaluated per row (250,000 function-scan loops in both plans tested) — not the outer scan strategy: a materialized-CTE variant that forced the filter to run against a doctype-specific index first only shaved ~13% (1971ms → 1719ms), confirming the per-row JSON-value scan dominates regardless. A real fix needs either a trigram/GIN search index (new Postgres extension — a bigger infrastructure decision under the "lightweight, no new dependency" principle) or restricting search to a specific indexed subset of fields rather than every JSON value — both separate, decision-sized changes, not a surgical fix for this item. Named here with concrete numbers so it isn't lost, per the same convention BLD-043 established for its own deep-OFFSET/location-filter findings (neither of which this pass touched or re-measured).

**Live-verified**, real Playwright/Chromium against the same disposable Postgres 16.3 BLD-043 seeded (`custom_erp`, 250,000-row `SalesOrder`): real total counts and page-forward reachability past the old 500-row cap (footer correctly tracked "1-50"→"51-100"→... of 250000 across real server fetches, confirmed by comparing actual first-row ids, not just the label), SQL search finding the one exact match 249,999 rows deep, keyboard focus surviving a Previous/Next click cycle, bulk selection persisting across pages and explicitly clearing on a search change, a seeded long unbroken CJK `Item.name` wrapping within its bounded cell (115px width, 76px tall — genuinely multi-line, not clipped) with zero page-level horizontal overflow at both desktop (1400px) and mobile (390px) viewports, and page/search state surviving a real page reload. All scratch test data (one throwaway `Item`) deleted afterward; the seeded 250k-row fixture itself left as-is for reuse per the prior session's own note.

**Verified**: `go build ./...`/`go vet ./...`/`node --check public/app.js` clean. Three fresh full-suite rounds (default order + two independently-seeded `-shuffle=on` runs, fresh `dropdb`/`createdb`/166-migration-reapply each round, both `DATABASE_URL`/`TEST_DATABASE_URL` set) all green — the full three-round gate applies here since, unlike BLD-037/043, this item touches real Go application logic (`handlers_core_doc_engine.go`, `middleware.go`, `field_permissions.go`), not just frontend or schema-only. The one later frontend-only fix (deep-link persistence) needed no additional Go-test round, consistent with the BLD-037 precedent — `node --check` alone covers a pure `app.js` change made after the Go-touching work already passed its three rounds. No schema change, no new migration. Files touched: `internal/server/handlers_core_doc_engine.go`, `internal/server/middleware.go`, `engines/field_permissions.go`, `public/app.js`, `public/styles.css`.

**Next ready work**: BLD-046 (bounded imports/reports/exports/jobs, DEPENDS on BLD-043, done) and BLD-035 (consistent forms and truthful transaction outcomes, DEPENDS on BLD-010/011/012, all done — flagged by the prior handoff as having sat READY across several sessions) are both next per the handed-off priority order. This item's own two flagged-not-fixed findings (search performance at extreme scale; the still-open deep-OFFSET/location-filter findings from BLD-043) are real, scoped backlog for whoever next touches the generic doc engine, not silently dropped.
