## 102. Stage 37.1.3-37.1.5 — multi-currency finished, and the 98.8% receivable understatement it uncovered (2026-08-16, code + schema + docs)

Asked to start building from the open parity backlog, whose first row is **Stage 37 — ERP core depth**, annotated "incl. finishing multi-currency (37.1.3-37.1.5)". That was the right place to start for a reason beyond ordering: 37.1 was the only half-built item in the list, and half-built multi-currency is not a missing feature, it is a live correctness liability. Full item-by-item detail in `micro_checklist.md` Stage 37.1. A concurrent session's Stage 42.1 work was uncommitted in this tree throughout — see the note at the end.

**The bug the stage existed to find.** 37.1.2 taught documents and postings to *carry* a currency and a rate, and stopped there. It never touched the settlement engines, so `PostSalesInvoice` read `total_amount` — which on a USD invoice is 1000, in *dollars* — and posted it straight into Accounts Receivable. **A USD 1,000 invoice at 83.00 booked a 1,000-rupee receivable instead of an 83,000 one.** The receivable, the ageing report, the credit exposure and the trial balance were each understated by 98.8%, silently, for any tenant that used the feature 37.1.2 shipped. Nothing failed; the numbers were simply wrong. The posting sites now read the `base_*` twin that stage already stamps, and `PayVendorInvoice` had the same defect on the payable side.

**The model, recorded once so it is not re-derived or "simplified" later.** A receivable is booked at the invoice-date rate and carried at that rupee value. The cash eventually received is worth a different number of rupees, and that difference is **realised** — it is cash, it has happened, it cannot reverse (4200 gain / 5600 loss). While the invoice sits open across a period end its carrying value is stale, so revaluing it to the closing rate recognises the movement so far as **unrealised** (4210 / 5610), tracked cumulatively on the document itself. **At settlement the realised figure is measured against the revalued carrying amount, not the original.** Total P&L across every period is then exactly (cash received − amount booked), however it splits between them; measuring against the original would double-count every rupee already recognised at a period end. Four accounts rather than two because realised and unrealised must never merge — one is cash, the other is an opinion that can reverse next month, and every auditor treats them differently. There is deliberately no reversing entry on day one of the next period: this codebase has no automatic reversal machinery, and a cumulative carrying amount reaches the same answer with one fewer moving part.

**The sign flips by side, and that is the thing most likely to be broken by a later edit.** When the foreign currency strengthens a receivable is worth *more* (gain) and a payable costs *more* to settle (loss). `resolveSettlementFX` is told which side it is on rather than inferring it from the amounts, and the tests assert both directions against the same numbers so a sign error cannot pass.

**Reuse rather than new mechanism, throughout.** `SettlementOptions` is variadic (the `PostingOptions` precedent), so all six existing callers of the four settlement engines needed zero changes. The revaluation posts through the existing `PostDoubleEntry` and therefore inherits the closed-period check and the idempotency key for free. All three of 37.1.5's reports are `ReportDefinition`s, so they arrive with the catalog's screen, parameter form, CSV/PDF export, scheduling and column redaction already attached — no frontend code at all. The migration adds four GL accounts and **no table and no column**: the revaluation state lives as engine-written JSON keys on the document, so a document never revalued is byte-identical to what it is today.

**`GET` previews the revaluation and `POST` commits it** — the split is the HTTP verb, not a `dry_run` flag on one endpoint. A GET cannot change the ledger however it is called, which is what makes it safe behind a button a controller will press repeatedly while checking the numbers, and safe against a retry, a prefetch or a double-click booking a second adjustment.

**Three bugs live verification caught that the tests could not have**, all on paths a unit test never travels:

- **An `ExchangeRate` with no end date was unsaveable** — an open-ended rate, which is how essentially every rate table is really maintained. `fmt.Sprintf("%v", nil)` is the four-character string `"<nil>"`, not `""`, so an omitted optional field arrived at validation as a *non-empty* value that then failed its format check, and the message blamed a field the user had never filled in.
- **A `Currency` whose document id was not its ISO code broke every invoice that selected it.** The `currency` field is a `Link`, so the generic check resolves against `documents.id`, while `ApplyDocumentCurrency` requires a three-letter ISO code — the only value satisfying both is one where id == code. The seeded INR row happens to be keyed that way, which is why nothing had noticed. It now refuses at authoring time, naming the id to use, rather than surfacing later on a different document as "Linked Currency record with ID \"USD\" does not exist" about a record sitting right there.
- **This stage's own first cut invented a foreign-currency amount.** `PostDoubleEntry` derives a missing per-account transaction amount as `functional / rate` — correct for a plain foreign posting, but on an FX line it fabricates a number: a 1,000-rupee revaluation at 84.00 recorded itself as USD 11.90, so summing `transaction_debit` reported an exposure of USD 1,011.90 against a real one of USD 1,000. An FX gain/loss line has no foreign value *by construction*, and a revaluation moves no foreign currency at all; both now post explicit zeros.

**Verified**: build/vet clean, migration applied to dev and `-migrate-status` clean afterwards, 22 new subtests plus `go test ./... -p 1` fully green. Then the whole lifecycle over real HTTP on a throwaway server (port 8151, stopped and released, every fixture deleted and residue confirmed zero): post at 83 → **83,000, not 1,000**; revalue at Closing 84 → +1,000 unrealised, previewed first, and the re-run skipped with its reason; settle at Spot 85 → cash 85,000, AR cleared at the 84,000 *carrying* amount, realised gain **1,000 not 2,000**, AR netting exactly to zero; then the AP mirror on the same numbers producing a 2,000 *loss*. Both authoring-time refusals and all three reports confirmed, including the INR→USD presentation trial balance reporting balanced.

**Known limitations, stated rather than hidden.** The presentation trial balance applies **one rate to every line** — a real IAS 21 translation needs closing for balance-sheet items, average for P&L and historical for equity, plus an equity movement schedule and a translation reserve this codebase does not have, and a half-version would look authoritative and balance to nothing. It says so in a `rate_type` column on every row; real consolidation is 37.2's job. And the two new routes have **no frontend surface** yet — the revaluation is API-only, though the three reports render through the existing generic report screen.

**A concurrent session's Stage 42.1 work (traceability) was uncommitted in this tree throughout** — `engines/traceability*.go`, `internal/server/handlers_traceability.go`, `db/migrations_stage42_1_traceability.sql` and its edits to the WMS/inventory engines. File sets are disjoint apart from `internal/server/routes.go` and the three tracker docs, where both sessions appended in different regions. Its migration was already applied to dev, so `-migrate-status` showed exactly one pending migration — this stage's — which is the check that proved the isolation held. **Stage by explicit file list; never `git add -A` here.**

## 101. Stage 42.1 — the traceability foundation: batch, FEFO and recall (2026-08-16, code + schema + docs)

Asked to start building Stage 42 (WMS parity), which had been planning-only since 2026-08-11. The plan is unusually emphatic about where to begin — Phase 42.1, "start here, nothing else first" — because grep-verified there was **no lot/batch concept anywhere in the tree**: no `batch_no`, no `expiry`, no shelf life in `engines/` or `db/`. That absence is what made FEFO, expiry blocking and recall unbuildable, and it is why this WMS could not be sold into food, pharma, cosmetics or electronics. Six of the phase's eleven items are now closed (42.1.1–42.1.6) plus the recall traceability the plan asked for as proof the model is right. Full item-by-item detail in `micro_checklist.md` Stage 42.

Three decisions carried the design, and each one was a choice *not* to take the obvious route:

- **Batch stock is a new `bin_stock_batch` table, not a `batch_no` column on `bin_stock`.** The column reads as the smaller change until you follow it: two lots of one SKU in one bin must not collide, so `batch_no` has to enter the primary key, which means `DROP CONSTRAINT bin_stock_pkey` on a table holding live droplet stock plus rewriting every `ON CONFLICT (bin_code, sku, condition)` in two engine files. That is a destructive rework of an existing table. The separate table is the precedent 26.5.4 already set with `bin_stock_lpn` — `bin_stock` breaks down `inventory_availability`, this breaks down `bin_stock` — and it enforces the same invariant `AssignToLPN` does against the same `FOR UPDATE`-locked parent row: the breakdown may be *incomplete*, never larger than its parent.
- **`Batch.item` is a `Data` field holding the SKU code, not a `Link` to Item.** The generic Link check resolves its value against `documents.id`, and an Item's document id is not its code in this tree (`ITEM-<sku>`), so a Link would have rejected the exact string a clerk reads off a carton. And `batch_no` is a field rather than the document id for the reason the plan flagged in advance: ids are globally unique across doctypes, but a lot number is only unique *within* an item, so keying on it would make the second supplier shipping "LOT-001" unsaveable. Uniqueness of the pair is enforced in the master validator, where it produces a sentence instead of a primary-key violation.
- **One `AllocateFromStock` choke point, not a FEFO branch bolted onto each pick-list generator.** Both `GenerateWavePickList` and `GenerateBinPickList` had their own inline bin-selection query; both now call one function that picks FIFO or FEFO from the item's `tracking_mode`. FIFO's SQL is byte-for-byte what those blocks ran before, so a warehouse with no batch-tracked item gets the pick list it always got — locked by a test that exists solely to assert that. 42.2.8's strategy master changes where the answer comes from, not what it is.

**The bug worth recording, because the tests could not have found it.** The receipt gate — "a batch-tracked item must arrive with a lot number" — was originally only inside `PostGRNReceiptWithQC`. That function runs *after* the GRN document is written, and the GRN create hook cancels the receipt when posting fails. So a clerk omitting a lot number got a GLOBAL-0302 "unexpected error, contact support" with a silently cancelled GRN behind it. Every unit test passed, because they call the engine directly and never travel the document path. Live HTTP verification is what surfaced it. The fix extracts `ValidateReceiptBatchLine` and calls it from `validateGRNRules` (pre-write, the path a user actually takes) *and* keeps it at the posting choke point for callers that bypass the document validator — one definition so the two cannot drift. `wms_receiving.go`'s own header comment had already stated the rule I broke: that file owns *posting effect*, the validation file owns *field-shape checking*.

Two smaller things fell out. `PostInventoryLedgerWithVoucher` now carries `batch_no` on the line it already posts rather than the receipt writing a second batch-stamped entry — a second entry would double-count the same physical movement in every report that sums ledger quantity — and its idempotency key gains the lot when there is one, so two lots of one SKU on one receipt are two real movements instead of the second being swallowed as a replay. And four codes that had sat in the message matrix since Stage 23 with no callers finally have them: INV-0256 (FEFO violation), INVENT-0106 (batch expired), INVENT-0104 (blocked stock), INVENT-0115 (serial/batch mismatch). The matrix anticipated this feature; only the feature was missing.

Recall traceability is deliberately **two report definitions and no storage** — `batch-movement-history` reads the append-only stock ledger's new `batch_no`, `batch-stock-inquiry` answers "where is this lot now". That was the plan's own test of whether the 42.1.3 model was right, and it passes: neither direction needed a history table of its own.

**Verified**: `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./... -p 1` all clean, including the finance suite that historically false-fails; 8 new subtests in `engines/traceability_test.go`. Live-verified over real HTTP on a throwaway server (port 8143) as Super Admin: both receipt gates returning their catalog codes *before* the document is written, FEFO selecting the later-binned/earlier-expiring lot ahead of the older one (deliberately the case where FIFO and FEFO disagree — a FIFO regression would pass a weaker fixture), the bin-qty invariant refusing an over-assignment, quarantine removing a lot from allocation and reporting the shortfall, reasoned-release enforcement, consumption, and all three reports. All live fixtures removed afterwards, verified zero remaining.

**Still open in 42.1**: 42.1.7 (outbound lottable validation — `Batch.attributes` is built and ready to be consumed), 42.1.8/42.1.9 (serial register and inquiry — now the live blocker is decision **42.D3**, batch-and-serial or batch-only), 42.1.10 (UoM conversion), 42.1.11 (Code 128 symbology — gated on 42.D4). Automatic lot consumption at pick-scan is intentionally not here: `ScanPickItem` only updates the task's `picked_qty` and never touched `bin_stock` even before this Stage, so it belongs with 42.2.2's task-spine retrofit; `POST /api/v1/wms/batch/consume` closes the loop explicitly meanwhile.

## 100. Stage 36.2 — the PIM task & workflow engine, the biggest PIM gap (2026-08-15, code + schema + docs)

Asked to start building the open parity work, top of the list being **36 — PIM parity**, whose first named item is the task/workflow engine. It was also the thing 36.1.3 explicitly deferred ("task assignment: it follows 36.2's task engine, which does not exist yet"), so it is the item that unblocks the rest of Stage 36. Full item-by-item detail in `micro_checklist.md` Stage 36.2. A concurrent session held Stage 35.4 in the same tree throughout — see the note at the end.

**The distinction the whole stage rests on: an approval is not a task.** This codebase already has a maker-checker approval engine, and it was tempting to model tasks on it. That would have broken both. An approval asks "may this saved document proceed?", is answered once, by someone with the authority, about a document that already exists. A task says "someone must go and improve this product", is owned by a named person, has a due date, survives being half-done, and exists *precisely because* the product is not ready. An approval cannot be assigned, reassigned, or left open for a week; a task must never be able to move a document's approval state. So Stage 36.2 is four additive doctypes beside the approval engine, and no approval path was touched.

**Four decisions worth carrying forward.**

- **The task's state machine is the document `status` column, not a second field.** `handlers_core_doc_engine.go` already copies `data.status` into `documents.status`, so putting the vocabulary there means the generic list view, RBAC, soft delete, the audit trail, CSV export and every report understand a task's state without being taught. A parallel `task_status` field would have been one more thing only this module knew how to read.
- **Done is terminal, and that is a safety property rather than strictness.** A completed task may already have satisfied its workflow stage's exit condition and advanced the run past it, and there is no honest way to un-advance a run whose later stages have themselves created work. So the refusal names the alternative — a follow-up task, one button in the inbox — and the history stays truthful about what was actually finished.
- **The condition vocabulary is closed, and published from the same map the engine evaluates.** Stage conditions are seven named checks, each taking at most one operand, each implemented in Go. A workflow definition is authored by a category manager in a form, and a form that accepts an expression language is a remote-execution surface that also guarantees nobody can later tell what the definition does. `ListPIMWorkflowConditions` is generated from `pimWorkflowConditions` itself, so the form cannot offer a condition that never evaluates — and a test asserts the two cannot drift.
- **`AdvancePIMWorkflowRun` is idempotent, which is what makes three callers safe.** Run creation, every task completion, and an operator pressing Advance all call it, and none needs to know whether the others already did: on a run that cannot move it changes nothing but the recorded reason. Entry conditions are evaluated *before* leaving the current group, so a run held up by the next stage stays where it is with an explanation instead of being stranded between two stages. Advancing on task completion rather than from a sweeper is deliberate — a workflow whose progress waits on a cron feels broken to the person waiting on it.

**Reuse rather than new mechanism, throughout.** Product groups resolve through the *same* `ResolvePIMProductGroupItemCodes` seam 36.1.3's bulk edit and export use. Validation attaches at `ValidateDocument`'s shared exit, so a task written through the generic document API gets the same state machine the engine enforces. The three reports go through `RegisterReport`, arriving with the catalog's screen, parameter form, CSV export, scheduling and column redaction already attached. Bulk actions loop the single-item engines (the `BulkDecideApproval` precedent) so every per-item guard survives and a mixed selection reports exactly which rows refused. The inbox is a fourth tab in the existing PIM shell, so it needed no menu-map, view-router or menu-handler changes at all; templates and workflow definitions get their authoring surface from the generic doctype table.

**One endpoint was added because the feature is unusable without it.** `GET /api/v1/pim/assignable-users` exists because `/api/v1/admin/users` is Super Admin gated: a Store Manager who may create and reassign tasks could not otherwise see who to hand work to, and the assignee field degrades into a free-text box you spell from memory, where every typo is a task in nobody's inbox. It returns active users' username and role and nothing else, gated on the same *update* right that already lets you put a name on a task.

**Verified**: build/vet/`node --check` clean; the migration **applied** to the dev database and `-migrate-status` confirmed clean afterwards; seven new engine tests plus `go test ./... -p 1` fully green; every endpoint exercised over HTTP on a throwaway server (port 9107, stopped and released, all fixtures deleted); and a **real-browser pass** covering the inbox, the bulk bar, the detail modal, the run panel's state-dependent actions, and the Assign-task button on a real report row creating a real task — zero console errors, no horizontal overflow.

**Known limitation, stated rather than hidden**: editing a workflow definition while runs are mid-flight can orphan a run whose stored stage group no longer exists. Such a run is stopped with a named reason rather than silently restarted (restarting would re-create every task the product has already been through). Versioning definitions so in-flight runs keep travelling the shape they started on is the proper fix, and is not in 36.2's scope.

**A concurrent session was live in this tree throughout** (Stage 35.4 — `engines/shipping_package.go`, `pack_invoice.go`, `gate_pass.go`, `cancellation_credit_note.go`, its own migration and handlers, plus edits to `orders.go`/`order_invoice.go`/`marketplace.go`/`cmd/minttoken`). File sets are disjoint apart from `routes.go`, `public/app.js` and the three tracker docs, where both sessions appended in different regions. **Stage only the files listed above — never `git add -A` here.**

## 99. Stage 35.4 — the outbound document chain, and the pack object that made it possible (2026-08-15, code + schema)

Asked to start building Stages 35/36/37. That span is ~30-45 sessions by the parity plan's own sizing, so it was worked in the plan's dependency order rather than scaffolded shallowly across three stages: R1 was already done, and **R2's head item, 35.4**, is what this section covers. (A concurrent session picked up 36.2 in the same window — see the note at the end.)

**The one structural fact this rests on.** The chain ran FulfillmentTask → LogisticsBooking and stopped. That assumes one task ships as exactly one parcel — false the moment a packer needs two boxes — and it left nothing to hang an invoice off. That is precisely the reason 26.12.3 gave when it deferred invoice-from-pack: *"there is nothing real to generate the invoice from yet."* R1 built the order chain; 35.4.1 builds the pack object; the deferral's stated reason has therefore expired, and 35.4.2 closes it.

- **35.4.1 `ShippingPackage`** — Draft → Invoiced → Shipped, Cancelled as the exit, and only Draft is mutable. Built from a Packed task's `packed_qty` rather than `qty`, so a short-picked line ships short instead of being invoiced for units that are not in the box. Creation is idempotent on the task. Split refuses rather than clamps in both directions (moving every unit out; moving more than a line holds), sums duplicate SKU rows before validating, and writes both halves in one transaction.
- **35.4.2 Invoice from pack** — invoices *the package*, not the order, so a split order's parcels each carry their own correct tax document. Prices come from the order's own lines, never today's item master. Produces a Draft; posting stays a finance action.
- **35.4.3 Invoice → label → manifest ordering** — Uniware's hard rule, enforced as a refusal because a label is what authorises a parcel to move. The manifest half is a `WHERE` clause so an unlabelled parcel waits rather than being silently dropped. Binds only bookings that have a package, so every pre-35.4 booking is unaffected.
- **35.4.4 `GatePass`** — per vehicle, hung off the Manifest, taking carrier/location/count *from* it rather than from the caller. Vehicle number required at issue, not at creation.
- **35.4.5 Credit note on cancellation-after-invoice** — reuses the Stage 20c `CreditNote`; voids a Draft invoice rather than crediting it (nothing was posted, so there is nothing to reverse); wired into `CancelOrder` after the order is durably cancelled.

**Two defects found and fixed while building, both outside the nominal scope:**
- **The shared GST breakdown does not reconcile to the paisa.** `ComputeGSTForLinesMode` rounds each line's CGST/SGST halves independently and sums them, so a plain two-line 18% order totalling 900 produced **CGST+SGST = 137.30 against its own TotalTax of 137.28**; `TaxableAmount` is accumulated unrounded and would have been stored as `762.7118644067798`. On a tax document that is a defect, not a display nicety. Fixed in the new code only, via `reconcileInvoiceAmounts` — components sum to the tax total exactly, and taxable is derived so exempt/nil/zero-rated turnover stays out of the filed GSTR-3B 3.1(a) figure while still reconciling. Deliberately **not** applied to the shared engine, whose contract is that every existing sale-side caller (POS checkout among them) stays byte-identical.
- **The order-shipped path would have double-invoiced every package-invoiced order.** `CreateSalesInvoiceFromOrder` only ever looked for its own `INV-<orderID>`, and the handover cascade calls it on the Shipped transition. Its existence check is now against any invoice carrying that `sales_order_id`.

**Verified**: `go build`/`go vet` clean, `go test ./... -p 1` fully green including 12 new tests, then the whole chain over HTTP on a throwaway server (port 8093, stopped and released, fixtures removed) — package → split (3+1=4) → label refused → manifest refused → invoice (`635.59 + 114.41 = 750.00`) → label → manifest → gate pass → handover → package Shipped → credit note, idempotent on retry. Full per-item detail in [micro_checklist.md](micro_checklist.md) Stage 35.4.

**Concurrent-session note.** A second session was building Stage 36.2 (PIM task/workflow engine) in this same working tree throughout. Its in-progress files broke the package build for a while, so this stage's verification ran against an isolated copy — `git archive HEAD` plus only this session's own files — which is also why the migration was applied from that copy rather than the live tree, avoiding `db/migrate.go`'s `//go:embed *.sql` sweeping in the other session's unfinished migration. Both sessions' routes coexist in `routes.go`; nothing of theirs was staged, edited or reverted here.

## 98. Stage 44 — per-tenant hostnames, and the domain that unblocked go-live (2026-08-14, code + schema + proxy config + docs)

User registered **wholeops.in** and asked to close the domain-gated items. Two things came out of it: the go-live path was unblocked, and the URL scheme they described turned out not to exist.

**The scheme was checked before it was built.** The ask was `clientbrandname.wholeops.in/oms`. Reading the code first showed neither half was real: `apiMiddleware` never looked at the `Host` header (tenant came from `X-Tenant-ID`, then `?tenant_id=`, then the bearer token's claim — one hostname served every tenant), and the SPA is hash-routed, so the module segment is `#oms`. Saying so before building was the point; the alternative was shipping something adjacent and calling it done. The host→tenant half was then built; the path-routing half is left open as a decision (44.6), because the hostname half already delivers the per-client branding and the hash form costs nothing.

**The design's load-bearing choice is that the token stays authoritative.** Host resolution sits *ahead* of `X-Tenant-ID` in the pre-auth chain — a hostname had to survive DNS and a certificate to arrive, those headers are whatever the caller typed — but it never overrides a token claim. A session for tenant A presented on tenant B's hostname is refused with the same generic 401 as any dead session. "Host wins" would have been a genuine cross-tenant escalation; this is the narrower guarantee that the claim still scopes every query and the hostname merely has to agree.

- **`public.tenants.host_slug`**, additive, backfilled from `tenant_id` **only where `tenant_id` is already a legal DNS label**. That guard earned itself immediately: on the dev DB `default` backfilled and `tenant_new` correctly did not, because `_` is illegal in a hostname. A blind backfill would have minted a slug that could never be served, surfacing much later as an unexplained certificate failure.
- **On-demand TLS without a custom Caddy binary.** Per-tenant certificates are issued on first handshake rather than listed in config, gated by a new `/internal/tls-ask` endpoint the app answers — Caddy proceeds only on a 2xx, so a hostname that is not a live tenant never reaches Let's Encrypt. The wildcard-certificate alternative needs a DNS-01 provider plugin and therefore `xcaddy` and a custom binary to keep patched, which is the exact trade 26.1.3a already rejected. **Validation caught a real trap**: current Caddy has *removed* `on_demand_tls`'s `interval`/`burst` throttle, so `ask` is the only gate left — which is why the handler fails closed on every path, including unset config and a DB-less startup.
- **CORS widened by shape, not by list.** https origins at the base domain or one label under it are accepted, so provisioning a tenant no longer needs a restart with a longer `CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGINS`. The suffix test is anchored on `"." + base`, so a domain someone else registers ending in the same letters (`acme.evilwholeops.in`) is not mistaken for ours — locked by a test rather than left to reading.
- **Inert unless configured.** The whole feature is off without `TENANT_BASE_DOMAIN`: host resolution returns no match, CORS falls back to the old exact-match allowlist, and the ask gate refuses everything. That is what keeps the localhost and SSH-tunnel paths unchanged, and it is tested rather than assumed.

**Verified**: `go build ./...`, `go vet` clean; 40 new subtests green; full `internal/server` and `db` packages green; migration applied to the dev DB and its backfill inspected; `caddy validate` returns `Valid configuration` for the rendered config on the droplet. **Not yet verified**: anything end-to-end over real TLS. `wholeops.in` was delegated to Cloudflare the same day and the `.in` registry had not published the new nameservers before this was written, so 44.7 stays open and this section does not claim a live pass it has not had.

**On the go-live side, DNS is finished and TLS is not.** DNS was set up Cloudflare-first (zone added, `app` and `*` A records at `139.59.17.16`, both grey-cloud) deliberately: because the `.in` registry had not yet published *any* nameservers, switching to Cloudflare then cost one delegation instead of two. That completed, and both names now resolve correctly from public resolvers.

`enable_tls.sh` was then run for the first time since it was written in Stage 26.1.1 — and **first execution found two bugs that would have hit whoever ran it first, not just this session.** An apostrophe inside `${PUBLIC_IP:-this box's public IP}` opened a quote bash never closed, killing the script at parse time with an `unexpected EOF` that pointed at the last line rather than the culprit. Then, with that fixed, the reload failed `permission denied` on the access log: the script chowned `/var/log/caddy` *before* running `caddy validate`, and validate does not merely parse — it provisions every module, including opening the log, as root. Chowning first cannot help a file that does not exist yet. Both fixed; the ordering now has a comment explaining why it is load-bearing. **The generalisable finding: `caddy validate` has side effects on disk and is not a dry run.** The script's own auto-rollback worked correctly through both failures, which is why neither cost anything.

**What blocked TLS was a DigitalOcean cloud firewall, and the diagnosis is the part worth carrying forward.** ACME failed on both challenge types with `Timeout during connect (likely firewall problem)` — yet `ss -tlnp` showed Caddy listening on `*:80` and `*:443` and `ufw` allowed both, so every signal available *on the box* said the box was fine. **`tcpdump` during an external connection attempt settled it: zero inbound packets arrived at all.** That is the decisive observation, because tcpdump sits below netfilter for inbound traffic — a local iptables DROP would still have shown the SYNs. Their total absence meant they were being discarded upstream. The DO console then confirmed it exactly: a firewall named `Test` with a single inbound rule for SSH/22, which was all the droplet had ever needed while the ERP was tunnel-only. **The reusable rule: when a port is open locally but dead externally, reach for tcpdump rather than re-reading the local firewall.** Caddy was reverted to `Caddyfile.holding` while this was outstanding, so it would stop retrying into Let's Encrypt's failed-validation quota (2 of 5/hour used).

**With 80/443 opened, `enable_tls.sh` succeeded and production went live on HTTPS — closing 26.1.1's last leftover.** `https://app.wholeops.in` now serves the ERP behind a valid Let's Encrypt certificate (expires 2026-11-12, renewed automatically by Caddy), with `CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGINS` set and the app restarted. **Verified from the dev machine rather than from the droplet**, which is the distinction that matters here — checking `127.0.0.1` would have proved nothing about the very layer that had been broken: health and version 200, SPA and its assets 200 with correct MIME types, `http://` 308 to https, a real `USERAC-0021` 401 envelope from `POST /api/v1/login` (so TLS → Caddy → Go → error catalog all work end to end), `/api/v1/debug/panic` 404 confirming `ENV=production` still holds, all six security headers present and `Server`/`X-Powered-By` stripped. The SSH tunnel was left working.

**Stage 44 itself was deliberately not deployed in the same motion.** The bring-up used the pre-Stage-44 single-host Caddyfile already on the box, because installing the `on_demand_tls` config against a binary with no `/internal/tls-ask` route would have meant an ask gate that refuses every hostname. 26.1.3b (Cloudflare orange-cloud + `TRUSTED_PROXY_CIDRS` + origin lockdown) remains `[ ]`, and is now pure activation.

---

## 97. The dev cluster converted to UTF-8, closing the 20.6 defect (2026-08-13, database + one test + docs)

User asked for the three defects §96 recorded to be fixed. Two were already fixed in the tree and were re-verified rather than re-asserted: the SPA's relative asset paths (no relative `src`/`href` remains in `index.html`, no relative `fetch` in `app.js`) and the OpenAPI dynamic-schema trap (the paged envelope is inlined per endpoint, `PublicPage` is not a cached component). The third was real and outstanding.

**The dev cluster was WIN1252, so `₹` could not be stored and the migration runner stopped dead at Stage 37.1** — blocking 37.1.2, 38.2, 38.3 and 35.3.7 from ever reaching the database the tests actually run against. Fixed by conversion, not by weakening the migration: a currency master that cannot hold a currency symbol would have been the wrong repair.

- **Method**: `pg_dump --encoding=UTF8` → `CREATE DATABASE ... ENCODING 'UTF8' LOCALE_PROVIDER icu ICU_LOCALE 'en-US' TEMPLATE template0` → restore → `ALTER DATABASE ... RENAME`. **ICU `en-US` rather than `C`** is the decision worth recording: `C` collation is the path of least resistance on Windows and would have silently changed every text `ORDER BY` in the application to byte order, sorting uppercase before lowercase. PostgreSQL 16's ICU provider keeps the linguistic ordering the old `English_United States.1252` collation had.
- **Proven lossless before the swap, not after**: 127 tables compared with exact `count(*)` (not `reltuples` estimates) plus an `md5` checksum over the concatenated contents of `tenant_default.documents` — both identical. Only then were the databases renamed.
- **Reversible**: the originals are kept as `custom_erp_win1252_backup` / `custom_erp_test_win1252_backup`. Nothing was dropped. `custom_erp_test` was converted too, since it is the restore-drill target and would have hit the identical wall on the next drill.
- **Confirmed after**: all 5 blocked migrations applied; `₹` stored as 1 char / 3 bytes; CJK, Arabic and emoji now storable (verified server-side by byte length, because the Windows console mangles the *display* and would otherwise look like a failure); the startup `[WARN] server_encoding` line is gone, which is the same condition `ENV=production` hard-stops on; `go test ./... -p 1` green against the converted database.

**What the conversion revealed.** With the 35.3.7 migration finally applied to a database holding real history, `custom_erp` turned out to have **5,395 sweepable reservations out of 5,419** — 99.6% of every reservation in it was holding stock against a line that had been cancelled or deleted. The sweeper drained it to zero with no negative `reserved` counts. That is the leak the stage was written for, measured rather than argued.

It also **caught a wrong assumption in the sweeper's own test**. The sweep is batched at 500 rows per call — deliberate, so one long transaction cannot hold locks the live order path needs — so a single call on a database with a five-thousand-row backlog never reaches a freshly inserted fixture. The test passed against a pristine throwaway database and failed against the real one. The test now drains in a bounded loop, which is what the 5-minute worker does over successive ticks, and fails rather than hangs if the sweep ever stops converging. The batching itself was correct and was not changed.

**The cluster itself was finished on 2026-08-13** (same day, recorded here rather than as a separate section because it closes the same defect). The conversion above fixed the two application databases, but left the thing that *mints* new ones: a bare `CREATE DATABASE` copies `template1`, and `template1` was still WIN1252 — so every new database on this box was still born unable to hold `₹`. `template1` was pristine (nothing in it but `plpgsql`), so it was dropped and recreated from `template0` with the same `ENCODING 'UTF8' LOCALE_PROVIDER icu LOCALE 'en-US'` clause the application databases got, which touched no user data. The clause was proven on a throwaway database *before* `template1` was dropped, so the cluster was never left without one. **Verified the way that actually matters**: a database created with no encoding clause whatsoever now comes out UTF8 + ICU `en-US` and stores `₹`, CJK and emoji correctly (4 chars / 13 bytes).

- **`template0` is still WIN1252, and that is correct, not an oversight.** It cannot be re-encoded without `initdb`, and it does not need to be: it is precisely the template a *differing* encoding may legally be copied from, which is exactly what makes every explicit `TEMPLATE template0 ENCODING 'UTF8'` clause in this repo work regardless of the cluster default.
- **The `postgres` maintenance DB is still WIN1252, deliberately.** It is not what new databases are copied from, so it mints nothing broken. It holds 20 empty tables and a single `test-tenant` row — debris from the retired standalone WMS project — and recreating it would have destroyed those schema objects for no encoding benefit. Left alone by explicit decision.
- **The two WIN1252 backups are retained by explicit decision**, pending a live UI pass against the converted database. `custom_erp_win1252_backup` (62 MB) and `custom_erp_test_win1252_backup` (15 MB). `ditos_erp` is an unrelated database and was never touched.

**Pushed and deployed the same day.** `main` went to `origin/main` for the first time (95 commits; it had been local-only) and `deploy.ps1` put `b572196` on the droplet. **Worth being honest about what that deploy did:** nothing functional. Every Go change since the deployed `20a8878` was a comment or a test file, and `public/`, `internal/` and `db/*.sql` were untouched — so the only real effects were re-stamping the ldflags build identity (so `/api/v1/version` stops claiming `20a8878`) and hand-shipping the `restore_drill.sh` fix that `deploy.ps1` structurally cannot carry. 0 migrations applied, 91 already current. Full detail and the verification list in `ai_handover.md` §6.

---

## 96. Stages 35.3.7 / 36 / 37.1.2 / 38 / 39 — the platform layer: public API, Knowledge Center, multi-currency (2026-08-12, code + schema + docs)

User handed over a ranked gap list and an explicit build order, deduplicated across the two: finish 36.1.3 and 36.7.2, build 38.3/38.5/38.9 together, open the first read-only 38.1 routes, build 38.8, land 39.2-39.7 as one safe unit, then 37.1.2 — plus 35.3.7, the correctness gap they ranked first. All eight built. Item-by-item detail in `micro_checklist.md`.

**One theme runs through the whole batch: attach behaviour at the choke point that already exists.** The currency stamp hangs off `ValidateDocument`, so the generic API, CSV import and every engine get it without a sweep. Group bulk edit resolves to ids and hands them to the *existing* `BulkUpdateDocuments`, inheriting its cap, validation, variant-uniqueness and re-approval rules rather than reimplementing them. The group export refactors `GetSearchFeedExportCSV` into a shared row reader so two exports cannot disagree about a product. The public API's OpenAPI spec is generated from the same route table the server registers from. None of these needed a second way of doing anything.

- **35.3.7 — reservations become attributable, and the table finally gets swept.** `inventory_reservation` gains nullable `order_id`/`line_id`; releasing a line now releases *that line's* row, with the old (sku, location, quantity) heuristic surviving only for pre-migration rows. **The sweeper deliberately refuses to expire an order's reservation on time alone** — `inventory.reservation_ttl_seconds` is a cart-hold TTL, and applying it to a confirmed order would release stock out from under a live order the moment it queued longer than the TTL, which is a far worse bug than the leak being fixed. Unattributed holds expire on `expires_at`; attributed ones only when their line is gone, deleted or no longer holding stock. The test asserts the negative case first.
- **36.1.3 / 36.7.2 — two seams that existed but had no callers.** The product-group resolver and the content-assist endpoint were both built earlier with no production consumer. Groups now drive bulk edit and CSV export from one modal on the Item screen; the Workbench has an **Assist** button that fills the content form and never posts, which is exactly the human-in-the-loop guarantee 26.4.11's design rests on.
- **38.3 + 38.5 + 38.9 — one mechanism, not three.** A public request is admitted, de-duplicated and recorded by the same middleware pass, and the traffic log is what makes the daily quota survive a restart. Built together because shipping any one alone leaves the surface unbounded, unsafe to retry, or unobservable. `publicAPIMiddleware` is a *second* middleware rather than a branch in `apiMiddleware`: a human session presents a JWT and inherits a role, an integration presents an opaque key and holds only its explicit scopes, and folding them together is precisely how a public route inherits a human's permissions.
- **38.1 — four read-only endpoints, each a curated projection.** Not a document dump: publishing the generic document shape would turn every future internal field into an accidental public contract. No cost or margin on a product; availability without the held-back buckets; order tracking that accepts the caller's own channel order id. Paging clamped at 200. Read-only by choice — the idempotency spine is already underneath for when writes are curated.
- **38.8 — the spec cannot drift.** One declarative route table read by both the registration loop and the generator; schemas reflected from the declared Go response types. Renaming a json tag changes the published contract in the same commit.
- **39.2-39.7 — a Knowledge Center that is authenticated by construction.** The generated output goes to `internal/kb/content/` and is embedded in the binary, *not* to `public/`, because anything under `public/` is served straight off disk by `http.FileServer` — so "authenticated by default" is a property of where the content lives rather than a check someone can forget. The public subset is rebuilt narrowed, never filtered in the browser, since an index listing an internal article's title has already leaked it. Stdlib only: no static-site generator, no bundler, no search service.
- **37.1.2 — dual currency without touching a single report.** `gl_postings.debit`/`credit` keep meaning functional currency, because every report, trial balance and GST return sums them and none is being taught about currency; the transaction amounts arrive in new columns beside them. A real correctness fix falls out: approval slabs are written in the tenant's currency and would have compared a USD 100 invoice against an INR threshold.

**Three defects found and fixed outside the nominal scope, all worth remembering:**

1. **The SPA could not boot on any two-segment URL.** `index.html` referenced its assets relatively, so `/help/<article>` resolved `styles.css` to `/help/styles.css`, got the SPA shell back, and the browser refused every stylesheet and script on MIME grounds. Single-segment product prefixes like `/wms` never exposed it; the first two-segment URL did. Five references made absolute, which also hardens `/wms/<anything>` and every future deep link.
2. **The dev database could no longer take migrations — since fixed (see §97).** `migrations_stage37_1_currency_foundation.sql` seeds `₹` and the local cluster was **WIN1252** — the 20.6 defect, finally biting. Migrations stop at the first failure, so everything from 37.1 onward was blocked there. The migrations themselves were fine: all 92 apply cleanly to UTF-8, which is where this session's verification ran. `db/migrate.go` now recognises SQLSTATE 22P05 and explains it instead of leaving someone debugging their SQL.
3. **A latent OpenAPI trap, caught before it shipped.** A type whose shape depends on its example value (the paged envelope's `interface{} Data`) must not be cached as a named component: two endpoints returning `PublicPage` carry different `Data`, and one named schema could only describe one of them while silently mis-documenting the other.

**Verified**: `go build`/`go vet`/`node --check` clean; `go test ./... -p 1` fully green across all four packages; every new endpoint exercised over real HTTP on a throwaway UTF-8 database and server (port 9101 — both destroyed afterwards, along with the seeded fixtures), including the full 429/quota/traffic-log cycle; and a real-browser Playwright pass over the Knowledge Center shell, search, contextual drawer, the Group Actions modal and the Assist button — **zero console errors**, no horizontal overflow. `cmd/minttoken` was added to the tree because every verification pass otherwise rewrites the same throwaway token minter.

**Concurrent-session note**: §95 (Stage 43) records that this session's in-flight `registerPublicAPIV1Routes` blocked `internal/server`'s compile mid-flight. That is resolved — the package builds and its tests pass. File sets are otherwise disjoint; **stage only reviewed files, never `git add -A`.**

## 95. Stage 43 — Go-live blocker sweep: the buildable half of the parked items (2026-08-12, code + docs)

User handed back eight parked items from `go_live_decisions.md` — escalation contacts and the alert webhook, connector/GSP/e-way/payment-terminal credentials, Cloudflare zone access, a pen-test vendor, UAT participants and hypercare sign-off, real QZ Tray printer verification, the competitor-scraping legal call, and the data mart — with "build from this which is possible… skip which needs decision for now."

Five of the eight are genuinely credential- or decision-shaped and stay open. Three had ungated repository work behind them, and the operating lesson is that **"blocked on a vendor" is not the same as "blocked on everything"** — one of the three turned out to be a live security defect that had been sitting in the tree the whole time, parked behind an item nobody could close.

- **43.1 Token claim delimiter smuggling — fixed.** The P1 that `pentest_scope.md` §4 names first, closed in-house instead of waiting for 26.11.1's vendor. Token payloads are a flat `k=v&k=v` string built with `fmt.Sprintf`, and every value in one comes from a column with no character restrictions, so a `&` or `=` in a username or location code changed the payload's *shape* — and the server then signed the forged result, so it arrived with a valid HMAC. Four cases, each written as a failing test first: `purpose=extension` smuggled through a username made `apiMiddleware` skip the Stage 29.8 live user-state re-check (so **deactivating that account stopped ending its session**); a location code overrode `role` and `tenant`, which are emitted before it under a last-write-wins parser; an MFA-challenge token could be given the `role` claim `SignPurposeToken` deliberately omits; and an `=` in a value silently dropped the claim entirely, emptying `Resolved-Username` in the audit trail. Both write paths are `requireHRAdmin`-gated, so the honest severity is insider persistence/escalation rather than anonymous remote escalation. Fixed by escaping values at the one choke point all three issuers share (`claimVal`/`url.QueryEscape`, with `SplitN` + unescape on parse), backward-compatible with tokens already in flight.
- **43.2 Nightly-backup failure and absence now alert.** Closes 26.11.7's own still-open note and clears one of `hypercare_plan.md`'s two stated preconditions. `deploy/backup.sh` gained ERR/EXIT traps that POST failures to the same `OPS_ALERT_WEBHOOK_URL` the Go server uses, sending only the failing command's first word so credential-bearing arguments never leave the box; and `StartBackupFreshnessMonitor` watches the backup *artifact's* age hourly, which is the half a script cannot cover — 26.11.3 found production had never run a nightly backup at all, and a cron that never fires produces no failure to report. Deliberately not exposed on `/api/v1/health`, which is public and would hand an unauthenticated caller the backup schedule and paths.
- **43.3 `X-Forwarded-For` parsed right-to-left.** §5 pre-specified this as the code-side prerequisite for Cloudflare while recording "no ungated repository work pending" — this was the part that was ungated. `clientIP` took the left-most entry, which is the end of the header a client writes; the app was correct in practice only because `deploy/Caddyfile` replaces forged headers, leaving a silent total bypass of the 5/min/IP auth limit one config line away. Now walks right-to-left past known proxies (loopback + private ranges, extensible via the new `TRUSTED_PROXY_CIDRS`), which also makes Cloudflare activation an env-var change rather than a code change.

**Verified**: `go build ./...`, `go vet`, `go test ./engines/ -p 1` clean; the backup webhook confirmed end-to-end against a mock receiver (real POST, valid JSON, names the failing stage, no secrets). `internal/server`'s 10 `TestClientIP` cases passed before a concurrent session's in-flight Stage 38 Public API work started blocking that package's compile (`registerPublicAPIV1Routes` referenced but not yet defined) — unrelated to this stage, to be re-run once it lands. Per-item detail in micro_checklist.md Stage 43, including the table of which parked items stay open and why.

---

## 94. Stage 38/39 foundations — scoped API identities and safe Markdown rendering (2026-08-11, code + one unapplied migration + docs)

User asked to start Platform & Extensibility and the Knowledge Center while the shared tree still contained concurrent Stage 35-42 work. Both starts were therefore taken at dependency foundations that add no live surface: external credentials before public routes, and a renderer before generated static help.

**Stage 38 — an integration identity that is not a person.** The repo already had `SignExtensionToken`, but that is deliberately a short-lived read token for exactly one doctype and cannot be rotated or revoked individually. It was not widened. New `api_credentials` is a tenant-local raw security table rather than a generic document, because generic document APIs expose stored JSON and a credential digest should never be retrievable. A key contains 256 bits from `crypto/rand`; plaintext is returned exactly once and only a SHA-256 digest plus operator-visible prefix is stored. SHA-256 is appropriate here because the input is high-entropy random material, not a human password. Scopes come from a small allowlist, expiry is optional, authentication uses constant-time comparison, and every malformed/unknown/revoked/expired/mismatched case returns the same error so prefixes cannot be enumerated.

New private Super Admin routes issue, list, atomically rotate and revoke credentials. Rotation inserts the replacement and revokes the predecessor in one transaction; issue/rotate responses use `Cache-Control: no-store`; list responses never contain plaintext or digest; lifecycle actions use the existing audit log. `engines.ProvisionTenantSchema` now clones the table for future tenants. The contract and first scope catalog are fixed in `docs/specs/public_api_v1.md`, but **no `/api/public/v1` business route exists yet**. This is the key safety boundary: per-credential limiting (38.3) and mutation idempotency (38.5) come before a key is accepted on external traffic. OAuth2 client credentials also remain open rather than being implied by the opaque-key implementation.

**Stage 39 — render safely before serving anything.** New `internal/kb` is a stdlib-only Markdown renderer covering the planned headings/lists/tables/fenced-code/links/emphasis/images/admonitions vocabulary. It produces stable duplicate-safe heading anchors, escapes all raw HTML, and allowlists URL schemes rather than trying to blacklist every script spelling. Tests cover the vocabulary plus script/data URLs, raw HTML, invalid UTF-8, malformed emphasis and an unclosed code fence. `cmd/genkb` and `public/help/` were deliberately not created: generated files under the existing static root would become public before 39.6's authenticated-by-default access path exists.

**Concurrency and verification.** New migration `db/migrations_stage38_2_api_credentials.sql` was executed in PostgreSQL inside `BEGIN`/`ROLLBACK` and is not applied or recorded. An end-to-end engine test created a uniquely named scratch tenant/schema, exercised issue → authenticate → rotate → old-key rejection → revoke → new-key rejection, then removed both; follow-up queries confirmed zero scratch rows/schemas. The first aggregate suite run caught one real pre-migration issue: `ProvisionTenantSchema` tried to clone `api_credentials` while its intentionally-unapplied template table did not exist. It now skips only that new table during the pre-migration window and clones it normally afterward; the exact engine/server provisioning tests pass. A later aggregate run surfaced the pre-existing WMS slotting fixture transiently empty; its exact test passed immediately in isolation and the final full `engines` package passed. Final bounded package runs are green for `db`, `engines`, `internal/kb`, `internal/server` and every `cmd`, with `go build ./...`, `go vet ./...` and `node --check public/app.js` clean. No process was restarted, no public route or frontend file was changed, and no unrelated concurrent file was replaced.

---

## 93. Stage 35 release train R1 — the OMS becomes a real product (2026-08-11, code + schema + docs)

User asked to work the open plan phase by phase. The open plan is the Parity Master Plan's Stages 35-39, and its own sequencing puts release train **R1 = 35.1 + 35.2 + 35.3** first, because those three items exist to clear the three structural debts everything downstream assumes are gone. All three are now closed. Full item-by-item detail in `micro_checklist.md` Stage 35.

**The plan's premise was checked before it was built on, and was partly stale.** `ImportChannelSalesOrder` already routed Shopify and Unicommerce onto `CreateSalesOrder` (commit `efed245`, 2026-07-27), and 35.1.4's notification wiring was already in place in `evaluateOrderShipmentClosure` and `RecordDeliveryEvent`. Building 35.1 as written would have re-done work that existed. What was actually open was different, and worse:

- **35.1.1** The two legacy importers (`ImportChannelOrder`, `ImportUnicommerceOrder`) never created the POSCart their own comments promised — they wrote reservations plus a `channel_order_mapping` row and *no document at all*, so a legacy channel import left stock reserved against nothing. Both are now thin adapters onto the SalesOrder path, keeping their `ORDER_ALREADY_IMPORTED` replay contract. The loose-map adapter also fixes a latent qty-0 bug (`item["qty"].(int)` never matches a `json.Unmarshal`'d `float64`).
- **35.1.2** The **BigCommerce webhook verified, audited, and then discarded every order**. Its hook body carries only `{scope, data:{type,id}}`, so `ImportBigCommerceOrder` now reads the order back over the v2 API and imports it (credential-gated, 26.2.x shape; answers 200 on failure so a bad SKU can't cost the store its subscription). The **Magento poller counted changed orders and logged the number** — it now imports them. The `unicommerce.order.imported` outbox event, dropped by the 26.12.1 rewire, is restored.
- **35.1.3** Decision: no backfill. New `orphaned-channel-orders` report lists pre-35.1 debris. Building it exposed a real defect — a dangling mapping row was honoured without checking the order existed, which made every legacy channel order *permanently un-importable*; now guarded, with `DO UPDATE` so the table converges on truth.
- **35.2** New `engines/oms_console.go` + `handlers_oms_console.go` + 8 routes. The old screen pulled four doctypes in full and joined them in the browser; filtering, counting and joining moved to SQL, and the browser now gets one page plus facet counts. Faceted list with saved views, one-call order detail (9 sections), report-backed tiles, bulk actions with per-order outcomes, and global search across order id / channel order id / AWB / phone / name / SKU.
- **35.3** New `engines/order_mutations.go` + `handlers_order_mutations.go` + 7 routes: item-level hold, order edit re-validated through the *same* `validateOrderChain`, switch facility (reserving at the destination before releasing at the source), priority honoured by a real pick worklist, and split into fulfilment groups rather than into a second order. All six gated through Stage 29.8's existing `lookupStatusTransitionRule` — `isCancellationBlocked`'s inline copy was consolidated onto it rather than a second reader being added.

**Two bugs found and fixed outside R1's nominal scope, both worth remembering:**

1. **SLA breach detection had never fired.** `documents.created_at` is a bare `TIMESTAMP` holding the database server's *local* wall clock, but `lib/pq` returns it tagged UTC. `GetSLABreaches`' `time.Since(created_at)` therefore reported every open task as 330 minutes *in the future* on this Asia/Calcutta deployment, so `elapsed > threshold` was false for anything younger than 5.5h plus the threshold. Elapsed time is now computed in SQL. The console's SLA tile reads **230** where it would have read 0. The test fixture that "proved" the old behaviour was inserting `time.Now().UTC()` — a frame nothing else in the application writes — so it agreed with the buggy reader and disagreed with every real row.
2. **`.stats-grid` and `.stat-value` do not exist in `styles.css`.** The old OMS screen used both; that is why its tiles rendered as full-width stacked bars with an unstyled number. Corrected to the real `dashboard-stats-row` / `.stat-val`.

**Verified**: `go build`/`go vet`/`node --check` clean, `go test ./... -p 1` fully green, every new endpoint exercised over HTTP on a throwaway server (:8097, stopped and released, scratch accounts deleted), and a real-browser Playwright pass — 4 tiles, 7 facets, working pager, detail modal with 7 actions and all 9 sections, zero console errors, no horizontal overflow.

**Known limitation, recorded rather than hidden**: `inventory_reservation` has no order or line reference, so reservations can only be matched to an order by (sku, location) pair — correct in aggregate, not provably per-line under concurrency. And nothing anywhere sweeps that table: `expires_at` is written and indexed but never read, so reservations are effectively permanent. Carried as 35.3.7.

**A concurrent session was live in this tree throughout** (Stage 36.1/37.1, §92 below). File sets are disjoint — that session owns `engines/pim_*`/currency and its own migrations; this one owns `engines/oms_console.go`, `order_mutations.go`, `channel_orders.go`, `sourcing.go`, `unicommerce.go`, `connector_bigcommerce.go`, `connector_magento.go`, `oms_reports.go`, `optimization.go`, `orders.go` and `db/migrations_stage35_2_oms_console.sql`. **Stage only those files — never `git add -A` here.**

## 92. Stage 36.1 / 37.1 foundations — Product Groups and effective-dated currency rates (2026-08-11, code + schema + docs)

User explicitly asked to start both Stage 36 and Stage 37 while protecting the ongoing session and partially completed work. The implementation therefore begins at each stage's dependency foundation, is additive, avoids `public/app.js`, and leaves both new migrations unapplied. This request also resolves parity-plan decisions D6 and D7: multi-currency, multi-entity, Projects and Service are real build scope, to be taken in plan order rather than skipped as speculative.

**Stage 36.1 — usable product groups without a parallel UI framework.** New `PIMProductGroup` is a normal PIM Master, so existing generic forms, CSV import, audit history and role permissions do the administrative work. Static groups store validated Item links. Dynamic groups use four typed fields—family, completeness-below, missing attribute and item status—mapped to the Report Catalog's existing flat parameter vocabulary; filled filters are ANDed and recalculated from current product data on every resolve. There is deliberately no saved query language or materialised membership to secure and refresh. `ResolvePIMProductGroupItemCodes` is the common downstream seam, `GET /api/v1/pim/product-groups/{id}/members` provides a permission-checked read path, and the registered **PIM Product Group Readiness** report makes current membership/completeness/missing fields usable from the existing Reports screen. This closes 36.1.1, 36.1.2 and the readiness-report part of 36.1.3; bulk actions, task assignment and export consumers remain open.

**Stage 37.1 — exchange-rate truth before posting behaviour.** New `Currency` and `ExchangeRate` Finance Masters carry ISO currency metadata, Spot/Average/Closing rate types, effective date windows and Manual/Imported provenance. INR alone is seeded because that was the application's existing implicit base; foreign currencies are not guessed. Partial unique indexes prevent ambiguous currency codes and duplicate active rate starts. A shared resolver accepts record IDs or currency codes, selects the latest applicable direct rate, safely inverts a reverse rate, returns identity for the same currency and emits `FIN-0021` when no rate exists. Validation is attached at `ValidateDocument`, so API saves and generic CSV imports share it. No existing financial document or posting path consumes these rates yet—37.1.2 remains the explicit point where transaction and functional amounts begin—so the foundation is non-behavioural for current work.

**Concurrency and verification.** New migrations are `db/migrations_stage36_1_product_groups.sql` and `db/migrations_stage37_1_currency_foundation.sql`. Each was executed through PostgreSQL inside an explicit `BEGIN`/`ROLLBACK`, including all tenant-propagation blocks and indexes, and neither was recorded or applied. Focused tests cover validation, static/dynamic resolution, the readiness report, effective dating, direct/inverse rates and the missing-rate failure. Final `go build ./...`, `go vet ./...` and `go test ./... -p 1` are clean. No server was restarted, no shared schema was changed, no live data was seeded, and no concurrent Stage 35/40/41/42 file was replaced.

## 91. Stage 42 — WMS parity plan against SAP EWM and Infor WMS (2026-08-11, planning only, no code)

User request: review the WMS module of SAP EWM and Infor WMS thoroughly, prepare a plan, update the checklist. Both benchmark URLs were supplied. Output is `docs/specs/wms_parity_plan.md` (the source of detail) plus the Stage 42 tracker skeleton in `micro_checklist.md` — the same two-file relationship Stages 35-39 have to `parity_master_plan.md`. **No source, schema or config was touched.**

**Sourcing, stated honestly because it affects how the plan should be trusted.** Infor WMS 11.5.x was retrieved and parsed in full — 28 TOC sections, and every `Infor §n` citation in the plan is verbatim. SAP was **not** retrievable: `help.sap.com` serves a ~1 KB JavaScript shell to any non-browser client, and its content API (`http.svc/deliverable`, `http.svc/pagecontent`) answers `Access denied` / `Internal Server Error` unauthenticated. Four fetch strategies plus the EWM 9.5 Master Guide PDF all failed. The SAP column is therefore reconstructed from indexed EWM application-help node titles plus EWM's established component structure — directionally right, not a verbatim read. The plan says so at the top and flags that any item justified *only* by a SAP row needs a browser check first.

**The finding.** Stage 20 Track B.2 + Stage 26.5 (16 items, closed 2026-07-25/27) built a broad WMS *feature* surface that holds up well against both TOCs — putaway, cross-dock, LPN, wave picking, cartonization, ABC counting, blind recounts, slotting, labour productivity, 3PL billing, a robotics endpoint, voice picking. But two things both benchmarks treat as foundational are grep-verified absent:

1. **No lot/batch or serial traceability and no UoM conversion.** No `batch_no`, no `serial` on stock, no `expiry`/`shelf_life`, no `conversion_factor` anywhere in `engines/` or `db/`; the only serial in the tree is `Asset.serial_number` (`db/migration.sql:781`), and the single `Batch` occurrence (`db/migrations_stage14a_modules.sql:86`) is a module mapping for a doctype that was never created. Consequence: this WMS cannot be sold into food, pharma, cosmetics or electronics, and FEFO, recall traceability, expiry blocking and lottable pick constraints are all unbuildable. Infor spends three sections on this; SAP makes it warehouse-level configuration.
2. **No generic Warehouse Task object.** Both products are architecturally task systems (SAP's Warehouse Task/Order + WOCR; Infor's Task Manager + Task Dispatch Strategy). This repo has parallel unrelated actions — `PutawayToBin`, `ExecuteBinReplenishment`, `ScanPickItem`, `PostCycleCountAdjustment` — with no shared queue, priority, assignment or ageing. That is precisely *why* 26.5.13 could only instrument three of them, and why there is no warehouse cockpit.

**The plan.** 33 gap rows, 58 items across 6 phases, ≈34-44 sessions (~2-2.5 months), foundation-first: 42.1 traceability (11), 42.2 the task spine (10), 42.3 inbound depth (10), 42.4 outbound depth (11), 42.5 inventory control (8), 42.6 labour + billing (8). Grouped into release trains W1-W6, of which W1 (traceability) is the single largest commercial unlock and W6 (3PL billing) should only be built against a real prospect. Three previously-documented carve-outs get closed as side effects: 26.5.13's uninstrumented picking (by 42.2.2), 26.5.15's report-only billing with no invoice (by 42.6.8), and its storage-charge approximation (by 42.6.9). The scannable-barcode gap first flagged in `wms_master_blueprint_reference.md` §3 is still open and becomes 42.1.11.

**Honest target, carried into the checklist.** Infor WMS is the benchmark to actually aim at — right shape, right size, ~70% of its TOC achievable here. **SAP EWM feature-list parity is explicitly not being attempted**, the same call §0 of `parity_master_plan.md` made about S/4HANA; SAP is read as a concept checklist only. Seven things are named as out of scope on the record (MFS/PLC control, Infor's 3D yard view, SAP's RF/ITSmobile and resource-group constructs, engineered time-study tooling, EDI mapping depth, a separate Warehouse Order object, voice-picking vendor SDKs). Six open decisions are logged as 42.D1-42.D6 — regulated category, 3PL/multi-owner, serial-or-batch-only, scanner hardware, the wave scheduler, and the `Zone`-to-master migration on live tenant data. **42.1 can start regardless of all six.**

This complements rather than competes with Stages 35-39: that plan is OMS/PIM/console work, this is warehouse-floor work. Two touchpoints noted — 35.1 should land before 42.4.1, and 35.7 overlaps 42.4.11.

Two checklist entries mixed completed engineering decisions with nonexistent or externally gated work. **23.11 is now closed as not applicable, not implemented**: the catalog's one-row inline-grid style has no paste-into-grid product surface or roadmap item, so leaving it unchecked permanently misreported an already-settled scope decision as unfinished work. Stage 23 is fully closed and moved to the closed-stage archive.

**26.1.3 is split along the real ownership boundary.** 26.1.3a records the already-complete repository work (Caddy hardening, trustworthy forwarded IPs for the current direct-to-origin path, application security headers, and the deliberate decision to keep rate limiting in the app rather than maintain a custom Caddy build). 26.1.3b is the only remaining action: activate Cloudflare once the user supplies a public domain and access to its Cloudflare zone. That activation must add Cloudflare's current published CIDRs to Caddy's trusted proxies, enable strict right-to-left forwarded-IP parsing, verify client-IP propagation, and lock the origin firewall to those same ranges. This was documentation-only reconciliation. No source, schema, executable deployment behavior, running service, or in-progress Stage 40/41 work was changed.
