## 89. Stage 41 — user-reported batch: schema editing, sticky headers, country-aware phone data, setup guidance, POS location (2026-08-10, code + schema + docs)

Five gaps reported directly by the user. **Numbered Stage 41, not 34.4** — 34.4 is the gated JSON-endpoint harvester in Stage 34, blocked behind 34.6's legal/ToS decision, so reusing that number would have read as the gate being crossed. Same reasoning §88 gives for Stage 40 not being 35. Full item detail in `micro_checklist.md` Stage 41.

**What was actually wrong, and the shape of each fix:**

**Schema Design could not edit.** `SaveFieldDefinition` is create-only by design, so correcting a typo'd label meant delete-and-recreate — which discards that field's data on every existing document. Added the missing half (`UpdateFieldDefinition`, addressed by row id so the fieldname itself is editable) and wired up `#add-field-modal`, which turned out to have been sitting in `index.html` since it was written with **nothing referencing it**, styled with a `form-control` class this stylesheet does not define. The screen had been using a chain of six `prompt()` dialogs instead.

**Sticky headers were declared unfixable and were not.** The existing comment correctly diagnosed CSS2.1 §17.5.1's table paint order and then drew the wrong conclusion, listing four failed attempts. All four fought the paint order; the fix is to leave it — moving `position: sticky` from the `thead` **row group** onto the `<th>` **cells** puts them in the positioned-descendants layer, above every in-flow `<td>`.

**Phone data had no country and was never cleaned.** One hardcoded Indian regexp, applied to exactly one master, rejecting correctly-formatted numbers for containing a space and letting the same customer exist three times under three spellings. Replaced with a 54-country table and a normalizer, attached at `ValidateDocument` so every phone field in the schema is cleaned without per-screen wiring. The design point is the **separation**: cleaning is unconditional and never rejects; validation is a policy the caller applies. Masters stay strict; channel orders are cleaned, tagged with their origin country and **saved regardless**, because the existing hold codes all answer "can this be fulfilled?" and a contact number cannot.

**Setup guidance now travels to where the user is.** Stage 30.5.1 could only speak after a list had been queried and come back empty. `GET /api/v1/setup/status` returns per-Master counts in one query, so any screen can answer "is X set up?" instantly. Three surfaces share one three-sentence vocabulary, attached at the two existing single doors (`attachLinkTypeahead` for ~45 pickers, `renderView` for every screen). Loud when the user is genuinely blocked, focus-only when they are not, permission-aware throughout, and never a dialog. Deep links (`#/setup/<Doctype>`) were added so "open in a new tab" is a real link rather than a promise — this app had no addressable views at all before.

**POS showed a code where a cashier needs a name.** Split into a visible name box and a hidden code input, so the fifteen downstream callers that key off the code needed no change.

**Three bugs found during verification, all fixed:** a foreign number was being retagged as domestic (the country was read after the pass that stripped the evidence); `phone_country` collided with Stage 40.2's field-format matcher, so the server rejected a value it had just written itself (fixed with a shared `IsDerivedCompanionField` both sides consult); and `rawSettingForSchema` **panicked** on a nil `db.DB` despite its own contract promising a config read can never crash a request path.

Verified with `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./... -p 1` clean, plus live HTTP and real-browser checks on a throwaway server as both a Store Manager and a Super Admin.

---

## 88. Stage 40 — user-reported batch: PO line items, field formats, the Super Admin rename, speed and motion (2026-08-10, code + schema + docs)

Six gaps reported directly by the user in one pass, built in the priority order they chose. **Numbered Stage 40, not 35** — Stages 35-39 are already allocated to the Parity Master Plan (§86), and 35.1/35.5/35.6 there mean entirely different things.

- **40.1 Purchase Order line items, GST treatment and place of supply.** The PO screen had no item selection at all: `createDraftPurchaseOrder` literally posted `items: '[]'` and asked for one hand-typed header total, so a PO recorded what it cost but never what was bought. It now edits real lines (Item / Qty / Purchase Price / optional MRP) with HSN, GST %, taxable, tax and line total resolved **server-side** and shown read-only, so tax classification keeps one source of truth. `PurchaseOrder.items` became a `JSONTable` (the Stage 30.5.3 pattern, storage unchanged). Two new decisions became configuration rather than assumptions: **inclusive vs exclusive** purchase pricing (`procurement.po_gst_mode`, defaults Exclusive, overridable per PO — `ComputeGSTForLinesMode` splits it out so every sale-side caller keeps its existing inclusive behaviour byte-identical), and **inter-state vs intra-state**, now *derived* by comparing the buying Location's Legal Entity state against the Vendor's (GSTIN's first two digits, falling back to a new `Vendor.state`) at the generic-doc choke point via `ApplyPlaceOfSupply`, with an explicit human override that survives later saves. An underived supply type reports *why* rather than silently defaulting to intra-state. New `engines/purchase_order.go` prices lines once for the screen, the printed copy and the vendor's copy alike; new A4 **print sheet** (both parties, GSTIN, HSN table, tax split, amount in words via new `engines/amount_words.go`, Indian digit grouping) with **MRP stripped server-side**, and **Send to Vendor** recording the dispatch through the existing `DispatchNotification` engine with a `mailto:` fallback. `amendPurchaseOrder` replaced its four-`prompt()` chain with the same composer, so an amendment can finally change the lines. **Confirmed RFQ is not mandatory** — `ConvertRequisitionToOrder` already targets RFQ *or* PurchaseOrder, and the PO screen never required one.
- **40.2 Field formats everywhere.** Every field accepted any string. New `engines/field_formats.go` declares GSTIN / email / phone / PAN / IFSC / PIN code / URL, detected **by fieldname** (generalising Stage 41's `IsPhoneField` — no migration per field, covers every such field today and every one added later) and enforced at `ValidateDocument`, the choke point every document write already passes. **Nothing becomes mandatory**: empty always passes; the rule is "if you fill it in, fill it in properly". Messages state the rule *and* an example. The same declarations are served to the frontend (`GET /api/v1/meta/field-formats`) which drives placeholders, keystroke filtering (a phone field refuses letters; `+ - ( )` and spaces pass), auto-upper-casing and inline blur messages — one list, not two, so the form cannot promise a format the server rejects. Reuses the existing `gstinPattern` and keeps `MASTER-0049`/`MASTER-0051` so catalog behaviour is unchanged.
- **40.3 `HR/Admin` → `Super Admin`.** The name was inherited from the first migration and read like a job title rather than a privilege level. 37 scattered `role != "HR/Admin"` literals across nine files collapsed into one `engines.IsSuperAdmin` predicate (new `engines/roles.go`), the stored value was rewritten in `users`/`role_permissions`/`approval_rules`/`approval_log`/`field_permissions` across every tenant schema, and the role is canonicalised once in `apiMiddleware`. **The legacy name is accepted permanently** — a token minted before the rename, an external script, or a restored older snapshot all keep working, which is what makes the migration safe against a live database with sessions open. `RequiresMFA` deliberately moved off a map keyed on the literal, which would have silently dropped MFA from exactly the accounts that most need it.
- **40.4 Speed.** Stdlib `compress/gzip` response compression plus versioned-asset immutable caching, both wrapping the whole mux so the SSH-tunnel path behaves like the Caddy-proxied one instead of differently. Measured on the dev server: `app.js` 749KB → **173KB**, `styles.css` 74KB → **19KB**, the shell 36KB → **8KB** — a cold load drops from ~822KB to ~200KB. The Google Fonts `@import` was deleted: a CSS `@import` is only discovered *after* `styles.css` parses, so it cost three chained third-party round trips before first paint. Replaced by the platform UI font (SF / Segoe UI Variable / Roboto) — zero bytes, no FOUT, and it let the **CSP drop its two Google Fonts exceptions**, so the app now loads no third-party resource at all. The boot chain's four sequential `await`s (labels, doctypes, permissions, modules — none reads another's result) became one `Promise.all`, so boot pays for the slowest rather than the sum.
- **40.5 Motion.** "Cheap flick" was diagnosed as the right complaint about the wrong curve: instant view swaps, a loading bar flashing for ~80ms on fast responses, and middling easing. Views now enter on a decelerating curve (8px rise + fade, `transform`/`opacity` only, so no layout or paint), the loader is delayed past the point most responses return, presses answer inside ~110ms with a sub-perceptual 1.5% scale, modals scale from 98% rather than sliding, and long tables get `contain: paint`. All of it sits under the existing `prefers-reduced-motion` block.
- **40.6 Manual orders in the OMS.** `POST /api/v1/orders` had existed since the Order Engine was built — it is the same entry point every channel import reaches — but nothing in the UI could reach it, so a phone or walk-in order had no path at all. New **New manual order** panel posts to that same endpoint deliberately, *not* through the generic doc API, so allocation, reservation, hold evaluation and idempotency all apply (a manual order that is refused for insufficient stock is the system working, not failing). The Orders table's Channel column became **Source**, showing the originating system **and its own order id**, which is what makes "did that Unicommerce order actually reach the ERP?" answerable on screen.
- **Docs**: `USER_GUIDE.md` §6 rewritten against the real screen (its old step 3 promised item selection that did not exist), new §6.2 on inclusive/exclusive GST and place of supply, new §8.1 on field formats, new **§9A** covering manual order entry, the sync trail and the full order-to-cash walkthrough with a note mapping it onto Unicommerce's vocabulary. Role renamed across all six hand-written guides and in `cmd/gendocs` so the generated two stay right.
- **Verified**: `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./... -p 1` all clean (the historically flaky finance-GL case passed too). 17 new tests across `field_formats_test.go`, `purchase_order_pricing_test.go` and `purchase_order_test.go`. Live-checked over HTTP on a throwaway :8099 instance: the PO preview derives Karnataka→Maharashtra as inter-state IGST, Exclusive gives 4500 taxable / 4725 total where Inclusive gives 4285.71 / 4500, an unclassified item flags its own line without failing the rest, an override beats the derivation, a vendor with no GSTIN reports why; the migration left **0** rows under the old role name and 108 under `Super Admin`; gzip and cache headers confirmed with `curl`; a manual order was created and correctly refused a second one for insufficient stock. **Not live-verified**: `customer_phone` storage on a manual order (the dev DB has no stock left for the test SKU, and that half is Stage 34.4's concurrent work, not this batch's) and the browser rendering of the new PO screen/motion — both flagged in `ai_handover.md` §6.

## 87. Stage 26.6.11 — tax-exempt / nil-rated / zero-rated goods (2026-08-09, code + schema + docs)

The last substantively-buildable item in the live checklist, and the only one still gated on a product decision. Stage 30.1.2 had made `hsn_code` **and a positive `gst_rate`** mandatory on Item — correctly, since checkout and PO creation both rejected an item lacking them, so "saveable at 0%" only ever meant "unusable later". The side effect was that genuinely untaxed goods (unbranded grain, fresh produce, salt, books, exports) became unsaveable, so a tenant selling ordinary exempt stock could not create the Item at all.

The user took both decisions up front: **all four treatments** (Taxable / Exempt / Nil-Rated / Zero-Rated), and **HSN still required on all four**. The fix deliberately does *not* let a bare `0` through — `0` is indistinguishable from "not filled in yet", which is the exact hole 30.1.2 closed. The item declares its treatment, and `0` becomes valid only once that declaration exists.

- **Additive by construction.** `Item.tax_treatment` is a `Select` with `mandatory = FALSE`; blank normalizes to `Taxable`, so every existing Item and every existing CSV template validates exactly as before. The field sits at `display_order` 8, between HSN Code and GST Rate (which moved 8 → 9, with the PIM fields 9-11 → 10-12) — metadata only, no document data rewritten.
- **One resolution choke point.** New `GetItemTaxInfo`/`ItemTaxInfo` (`engines/gst.go`) is where a transaction turns an Item into tax facts; `GetItemGSTInfo` became a thin accessor onto it, so no existing call site or test changed. `NormalizeTaxTreatment` is alias-tolerant for CSV/API input but **rejects an unrecognized value rather than defaulting it** — quietly reading `Exmept` as Taxable would charge tax on goods someone was marking exempt.
- **The compliance half, which was the real work.** `GetGSTReturnSummary` reads account `4100`'s balance as the period's *taxable* value — sound only because `PostSalesGSTBooking` moves tax out of `4100` and every sale was taxable. An exempt sale moves nothing, so its turnover would have been filed as taxable: GSTR-3B 3.1(a) overstated and 3.1(c) understated by the same amount. New `PostExemptSalesReclass` moves non-taxable turnover into new accounts `4110`/`4111`/`4112`, kept as three rather than one because GSTR-1 columns nil-rated and exempt separately and GSTR-3B splits zero-rated 3.1(b) from exempt+nil 3.1(c). `GSTBreakdown` gained three `omitempty` buckets that are deliberately **not** part of `TaxableAmount`.
- **`MASTER-0044` finally wired.** The rate rejection moved off `MASTER-0042`, whose catalog headline is about HSN — the old code showed an HSN headline over a GST-rate detail. `MASTER-0044` ("Tax category is required for this item") had existed unused since Stage 25 precisely because Item had no tax-category field until now.
- **Scoping note:** Zero-Rated models the LUT/bond route only. Export-with-payment-then-refund needs a refund-claim workflow that does not exist here and was not invented.
- **Verified**: `go build`/`go vet`/`node --check` clean; new `TestExemptGoodsCanBeSold` (8 subtests) and `TestNormalizeTaxTreatment`, `TestItemTaxClassificationRequired` extended to 18; full `go test ./... -p 1` green. Live on a scratch `:8099` (migration applied via `erp-server -migrate`): `Item/meta` confirms field order 7→8→9 with the right Select options, and the GST return summary endpoint returns all four new keys. Item *create* is HR/Admin-only and `admin` is MFA-mandatory, so rather than weaken a shared dev account's MFA that seam is covered by a unit test running the same `ValidateDocument` → `ValidateMasterDataRules` pair the handler calls. Guides updated (User Guide §9 Step 3 + the error table; Admin Guide B.3 on the three new GL accounts and why they exist); `REPORT_CATALOG.md` regenerated. Full detail: `micro_checklist.md` 26.6.11.

---

## 86. Parity Master Plan — Stages 35-39 scoped against Unicommerce, Unbxd, SAP and Dynamics 365 (2026-08-09, docs, planning only)

The user named four external benchmarks and asked for one consolidated plan to reach that level, plus a knowledge center at the end. All four were read directly — the Unicommerce OMS product page and the Uniware documentation portal (sale-order model, WMS API index, client-integration structure), the Unbxd PIM help centre's full 49-section information architecture, SAP Help/S-4HANA Cloud's line-of-business scope, and the Dynamics 365 product surface incl. Business Central — then reconciled item-by-item against real code rather than against the trackers' own claims.

Output is **[docs/specs/parity_master_plan.md](specs/parity_master_plan.md)** (the detail) plus a Stage 35-39 skeleton in `micro_checklist.md` (the tracker), the same split Stage 26.12 has with `oms_master_blueprint_reference.md`. No code was written.

Three findings shaped the plan and are worth carrying forward:

- **The existing backlog is gated, not unbuilt.** Every open item in Stages 20/23/26/31/34 waits on credentials, a vendor engagement, real business users, a public hostname, a legal decision or a physical printer. So "the next level" is genuinely new scope, and the plan says so rather than pretending the backlog is the roadmap.
- **Three deliberate earlier deferrals are now the critical path.** Channel orders still create `POSCart` instead of `SalesOrder` (26.12.1's explicit carve-out), there is no OMS console at all (26.12.1 again), and order→shipment→invoice is unwired for `SalesOrder` (26.12.3's carve-out). The whole OMS engine layer therefore sits *beside* the real order flow rather than under it. Release train R1 exists to clear exactly this, and nothing else in Stage 35 compounds until it does.
- **SAP parity was assessed and explicitly rejected as a goal.** 60+ modules and decades of statutory localisation is not something this codebase should chase, and chasing it would violate CLAUDE.md's first principle. Business Central is the ERP benchmark the plan actually aims at; SAP is read as a concept checklist (multi-currency, intercompany, landed cost, statement builder, QM/PM), which is where the real Stage 37 gaps came from. §9 of the plan records that decision alongside seven other things deliberately out of scope — a scripting runtime for PIM transforms, a search engine, a frontend framework, a data mart, a mobile app, an AI app store, HTML-parsing scrapers — so none of them gets re-litigated later.

Sizing: ≈118 items, ≈61-79 sessions (~3-4 months at this repo's demonstrated cadence), across 7 dependency-ordered release trains. Eight open product decisions (D1-D8) are listed with a stated default each, specifically so none of them blocks R1 from starting.

The knowledge center (Stage 39) is designed to the `documentation.unicommerce.com`/`help.pim.unbxd.com` bar under this repo's own constraints: Markdown in `docs/kb/` rendered by a new `cmd/genkb` into static `public/help/` pages with a prebuilt client-side search index — no static-site generator, no bundler, no Lunr/Algolia, and the `%TEMP%`-then-copy write path that Controlled Folder Access forces on every generator in this tree. Three of its sections (error codes, report catalog, permission matrix) are already machine-generated by `cmd/gendocs`; four more (API reference, doctype/field reference, settings reference, module matrix) become generable once Stage 38's OpenAPI emitter exists, which is why Platform is sequenced ahead of it.

---

## 85. Stage 34.1-34.3 built, the first production DR drill, and nightly backups finally switched on (2026-08-07, code + schema + ops + docs)

The user asked to clear everything actionable. That was two things: Stage 34's buildable half (34.1-34.3), and 26.11.3's restore drill. Both landed; both turned up something the checklist did not know. The drill's finding — that production had no nightly backup — was then signed off and fixed the same day (26.11.7). Item detail in `micro_checklist.md` (34.1-34.3, 26.11.3, 26.11.7).

- **34.2's premise was wrong, and the honest implementation was the interesting decision.** The item said "join `CompetitorPrice` to our `Item`/price list". **There is no price list.** `Item` has no price field of any kind, and the POS cashier types the sale price by hand into an editable input that starts at 0. So "our price" is resolved at report time as the last price we *actually transacted at*, newest wins across `SalesOrderLine.unit_price` and `POSCart items[].sale_price`. The alternative — inventing a price master — is a real feature with real product decisions behind it (per channel? per customer group? effective dates?) and had no business being slipped in under a competitor-pricing item. The cost is stated rather than hidden: a SKU never sold reports `Position = "No price on file"` with null gap columns, not a fabricated zero that would rank top of the gap list.
- **Registering `CompetitorPrice` as a Master doctype rather than a Transaction was what made it cost zero frontend code.** The Setup flyout and the generic doctype table both filter on `document_type = 'Master'`, so Master is what inherits the list, the New form and the Bulk Import button. A Transaction doctype would have been unreachable from the UI entirely — the exact trap `CycleCountLine` hit and had to be worked around with bespoke JavaScript. The rows are timestamped observations, so "Master" is a mild semantic stretch; the UI consequence decided it.
- **Following the documented import path literally exposed a bug in 89 doctypes.** `GenerateCSVTemplate` omits `id`/`status` from the header it emits, and `ValidateDocument` skips the mandatory check for those same two system fields — but `missingMandatoryColumns` did neither, so **any** doctype with a mandatory `status` rejected its own generated template with "missing mandatory column(s): status". Brand, Color, Customer, Vendor, Location, Employee, `CycleCountLine` — 89 in total. The tempting fix was to make `CompetitorPrice.status` optional and move on; the right one was the shared choke point, which repaired all 89 at once. This only surfaced because the verification downloaded the template and uploaded it, instead of hand-writing a CSV that happened to include `status`.
- **The undercut worker's de-duplication is the part that makes it usable rather than noise.** An hourly worker that alerts whenever a competitor is cheaper re-alerts on the same standing undercut every hour forever. State lives in `public.competitor_undercut_state.last_run_at` (the `patch_intake_state` shape), so only observations *recorded since the last cycle* can alert — one observation, at most one alert. The threshold setting defaults to **0 = disabled**, so a tenant that never loads competitor prices is never alerted about them.
- **The DR drill passed, but only after three blockers that each would otherwise have been met mid-incident.** The script **had never been deployed** (`deploy.ps1` ships the binary and `public/`, not `deploy/`). Its own documented `sudo -u erp` invocation **can never work**, because production's `erp` role correctly has no CREATEDB and the script derived its admin connection from `DATABASE_URL` — fixed with an optional `DRILL_ADMIN_URL` override rather than by granting the app role CREATEDB, which would weaken the running system in order to test a backup. And the superuser **cannot read `/opt/erp/backups`** (`drwxr-x--- erp:erp`), so the newest backup had to be staged elsewhere — including rewriting the `.sha256` sidecar's absolute path, because `sha256sum -c` resolves the path recorded *inside* the file and otherwise fails a perfectly good copy with a misleading "corrupted".
- **The drill's most important output was not the drill.** It passed — checksum verified, `pg_restore` clean, all three sampled tables matching live exactly. But the box had **no nightly backup at all**: two manual-looking files taken around deploys, an empty crontab, no systemd timer, `install_backup_cron.sh` never run. A backup that restores is only useful if a recent one exists, and `hypercare_plan.md`'s "two consecutive failed nightly backups" rollback trigger cannot fire when there is no nightly backup to fail. Left for explicit sign-off rather than installed, since a read-only drill is not licence to change production's configuration — **sign-off came the same day, and the cron is now live** (below).
- **The same env-file trap from §85's own deploy notes had a second victim, and it was the backup cron.** The line `deploy/backup.sh` had carried in its header since day one — `0 2 * * * /bin/bash -c 'source /etc/erp/erp.env && backup.sh'` — **can never work**, for exactly the reason `deploy.ps1`/`migrate.sh` hit earlier: `/etc/erp/erp.env` is a systemd `EnvironmentFile` (bare `KEY=value`, no `export`), so `source` sets `DATABASE_URL` as a shell variable and `backup.sh`, a child process, never sees it. Fixed to `set -a; . file; set +a` in `install_backup_cron.sh`, `backup.sh`'s header, `deploy/README.md` (which was still instructing a hand-paste of the broken line, and whose quick-reference `migrate`/`backup` rows had the same defect) and `backup_restore.md`. Worth naming as a class rather than a bug: **`source` is not `export`, and every consumer of this env file that spawns a child has to know that.** The one-line fix is cheap; finding it three times is not.
- **It was found only because the installer runs a real backup instead of trusting the crontab.** That step-5 immediate run is the difference between discovering this now and discovering it during a restore, since the failure mode is a silent non-zero exit at 02:00 into a log nobody reads. A second bug surfaced the same way: run as root from `/root` (0700), step 5 drops to `erp`, which cannot traverse that cwd, so the retention `find` exited non-zero and failed the whole run under `set -e` **even though the dump had succeeded** — a working install that looked broken. Fixed with `cd /`.
- **"The cron line exists" was still not accepted as proof.** `erp`'s shell is `/usr/sbin/nologin` and cron's `PATH` is `/usr/bin:/bin`, both narrower than the `sudo` environment the manual verification inherits — so a manual run proves `backup.sh` works, not that *cron* can run it. A temporary `* * * * *` entry pointed at a scratch `BACKUP_DIR` was left to fire on its own and did (`CRON[131799]: (erp) CMD ...`), producing a valid backup, then removed. The newest backup was finally decrypted with the box's own `BACKUP_ENCRYPTION_KEY` and piped to `pg_restore --list` (valid archive, `dbname: custom_erp`, 286 TOC entries) — the only check that proves the key on the box actually opens what the box writes, which a checksum does not.
- **Still not solved, and stated rather than buried: nothing alerts when a backup fails.** The job appends to `/var/log/erp-backup.log`; hypercare's rollback trigger still depends on a human reading it each morning. An *empty* log is a distinct failure from an *error* in it — one line per successful night means no new line proves the job never ran. That wiring belongs to 20.2's webhook. Off-box copies remain deliberately absent for want of storage credentials, so losing the droplet still loses the backups.
- **Production runs PostgreSQL 14.23; dev runs 16.3.** Found while inspecting the droplet, and it retroactively validated a choice made hours earlier for portability reasons: the price-gap query deliberately avoids PG16-only `pg_input_is_valid` in favour of a `LIKE '[%'` guard. Had it used the tidier function, 34.2 would have built and tested clean on dev and failed on production.

---

## 84. Production formalisation, content assist, and go-live readiness docs (2026-08-06, code + schema + ops + docs)

The user asked to start building the whole blocked backlog, decisions first. Four decisions were taken up front (hosting: formalise the droplet; content assist: local/offline only; P2 bundles: two greenlit; credentials: all parked) and three more in a second round (no domain yet, deploy 33.2 + commit the loose docs, draft all three go-live docs). Item detail in `micro_checklist.md` (26.1.1, 26.1.3, 26.4.11, 26.10.6, 26.11.1/3/5/6, 33.2).

- **The hosting "decision" turned out to be a formalisation gap, not a decision gap.** The droplet had been serving production for weeks with systemd, loopback binding, ufw and encrypted backups — but three things had never been done, and each was found by inspecting the box rather than trusting the checklist. **`ENV=production` was never set**, so the seed-admin hard-stop, the non-UTF8 hard-stop and the removal of `/api/v1/debug/panic` were all silently off. Both hard-stop preconditions were verified *before* flipping it — a WIN1252 database or an unrotated seed password would have refused to boot, and the dev cluster genuinely is WIN1252 (20.6), so this was a real risk, not a formality. **Caddy was serving the stock Debian welcome page** with `reverse_proxy` commented out — running, publicly reachable, and doing nothing useful.
- **The most important thing here is what was deliberately *not* built.** Making Caddy proxy to the app is one line and would have looked like closing 26.1.1. With no domain there is no certificate, so that line publishes every password, bearer token and tenant row in cleartext — strictly worse than the SSH-tunnel posture it replaces. Refused, and the reasoning written *into* `deploy/Caddyfile.holding` so the next session doesn't helpfully "fix" it. `enable_tls.sh` does the whole switch in one command when a domain exists, and checks DNS resolves to the box first because a premature ACME run burns Let's Encrypt's weekly failed-authorization quota and can lock out the real attempt for days.
- **Two security headers were missing app-wide and were added at the app's choke point, not the proxy.** `Referrer-Policy` and `Permissions-Policy` were absent from `securityHeaders` (which already set HSTS/CSP/X-Frame-Options/nosniff). Attaching them to Caddy would have been easier and wrong: the tunnel path never touches Caddy and is currently the *only* access path. Caddy-layer rate limiting was rejected outright — it needs an `xcaddy` rebuild and a custom binary to keep patched, for something Stage 13.14 already does in-app.
- **Content assist (26.4.11) answers its governance questions by construction rather than by policy.** The local/offline decision means there is no provider and no network call, so "what data leaves the server" is answered by there being no egress. Human-in-the-loop is the interesting one: rather than a rule saying the AI must not auto-publish, `GenerateContentSuggestion` simply *returns* a draft and has no code path that writes `ProductContent` — with a test asserting zero rows are created, so a future refactor that helpfully persists the draft fails the build instead of quietly removing the guarantee. The honest trade is stated in the code and the checklist: deterministic templates can't write persuasive copy, but they also can't hallucinate a product feature, which is why this shape is defensible without a review model behind it.
- **26.10.6 was measured instead of built, and that is the outcome the item asked for.** Its greenlight condition was "measure first", and 26.10.7 had already built the measurement mechanism. The live database holds **no transactional data at all** — `gl_postings`, `POSCart` and `SalesInvoice` are empty; the largest tenant table is metadata. A read replica would have added a replication topology and a staleness window to a system whose reports return instantly. Reported back rather than built, with concrete revisit thresholds recorded so the condition stays checkable rather than re-litigated.
- **`go_live_decisions.md` was actively misleading and was corrected.** Its §12 described all five P2 bundles as un-started; **four had been built on 2026-07-27** and were `[x]` in the tracker. That staleness had already produced one wrong decision this session (26.7.8 was greenlit when `engines/crm_analytics.go` already implemented it). A decision worksheet that lags the tracker is worse than none, so §4/§5/§11/§12 and the summary table now carry per-item status.
- **The three go-live docs are drafted as far as an AI session honestly can take them.** None can *close* — they need a vendor, real business users, and the user's sign-off respectively — so each records its own blockers rather than implying readiness. The hypercare plan names 20.1/20.2 as hard preconditions, on the grounds that response targets are fiction if an out-of-hours P0 reaches nobody. The pen-test scope ranks priorities by consequence (tenant isolation first — schema-per-tenant means one missed qualification is a full breach) and lists known-accepted items so engagement budget isn't spent rediscovering documented decisions. The UAT run sheet is deliberately a process wrapper around the existing checklist rather than a second copy of its cases, organised by business scenario because the screen-by-screen walk proves screens function, not that the system supports how the business works.

## 83. Archive reconciliation + Stage 34 market-intelligence plan (2026-08-06, docs)

A planning pass, no code. Cross-referenced `docs/archive/micro_checklist_closed_stages.md` against the live tracker and turned §81's retained knowledge into a buildable Stage. Item detail in `micro_checklist.md`'s new header block, 26.6.11 and Stage 34.

- **The archive is clean by the obvious test and needed only one correction.** All 234 archived items are `[x]`; nothing was archived while still open. Two entries read as unfinished in their own text and were re-verified against live source rather than trusted: archived **25.9** (`FIN-0260` "still not wired") is **stale** — 26.6.6 wired it into `rejectIfCurrentPeriodClosed` and 26.6.10 records that, with `TestBackdatedPostingApproval` asserting the real `ValidationError`. Left as written; the archive is history, not a live claim.
- **One genuine orphan, now brought forward as 26.6.11.** Archived 30.1.2 deferred tax-exempt/nil-rated goods as a "follow-up worth a decision" and no live item was ever created — while `engines/master_data_validation.go:87` still tells the reader it is "tracked in docs/micro_checklist.md Stage 30", which has since moved to the archive. The gap is real: 30.1.2 made a **positive** `gst_rate` mandatory, so a 0% item cannot be created at all, and exempt/nil-rated goods are ordinary in Indian retail. Recorded with the reason it must not be fixed by letting a bare `0` through — `0` is indistinguishable from "not filled in yet", which is precisely the hole 30.1.2 closed.
- **The live tracker's own header was stale** and now names the right stages: it claimed to carry Stage 30 (archived) and omitted 31/32/33.
- **Stage 34 is planning only, and says so in its first line.** The shape is forced by one fact: `go.mod` has two dependencies and no vendor directory, so **there is no HTML parser** — DOM scraping means a new dependency and realistically a new runtime. That splits the Stage cleanly. 34.1-34.3 are buildable now with zero new deps (`CompetitorPrice` doctype fed by the existing `BulkImportCSV`, a price-gap `ReportDefinition`, an undercut alert on the existing `Start…Worker(ctx, interval)` shape used by 10 workers already). 34.4 is feasible *only* because §81's headline finding removes the browser requirement — Myntra's embedded `window.__myx` JSON blob is reachable with `net/http` + `regexp` + `encoding/json`. 34.5 (DOM scraping) is recorded with a **recommendation not to build it**.
- **34.6 is the gate and it is not an engineering question.** Marketplace ToS generally prohibit automated collection, and this is multi-tenant SaaS — harvesting on tenants' behalf is a different risk posture than the single-merchant use the retired project served. 34.4/34.5 stay blocked until the user answers scope, `robots.txt`, rate ceilings and retention. 34.1-34.3 deliberately do **not** depend on that answer, since a buyer pasting a competitor-price export from a seller panel is a legitimate source with no ToS question attached.

## 82. Clearing the decision-gated backlog: MFA recovery, backups, supplier portal, smoothness (2026-08-05, code + schema + docs)

Review of open item 32.4 concluded it was a real bug already fixed by `0bee663` and already deployed — so the only genuinely unblocked item was closed before this session started. The user then approved, in one pass, every remaining item that was gated purely on a *decision* rather than on something only they could supply (credentials, hosting choice, a pen-test engagement, real UAT users, a physical printer). Five items, built and verified in sequence. Full per-item mechanics in `micro_checklist.md` (32.2, 32.3, 32.5, 26.1.6, 26.4.10).

- **32.5 — MFA recovery (the lockout hole).** Single-use recovery codes (10 × 50 bits, SHA-256 hashed, never stored raw — high-entropy codes need no bcrypt and a fast hash makes redemption one indexed lookup instead of a loop), accepted at `/auth/mfa/verify` in place of a TOTP code, burnt by a single conditional `UPDATE` so concurrent logins cannot double-spend one. Plus the two adjacent gaps the item named, without which codes alone still leave a user stranded with a dead authenticator: **re-enrollment** (new secret parks in `users.mfa_pending_secret` so the *existing* device keeps working until the new one is confirmed — an abandoned attempt cannot itself cause the lockout) and **admin reset** (`POST /api/v1/admin/users/reset-mfa`, which clears enrollment and codes and forces re-enrollment at next login rather than disabling MFA). Re-auth on both self-service flows is by password, not TOTP, deliberately: the person who needs those screens is the one whose authenticator is already gone. Display-once UI with copy/download and an acknowledgement gate; a recovery-code login raises a toast naming the remaining count.
- **26.4.10 — Supplier portal, as a limited-role login.** A second portal app would have meant a second auth system, session story, deployment and set of security rules, for what is substantively a form an outside party fills in and an internal reviewer approves. New `SupplierSubmission` doctype mirroring `ProductContent`'s fields; QC via the existing maker-checker engine. **The real work is row-level scoping**, because doctype-level RBAC alone would let every supplier read every other supplier's submissions — enforced at `handleGenericDoc`, the one choke point every read and write already passes through, in all three of list, single-read and write. On create the supplier code is *overwritten* from the session (never picked by the payload); on update the *stored* code is checked, since the payload is attacker-controlled. Approval produces `ProductContent` as **Draft**, never Approved — otherwise an outside party's text could reach a live sales channel with no internal publish decision.
- **26.1.6 — Tenant-scoped export.** Extends the existing backup engine (`deploy/backup.sh --tenant`, `manage.ps1 export-tenant`) rather than adding a second one, so encryption, checksum sidecars and retention cannot drift apart. On-demand only: the nightly whole-DB backup already contains every tenant. Guards the schema name against existing as well as matching a pattern, because `pg_dump --schema` does not fail on a typo — it silently produces an empty dump.
- **32.3 — Backup cron.** The gap was never the backup script; it was that the script documented its cron line *in a header comment* nobody ran, which is how the droplet reached 2026-08-04 with an empty `/opt/erp/backups`. `deploy/install_backup_cron.sh` (idempotent via a marker comment, preflights every failure mode the box actually hit, and **runs one backup immediately** — a cron line existing is not evidence a backup works) plus `deploy/restore_drill.sh`, the Linux counterpart of a drill that previously existed only on the Windows dev machine. 14-day on-box retention per the user's decision. **Not run on production** — the user chose to deploy themselves, so production has no backups until they run it.
- **32.2 — Smoothness.** The "~20 remaining `transition: all` sites" estimate was stale; 11 remained, each read against the rule it pairs with before being changed, all found to animate only feedback properties and moved to explicit lists at `--transition-fast`. But the likelier cause of the user's original "I have to click many times" was found elsewhere: `renderView()` cleared the view root and *then* awaited, so a slow screen showed a blank white panel with no sign the click had registered. Split into a feedback-owning wrapper plus the untouched dispatch, with the loading placeholder removed in a `finally` so a throwing renderer cannot strand a permanent "Loading…".

**Verified**: `go build`/`go vet`/`node --check` clean; **`go test ./... -p 1` fully green** (`db`, `engines`, `internal/server` all ok — the documented finance-GL shared-DB flake did not reproduce). Six new handler-level tests against the real dev DB drive the real handler chains rather than hand-inserted fixtures: four for MFA (recovery-code accepted, single-use enforced, unissued code rejected, remaining codes still work, formatting/case tolerated, old authenticator survives mid-re-enrollment then stops after, admin reset forces re-enrollment and destroys codes) and two for the supplier portal (cross-supplier read/list/overwrite all blocked, approval yields Draft content). Both migrations applied to the real dev DB. Two incidental finds worth carrying: `handleMFAActivate`'s new `recovery_codes` array broke two existing tests decoding into `map[string]string` (fixed — caught by running the suite, not by reading); and the 5-req/min login rate limiter makes several MFA tests in a row fail on 429 unless each test is given its own source IP, since `httptest.NewRequest` hands every request the same `RemoteAddr`.

**Left undone, deliberately**: everything still blocked on the user supplying something — Track A (20.1-20.5), connector/GSP/e-way-bill/terminal credentials, the hosting decision (26.1.1, and 26.1.3/26.11.3 behind it), the pen test, business UAT, hypercare, the QZ live-printer check (31.1.8), 26.10.6 (whose own gating condition is "measure first"), and 26.4.11 (explicitly excluded until governance scope is defined). Nothing here was deployed: 33.2 and the frontend work in 32.2/32.5 remain undeployed at the user's instruction.

## 81. Retiring the OmniCore/"Buying Catalog" project, keeping its market-intelligence knowledge (2026-08-05, docs)

A standalone Python microservices stack (`Antigravity Projects/Buying Catalog/OmniCore` — FastAPI + Streamlit + SQLAlchemy/SQLite + Redis/RQ + docker-compose, services `crawler`/`pims`/`oms`/`erp`/`pos`, last touched 2025-12-27) was read in full, its knowledge extracted to `docs/specs/market_intelligence_reference.md`, and the folder deleted. Same disposition and the same reasoning as the standalone WMS project in §(1c1c050)/`docs/specs/wms_master_blueprint_reference.md`.

- **No code adopted, and the reason was not just the language.** Four of its five services duplicated shipped functionality in worse form — its `pims` against Phase 26.4 + `pim_media.go`, its `oms` stock reservation against `orders.go` (which does real row-locking where OmniCore `print()`ed), its `pos` against the POS that now one-click prints, its `erp` a 138-line FastAPI toy. Its "Amazon SP-API / Flipkart Seller API" poller was a `random.random() < 0.1` simulation with no API knowledge in it at all. Adopting the one non-duplicate part would have meant Python + Playwright + BeautifulSoup + TextBlob + Redis, against `CLAUDE.md`'s first principle.
- **The one genuinely net-new concept is competitor/market price intelligence.** `engines/marketplace.go` covers courier serviceability, labels/manifests, delivery/RTO and settlement reconciliation — not catalog or competitor-price harvesting. Not backlogged; the reference file is there for if it is ever asked for.
- **What was kept** is in the reference file: Myntra ships its search results as an embedded `window.__myx` JSON blob (parse JSON, not DOM — immune to CSS churn), the layered-fallback selector strategies for Amazon/Flipkart/Meesho/eBay, and the two-phase *harvest to disk in parallel, then parse offline* architecture whose skip-if-file-exists check buys resumability and a cache in one line. Plus the QA-gate-on-AI-output pattern, which is the part most likely to matter here if this repo ever adds AI extraction: the model's answer is stored with a `QA_Status` and validated before use, never trusted as fact.
- **It also did not work end-to-end**, which the reference file records so nobody resurrects it blind: an undefined `keep_raw` NameError at the end of every successful crawl, a six-argument call into a five-parameter `run_crawl`, a dead Flipkart parse branch, and two competing job systems (RQ worker *and* FastAPI BackgroundTasks).
- **Its GitHub remote no longer exists** (`Repository not found`) and 6 of its 7 commits were never pushed, so the reference file is now the only surviving record. Deletion was confirmed with the user first on exactly that basis; 66.6 MB freed, all but ~600 KB of it raw harvested HTML page dumps rather than source.
