## 80. Stage 31.1.9 — One-click print wired into the last three screens (2026-08-05, code)

Stage 31.1 built the QZ Tray bridge and wired stickers plus the marketplace-document file picker; 31.1.9 was the open remainder — a Print Label button on the logistics booking, the POS receipt over ESC-POS, and invoice printing. Item-by-item mechanics in micro_checklist.md 31.1.9.

- **Two new job types, no new route and no migration.** `Receipt` and `Invoice` are cases in the existing single `/api/v1/print/qz/payload` endpoint — the shape 31.1 chose deliberately so a new printable document costs one case rather than a route, handler and client method. `print_role` already offered both names (the 31.1 migration seeded `Shipping Label,Invoice,Sticker,Receipt,General`), so a tenant only has to set **Default For** on a Printer record. The role fallback in `resolveQZPrinter` is what makes these one-click: the operator configures each bench once and then never picks a printer again.
- **Both documents are rebuilt server-side from what was stored, never taken from the browser.** `BuildReceiptPayload` re-reads the `POSCart`, `BuildInvoicePayload` the `SalesInvoice`. That is what makes a reprint honest — a receipt printed an hour later shows what was actually rung up — and it closes the obvious hole where a tampered client prints a receipt for a bill that never existed. The reader is a small dedicated one rather than a call into `PostSalesInvoice`/`FinalizePOSCheckout`, because those live inside write paths (`FOR UPDATE`, "only a Draft may be posted") that printing has no business running.
- **A receipt prints only for a `Paid` cart; an invoice prints at any status but says so.** Pending Approval is money not yet collected and Failed is a sale that did not happen, so those are refused with `GLOBAL-0019` rather than printed with a caveat — a receipt is what a customer walks out with. An invoice is the opposite case: a Draft is a legitimate proforma to hand someone, so it prints, stamped `DRAFT` in both the ESC-POS and HTML renderings so it can never pass for a posted one.
- **Only ESC-POS gets raw commands, and that is narrower than the existing `isRawLanguage` on purpose.** ZPL and TSPL are label languages with no continuous feed; a receipt emitted as raw commands to a ZPL unit comes out blank. Everything else — PDF, HTML, a mis-set ZPL printer, unset — is rasterised as HTML through the OS driver, so there is no configuration that silently prints nothing.
- **Wiring the receipt path surfaced two real defects in code that was already shipped.** `printPOSReceipt` never deducted `offer_discount`, so any sale carrying a Stage 30.7 offer printed a total **higher than the cash actually collected** (checkout returns `amount_due = sale_total − loyalty_discount − offer_discount`). And `#receipt-print-area` had no on-screen `display: none` — only the `@media print` block ever hid it — so a printed receipt stayed rendered at the bottom of the live page until the next navigation. Both fixed here; the ESC-POS receipt additionally names the stored `applied_offers`, which is exactly what the comment on `storedPayload["applied_offers"]` had asked for since 30.7.
- **`qzTryPrint` learned to fail quietly, because the new call sites resolve a printer by role.** "No Printer record is Default For Receipt" is the *normal* state of a tenant that has not set QZ up at all — without this a cashier would get a `DEVICE-0298` modal on every single sale before the browser sheet appeared. `quiet` suppresses the payload-preparation and unmapped-printer dialogs (both `console.debug`'d instead) and falls through to the existing print path. A failure at the tray itself stays loud in every case: that one means printing was really attempted and really failed.
- **Verified against a running server, not just compiled.** Build/vet/`node --check` clean and `go test ./... -p 1` fully green, with 5 new Go tests pinning the column arithmetic, the amount-survives-clipping rule, the ESC-POS-vs-label-language choice, the Paid-only guard and the DRAFT stamp. Then driven live on a scratch :8261 as `manager1`: all three job types role-resolved to the right printer and produced correct payloads — receipt 500.00 subtotal → **450.00 due** after a 50 offer, shipping label with a real `^BCN` Code 128 of the AWB, invoice marked DRAFT — plus every error path. Finally **18/18 Playwright checks** against the real `index.html`+`app.js` with QZ Tray absent, so every assertion exercised the fallback rather than the happy path. The Stage 31.1 migration turned out to be the one item still pending on this dev DB; it applied cleanly, clearing the nested `$f$` dollar-quoting risk 31.1.8 flags locally as well as on prod.
- **Still open in Stage 31**: 31.1.8, live verification against a real QZ Tray and a real printer. Nothing here has printed on hardware.

---

## 79. Stage 33 — Dialog viewport fit & responsive form layout (2026-08-04, code)

User report with a screenshot of the New Location dialog, its header cut off at the top of the frame and no Save button anywhere: *"Nothing is visible here. So, set up. Make it more 2 column or 3 if required so it will be visible. All screen and config it should auto adjust with 100% to any percent 40% or whatever. If 120% also it should get auto adjusted."* Item-by-item mechanics in micro_checklist.md Stage 33.

- **The multi-column request was the fix the user could see; the actual defect was that no dialog in the app could scroll.** `.modal-container` had no `max-height` and no scroll region at all, and `body` is `overflow: hidden`. A form taller than the viewport was therefore centred by the overlay's flexbox and clipped off **both** ends — the classic flex-centring overflow — so the first fields *and* the footer holding Save/Cancel were unreachable, with nothing on the page able to scroll to them. That is why "nothing is visible": not a column-count problem but an unbounded box. Fixed structurally — the container is a flex column bounded by `calc(100dvh - 40px)`, header and footer are `flex: 0 0 auto`, and `.modal-body` takes the overflow with `flex: 1 1 auto; min-height: 0; overflow-y: auto`. `min-height: 0` is the load-bearing declaration; without it a flex child will not shrink below its content and the container overflows again exactly as before.
- **The `<form>` wrapper broke the flex chain, and only measurement caught it.** With the container fixed, the harness still failed every case at or below 1280x720. In `index.html` most dialogs wrap body+footer in a `<form>`, so the **form** is the container's flex child rather than `.modal-body` — it stayed a content-sized block, the body never received leftover height to scroll in, and the footer was clipped as before. Written as `.modal-container > form` so the handful of JS-built modals that put `.modal-body` directly in the container are untouched. Worth recording because the container fix *looks* complete when read, and reading is what would have shipped it.
- **The generic document form is now an intrinsic grid rather than a breakpoint list.** `openDynamicModal()` serves every master and document create/edit screen and has an unbounded field count — Item renders ~20. `#dynamic-modal-body` became `grid-template-columns: repeat(auto-fit, minmax(min(240px, 100%), 1fr))`: no media queries anywhere, which is precisely what answers "40%, 100%, 120%, whatever" in one rule — browser zoom only changes the CSS-pixel viewport, and both the grid and the `dvh` bound are recomputed from it, so there is no zoom level with special handling and none to forget. The `min(240px, 100%)` inner bound is what stops a single column from overflowing a heavily-zoomed narrow viewport.
- **Kept surgical in both directions.** The grid is scoped to `#dynamic-modal-body`, so the table and summary modals that share `.modal-body` keep their existing single-column block layout; and the wide container is opt-in above 4 fields, so a 3-field master still renders in today's 520px dialog (where the 240px grid minimum yields one column on its own) instead of two-thirds of an empty row. JS learned exactly one new fact — the field count — plus a `.form-group-wide` marker so the JSONTable/JSONMap line editors from 30.5.3 span the full row rather than being squeezed into a 240px track. Column count itself stays entirely in the stylesheet.
- **The same bug lived in the second dialog system and was fixed with it.** `.custom-dialog-box` (`showCustomAlert`/`showCustomConfirm`/`showApiError`/`showCustomPrompt`) had the identical structure and the identical unbounded height, so a long `showApiError` detail list clipped its own OK button off-screen. Verified with a 40-row error list.
- **Verified in Chromium at eight viewport sizes, and the before/after is the point.** One harness drives the real `styles.css` against the DOM `openDynamicModal` builds, across viewports standing in for **zoom 40% → 300%** plus a deliberately short 1400x380 window: 3 columns down to 1097px, 2 at 768px, 1 at 520px, container inside the viewport and **Save reachable in every case**, body scrolling exactly when it needs to. Against `HEAD` the 120%/140%/200%/300%/short cases all put Save *below* the viewport bottom — `save=[720,757]` in a 720px-tall window — with `scrolls=false`, which is the user's screenshot reproduced as a number. A second harness serves the **real `index.html`+`app.js`** over HTTP and confirms the new rules leaked into nothing: `add-field`, `edit-prefix`, `add-label` and `bulk-import` still measure `cols=1 display=flex w=520`. (Geometry was asserted via `getBoundingClientRect`, not eyeballed from screenshots — with no backend the login screen sits at `z-index: 500` over the dialogs, so the screenshots of the real page show the login card while the measurements underneath remain valid.)
- **Not deployed.** Per 32.1.8 the user's `localhost:8080` is the droplet, so none of this reaches them until the tree ships; frontend-only, no migration or rebuild needed. Tracked as micro_checklist.md 33.2, awaiting their go-ahead.

---

## 78. Stage 32.1 — Interaction responsiveness: the sidebar hover defect (2026-08-04, code)

User report after using the deployed app: *"The interface is not at all smooth. I have to click many times. Then it is working. Hover is also not working. I have to click on arrow then it is showing permanently else it is going away."* Item-by-item mechanics in micro_checklist.md Stage 32.1.

- **One line explains the entire hover complaint.** The sidebar's flyout logic had accumulated a careful-looking delay ladder — a switch dwell, a longer "aim grace" for the diagonal reach, a hide grace, a hover bridge across the 8px gap — and the test that chose between the two dwells was `flyoutPointerTrail.dx > 0 && r.left >= flyoutPointerTrail.x`. The panel always sits to the right of the sidebar, so the second half is true for essentially **every** pointer position in the nav, and the decision collapsed to "is the pointer moving right at all" — sampled frame-to-frame, which at 120Hz is a sub-pixel delta made mostly of hand jitter. Hovering a module while another menu was open therefore waited **500ms on roughly half of all approaches, chosen at random by noise**.
- **The reported symptom follows from that mechanically, and is worth stating because it is what makes the bug feel like the app ignoring you.** Nothing happens for half a second, so you move toward where the menu should be. That leaves the row — which cancels the pending open *and* schedules the hide. No menu ever appears. Clicking the arrow calls `show()` directly and bypasses the whole ladder, so clicking becomes the only thing that reliably works: precisely "I have to click on arrow then it is showing permanently else it is going away."
- **Replaced with a real trajectory test rather than a longer delay.** Velocity is now sampled over a 90ms window (not one frame), a deliberate reach must cover 12px horizontally, and the pointer's projected crossing point must land inside the panel's **vertical** span before any grace applies. Because the test finally distinguishes "cutting the corner towards the open menu" from "moving down the sidebar", the blanket dwell that was compensating for it could drop from 140ms to 60ms and the grace from 500ms to 220ms. The delay ladder got shorter *and* the corner problem it existed for is better handled.
- **A pending open now re-evaluates instead of running to term.** The delay was picked once at `pointerenter`, and the `pointermove` handler was guarded by `!flyoutOpenTimer` — so once a timer was armed, every subsequent move was a no-op and a pointer that had stopped aiming still served the full grace. Dwell is measured from a `hoverStart` stamp and recomputed on each move, so the wait only ever counts down. Timer cancellation also gained an owner, so leaving row B can no longer cancel an open that row C scheduled.
- **The second half of "not at all smooth" was the transition timings.** Every hover state in the sheet ran on `transition: all var(--transition-speed)` — 250ms, the duration intended for sidebar-width and panel slides. Interaction feedback has to land inside the ~100ms that reads as instant; at 250ms the UI visibly lags the cursor. Added `--transition-fast`/`--transition-med`/`--ease-out` and pointed the hot paths at them, with named properties instead of `all` (which was also animating padding and border-width on `.menu-item.active`). Separately, the sheet had **no `:active` state anywhere** — a button that looks unchanged the instant it is pressed reads as "didn't register", which is the direct cause of "I have to click many times", and on a submit the extra click is a duplicate request rather than a cosmetic problem.
- **Verified live in Chromium against the real files, not by reasoning.** The harness serves `public/` statically and seeds an `erp_token`; `setupModuleFlyouts()` runs synchronously at the top of `init()` before the first `await`, so the nav binds fully even though every API call 404s without a backend. 12 assertions, 5 consecutive clean runs. **The same suite was then run against `HEAD`** to prove the diagnosis: a jittery approach over 6 runs went **`[2, 81, 3, 445, 70, 477]`ms before → `[4, 1, 4, 2, 7, 4]`ms after**, and "hover a module, wait a beat, then reach for the menu" produced **no menu at all before → the menu after**. Worth recording one methodological point: a straight synthetic pointer path down the sidebar has `dx == 0` and so **never trips the old bug** — the first version of the harness passed against the broken code. Reproducing it required simulating a hand that wobbles, which is exactly the input the old test could not cope with.
- **The most important finding of the session was not a code defect at all: the user was never running this code.** After 32.1 shipped, the report persisted — "hover in POS is not showing continuously, in localhost:8080". `localhost:8080` is held by `Open-ERP.cmd`, which runs `ssh -N -L 8080:127.0.0.1:8080 root@139.59.17.16`: **the app under test is a DigitalOcean droplet**, and it was still serving a **Stage-28-era build from 2026-07-27** — `app.js` 589KB against the tree's 658KB, with `menu-flyout-bridge` absent entirely. The tell was in the screenshot before any code was read: a **Dashboard** entry that Stage 30.5.11 deleted, and "Point of Sale"/"Accounting" for what the tree renamed to "POS"/"Financial Accounting" on 2026-07-26. Generalised rule, now in `ai_handover.md` §6: **verify what is actually deployed before debugging a UI report** — the deployed build can be weeks behind, and every minute spent reading correct code is wasted.
- **The "AllocationRule needs two clicks" report was the pre-28.5 defect, and the user's own screen recording contained the proof.** No ffmpeg on the machine, so frames were pulled by loading the mp4 in Edge via Playwright (the bundled Chromium lacks H.264) and screenshotting the `<video>` at intervals. The frame at 15.25s shows **Stock's flyout open under the pointer while the page and the highlighted row are both HR** — a menu belonging to one module while the app sits on another. That is exactly the shape of the bug Stage 28.5 fixed: `hideTimer` was per-container but `closeSubmenus()` is global, so module A's already-armed 200ms timer fires *after* module B's menu has opened and closes it; the menu disappears from under the pointer and the click lands on the page behind. Setup is the worst case — longest list, anchored lowest, so `maxHeight = availableBelow` forces internal scrolling and a long pointer journey. Replaying that gesture mechanically: **deployed build 0/6 first clicks registered, local tree 6/6.**
- **Deployed to production the same day**, on the user's explicit instruction to ship the whole tree including the unverified QZ work after the risk was put to them. Backups first, because the box had **none** — no `erp` crontab and an empty `/opt/erp/backups`, so a droplet carrying real data had never been backed up. Both of Stage 31.1's documented risks then cleared against real Postgres 14: the nested `$f$` dollar-quoting **parsed and executed** (dry-run inside a rolled-back transaction rather than trusting it), and `Stores` had 0 rows so 30.5.5's data move was a no-op. 14 migrations applied, service restarted clean, and all four frontend files verified **byte-identical by SHA-256** to the ones that had tested 6/6 — the honest way to carry a local test result onto a server whose login is not ours to guess.
- **Using the deploy kit found a bug in it.** `deploy/deploy.ps1` did a plain `source /etc/erp/erp.env`; that file is a systemd `EnvironmentFile` (bare `KEY=value`, no `export`), so `DATABASE_URL` never reached `migrate.sh` as a child process and it died on its own guard. Now `set -a; source ...; set +a`. Two adjacent traps were recorded rather than re-learned: since 30.2.2 the migrations are **embedded in the binary**, so the new binary must be in place before migrating (against the old one it just tries to start a second server); and a hand-rolled psql pending-check inside an `ssh` here-string gets its quoting mangled and reports every migration as pending, base schema included — which, had it been trusted, would have re-run the base schema against live data.
- **Not claimed**: the user's broader "I have to click many times" was not traced to a specific second cause beyond the feedback timings above. The ~20 remaining `transition: all var(--transition-speed)` sites were left alone rather than swept blind — see micro_checklist.md 32.2, which is open pending the user pointing at the screens that still feel wrong.
- **32.4's console errors, diagnosed a day later (2026-08-05) as a stale-deploy artifact rather than a live bug.** The same invalid-token drive that produced the 32.1.8/32.1.9 findings above also threw `Cannot set properties of null (setting 'textContent')` ~10x. Root cause: `renderExecDashboard` wrote into `#exec-dashboard-cards` after its `Promise.all` resolved past a view change — introduced with the dashboard itself in `ef0e39b` (Stage 26.10, 2026-07-26), already fixed by a null-check in `0bee663` (2026-08-02). The box under test was still on the Stage-28-era build from 2026-07-27 — after the bug, before the fix — so the errors were real but already stale by the time they were seen; `32.1.10`'s deploy that same day carried the fix to production regardless. A live Playwright repro against `HEAD` (invalid token, full boot sequence, then all 45 views) found zero `pageerror`s — only the 10 browser-native 401 network-log lines that likely produced the original "~10x" count.

---

## 77. Stage 31.1 — QZ Tray silent printing (2026-08-03, code + schema + docs)

User request, framed from marketplace operations: *"For Myntra and other Marketplaces OMS, I want to crack QZ print automation"*, with Myntra support's own setup mail attached, and the clarification that it should be **one-click print for all** — from the ERP as well as for the marketplace documents the channels supply. Three scope questions were put to the user first (what it drives, how QZ is authorised per PC, and what the label hardware is); the answers were one-click across both, self-signed/fully silent, and support all printer types. Item-by-item mechanics in micro_checklist.md Stage 31.1.

- **The whole feature rests on one protocol detail, and it was verified from QZ's source rather than assumed.** QZ Tray rejects a wrongly-signed call *silently* — it just falls back to prompting on every label, which reads as "QZ is flaky" rather than as a bug. The trap is that `qz-tray.js` hashes the request and hands the **hash** to the backend to sign, so the obvious backend implementations (sign the JSON; sign the raw digest bytes) both look right and both fail. `qz/auth/Certificate.java:363` settles it: `verifier.update(getBytesUtf8(sha256Hex(data)))` under `SHA512withRSA` — the signed bytes are the UTF-8 bytes of the **lowercase hex string**, over the JSON `{"call","params","timestamp"}` in that key order (`PrintSocketClient.java:218`). Both facts are pinned by tests, including a negative test asserting that signing the raw digest bytes does *not* validate.
- **No new dependency, per the repo's first principle.** The official client is ~150KB and carries its own promise shim, its own SHA-256 and remote-relay logic this deployment does not use; what was actually needed is a WebSocket, a hash and a signature, so `public/qz-print.js` is ~330 lines of vanilla JS. The one non-obvious consequence: `crypto.subtle` does not exist outside a secure context, and a packing bench reached at `http://192.168.x.x` is exactly that case — so a pure-JS SHA-256 fallback is mandatory, not defensive. It was checked against Node's `crypto` on 8 vectors plus 300 random-length fuzz cases, specifically across the 55/56/64-byte padding boundaries where a hand-written SHA-256 goes wrong.
- **Reused the `Printer` Master instead of adding a registry.** The doctype already existed (code/name/location/status, module key `stickers`, already validated by `engines/stickers.go`'s DEVICE-0298 check), so Stage 31.1 adds six fields to it — `qz_printer_name`, `print_role`, `printer_language`, label width/height, DPI — and the whole configuration screen comes free from the generic Master machinery. `print_role` is what makes printing one-click: the server resolves role → printer, so no operator ever picks one. The routes share the existing `stickers` module key for the same reason Stage 30.7 flagged — a new key 403s every existing tenant until their plan is edited.
- **Marketplace labels are passed through byte-for-byte, never re-rendered.** A channel-issued PDF goes to the printer exactly as supplied, because re-rendering a courier's label risks altering a barcode they have to scan. Physical sizing is applied as driver options (4x6 → 101.6 × 152.4mm) rather than by touching the document. ERP-native shipping labels *are* generated, and gained something the old path could not do: `engines/marketplace.go`'s `GenerateShippingLabel` explicitly returned plain text ("not a real symbology/PDF"), so on a thermal printer the AWB now goes out as a genuine Code 128 symbol.
- **The signing endpoint is deliberately not a blind oracle.** QZ's own reference backends sign whatever arrives, which lets any authenticated caller get arbitrary content signed by the server key. This one accepts exactly one SHA-256 hex digest and nothing else, so it can only ever emit signatures over hash-shaped values. It also means only the 64-char digest leaves the browser — a 5MB marketplace PDF is hashed locally and never re-uploaded merely to be signed.
- **Degrades instead of breaking.** If QZ Tray is not installed or not running, `qzTryPrint` returns false and every caller falls back to the existing `@media print` sheet. Non-thermal sticker printers take the same route by design, signalled by the server as `fallback: "browser"`. Nobody is ever blocked from printing because the bridge is down.
- **Verified**: `go build ./...`, `go vet ./...`, `node --check` clean; 7 new Go tests green (signature validates under the reproduced `Certificate.java` algorithm; raw-digest signing rejected; non-hash input refused; ZPL control-character escaping; payload shapes). The client was then run end-to-end against a **mock QZ Tray** implementing that same verification with the real generated key material — 15 assertions covering handshake order (unsigned `getVersion` → certificate → signed calls), message shape, the exact signed string and its key order, print params, and a negative control proving a tampered signature is rejected. **Not verified live against a real printer or a real QZ Tray**, and the DB-backed endpoints were not exercised — no PostgreSQL or Docker is available on this machine (see micro_checklist.md 31.1 for exactly what that leaves open).

---

## 76. Stage 30.5.5 — Retiring `Stores`, folding its fields into `Location` (2026-08-02, code + schema + docs)

The last open item in Stage 30, and the only one that was blocked on a product decision rather than on work. `Stores` was promoted in the sidebar and documented as "your shop/warehouse locations", but it had **zero Link references and zero Go references** — nothing in the system could select one. A user following the manual created their shop somewhere no transaction could see it.

Three options were put to the user; they chose to retire it and fold its fields in. The deciding fact was that the four fields only `Stores` had — address, city, contact_phone, manager — were **write-only by construction**. There was nowhere else in the product to record a shop's address or phone at all, so the cheaper "keep it, just hide it" option would have left a real gap sitting behind a removed menu entry.

**The migration's ordering is the whole risk.** Both `documents` and `doctype_fields` declare `REFERENCES doctype_meta(name) ON DELETE CASCADE`, so removing the `Stores` doctype row before its documents had moved would silently cascade-delete every one of them. The file therefore adds the columns, moves the data, and only then removes the doctype — with the removal guarded on there being nothing left to lose, so a partial migration leaves the doctype standing rather than taking the data with it.

The move is an `UPDATE`, not an `INSERT` + `DELETE`: `documents.id` is the primary key and is unique across *all* doctypes, so a Stores row's id cannot collide with a Location's, no id remapping is possible or needed, and re-pointing `doctype` in place preserves the row's provenance. The four fieldnames were kept byte-identical so not one key inside a stored `data` blob had to be rewritten.

**The dev DB held zero Stores documents**, so applying the migration there proved only the empty path. The move was therefore exercised separately against a real Postgres — a throwaway program built a scratch schema carrying the same CASCADE declarations, seeded it, ran the real migration file and asserted the result: 15/15, including idempotence and the guard correctly refusing to cascade-delete an unmoved document. Plus 8 live browser checks and a re-run of the 30.5.8 suite (26/26).

USER_SOP §21 was **repurposed** from "Stores" to "Locations" rather than deleted, so section numbering and the module index stay stable, and it carries a note for anyone working from an older printout. The UAT check was inverted: it now asserts the screen's *absence*, so an older build fails it instead of quietly passing. **Stage 30 closes with this.**

## 75. Stage 30.5.8 — The picker consistency sweep (2026-08-02, code)

The audit item read as four unrelated chores: three screens missing a typeahead their siblings had, a 103-entry Location list with no structure, Employee picked with a `<select>` on five screens and a typeahead on a sixth, and icon-only buttons with no accessible name. They shared one cause — **there was no single place to be consistent in**. All 42 picker call sites configured `attachTypeahead` themselves, so "how a Location is chosen" was a decision made fifteen separate times, which is precisely how three screens came to be missing it without anyone noticing.

So the fix is structural before it is cosmetic. Every bespoke call site now goes through one door, `attachLinkTypeahead()`, which merges per-doctype defaults from a single `TYPEAHEAD_DOCTYPE_OPTS` table and delegates to the unchanged engine. The generic record form's Link field and a JSONTable's link column go through the same door, so a Location Link on any of the 53 Setup doctypes is now grouped without that screen having been edited — and any future picker improvement lands everywhere at once. The table is presentation only; a doctype absent from it takes the plain default, which is the safe direction to fail.

Two opt-in options carry it: `groupBy` (Location groups by `type`, with **first-appearance group order rather than a hardcoded `Store,Warehouse,HO` list**, so a tenant adding a fourth type gets it grouped for free) and `showAllOnFocus` (browse, not just search). The non-obvious constraint is that `highlight()`/`pick()` index into `items` — so grouping reorders that array itself, not merely the DOM, and headings are deliberately not `.typeahead-item`; otherwise arrow-keying past a heading would silently select the wrong record.

**Employee settled on the typeahead**, the control ~35 other pickers already use. `showAllOnFocus` preserves the one thing the `<select>` did better, and 30.5.1's empty-state guidance actually improves — a static hint becomes a focus-triggered row linking to where an Employee is created. The stored value is byte-identical and validation is untouched (Link fields are refused server-side with META-0198 regardless of control). It also deleted a real cost: the entire Employee list was being fetched on every HR tab render just to build `<option>`s, including for the eight tabs that never showed a picker.

The accessibility half went wider than the item's wording. `title` alone is the weakest accessible-name fallback and several screen readers skip it, so aria-labels were added to the seven `renderDocTable` row actions, the Approval Rules pair, and the five `index.html` modal-close buttons — and, most consequentially, to `copyableCell()`, one helper rendering ~30 copy chips per record list that each announced only "Copy, button". Fixed once at the helper, it names the value.

**Verified**: 6 unit tests over the grouping helpers (run against the shipped source, not a copy) and 26 live browser checks against a real Store Manager session, covering multi-type grouping, keyboard traversal across heading boundaries, unregressed type-to-search, all five converted employee pickers, and every icon-only button on a record list. `go vet`/`go build` clean. One incidental bug was caught by the run's uncaught-error assertion and fixed: `renderExecDashboard` dereferenced a possibly-detached container, throwing on every login where the user navigated away before Reports — now `DEFAULT_VIEW` — finished loading.

## 74. Stage 30.5.11 — Retiring the Dashboard landing screen (2026-08-01, code + docs)

User's call, made looking at the screen: *"Dashboard not required. Everything it is showing from config."* It was accurate — the screen held four derived stat cards (registered record types, audit-log count, active tenant, a hardcoded "Operational" pill) over nine shortcut tiles into **Database Schema Design, Dynamic Labels, Prefix Configs, Extension Hooks, Activity Log, Configuration, System Status, Tenant Entitlements** and **Tenant Usage**, every one of which the Settings module already lists. It owned no business data; it was a second front door to configuration.

Removed whole: the sidebar entry, `renderDashboard()`, its router branch and click handler, its `STATIC_VIEW_MENU_IDS` row, and the `.dashboard-grid`/`.dashboard-card`/`.card-content` rules. `.dashboard-stats-row` is a different class that Finance, the Reports exec dashboard, System Status and Tenant Usage all still use, and stays.

**The landing view is one new `DEFAULT_VIEW` constant, set to `reports`** rather than a literal repeated at each fallback. Reports is the only defensible universal choice: it is the single destination with neither a role gate (`MENU_PERMISSION_MAP` marks it `open`) nor an entitlement gate (no `MENU_MODULE_MAP` entry), so it renders for every role in every tenant — and its first tab is Stage 26.10.3's executive dashboard, which answers the same "what is going on" question with live figures instead of configuration links.

**The one way this could have shipped broken** was invisible to any fresh-profile test: `restoreLastView()` replays a saved view name, and every browser that had ever used the app carries `{"view":"dashboard"}` in `erp_nav_state`. Without a guard, existing users — and *only* existing users — would have restored into a router branch that no longer exists, i.e. a permanently blank screen. A saved `dashboard` now falls through to `DEFAULT_VIEW`.

Docs corrected in the same pass, including §73's own screenshot-derived count: the sidebar has **eleven** top-level entries now, three of them flyout-less. `USER_SOP.md` §2 was rewritten from "Dashboard" to "Where you land after signing in"; `UAT_CHECKLIST.md` §2 now asserts the screen's *absence*, so an older build fails the check rather than quietly passing.

**Verified**: `node --check` clean; live in a real headless browser as `cashier1` (the most restricted role) against a throwaway server on :8231 — 6/6 checks, covering the fresh-profile landing, the stale-`dashboard` reload, the absence of the menu entry, and a clean console in both. The exec dashboard rendered correctly for that role, falling back to an order-count trend under column masking. Screenshotted. Server stopped and port released afterwards.

## 73. Stage 30.3/30.4/30.5 + the 29.7/29.8 strays — the manual overhaul and the UX sweep (2026-08-01, code + docs)

User request: finish the whole remaining Stage 30 manual/UX backlog in one pass, plus three items the previous session had flagged as *open but mis-filed inside the closed-stage archive*. Three genuine product decisions were taken with the user up front so the rest could run end to end without stopping: Trial Balance scoping (**mandatory as-of date**), the screenshot workflow (**scripted Playwright captures**), and the two flagged status transitions (**allow both, reason-code required**). Full item-by-item detail in **[micro_checklist.md](micro_checklist.md)** Stage 29.7/29.8 follow-ups and Stage 30.

**The three strays (29.7.4, 29.7.5, 29.8.5).** All three were sitting unresolved inside `docs/archive/micro_checklist_closed_stages.md`, which by convention only holds fully-closed stages — so they were invisible to anyone reading the live tracker, and had been since 2026-07-29. Moved into `micro_checklist.md`, closed, with the archive rows rewritten to point back. **29.7.4**: `GetTrialBalance` now takes a mandatory as-of date, spelled sargably so the Stage 29.7.1 index can actually serve it; the period-close checklist passes the period's own end date, which is also the more correct question to ask. **29.7.5**: closed as a deliberate no-change — a second index on `(created_at)` would tax every posting write to speed up an async job nobody waits on. **29.8.5**: six `StatusTransitionRule` rows let an approved Leave be revoked and a selected VendorQuote be unselected, each demanding a reason code; no schema change and no new mechanism, exactly as the original note predicted.

**30.3 — the manual's false claims.** Eight items, and the pass found the problem was worse than catalogued in two places. ADMIN_SOP Part D listed nine capabilities as "backend only, no screen"; re-driving every row found **all nine** were false, not eight — Vendor Invoice override-pay has had a UI path since Stage 26.3.5. And USER_SOP §28's eight phantom Setup lists turned out to be **real dead markup**: `public/index.html` carried eleven hardcoded placeholder `<li>`s that `renderSidebarSubmenu()` wipes on first render, and the SOP had plainly been written from the file rather than from the app. The markup is now deleted as well as the prose, so it cannot mislead a third time. UAT_CHECKLIST §24 — which told testers there was "genuinely nothing to click" for eight shipped screens, and would have passed a release without them — became twelve real test sections. Corrections are stated as corrections rather than silently overwritten, because anyone working from a cached copy needs to know the old text was wrong.

**30.4 — coverage.** 13 new USER_SOP sections and 6 new ADMIN_SOP sections cover every previously-undocumented screen, written from each screen's real controls rather than from its name. Three reference appendices are **generated, never hand-written** (`cmd/gendocs`, Go, stdlib only): `ERROR_CODES.md` (302 codes), `REPORT_CATALOG.md` (46 reports across 12 categories), `PERMISSION_MATRIX.md` (106 record types × 3 roles). That choice is the whole point — a hand-copied list of 300 codes is stale the day someone adds one, which is precisely the drift 30.3 had just spent a pass undoing. USER_GUIDE gained an applies-to stamp, a task-phrased "How do I…?" index, an error-reading section keyed to real codes, and **§13, a ten-step worked example** from empty system to money in the till whose ninth step is a table proving stock, the sales register, the trial balance and the vendor ledger all moved without being told.

**30.5 — the UX sweep.** Attached at shared choke points wherever possible rather than swept per screen: empty-picker guidance goes into the generic form's Link field and `attachTypeahead` (two sites, covering every picker on 53 doctypes); the highest-leverage empty state is `renderDocTable`'s, which serves all 50+ generic lists and could not previously distinguish "nothing here" from "your search matched nothing". Two new fieldtypes, `JSONTable` and `JSONMap`, replace hand-typed JSON on six fields with an add-line editor **driven by a column spec stored in `doctype_fields.options`** — so adding one to a new field is a migration row and no code — and bring format validation to fields that previously had none at all. The Setup menu became 14 module groups with a filter box and a collapsed Advanced divider, driven by an additive `doctype_meta.setup_advanced` column. `PurchaseOrder`'s `vendor`/`vendor_id` pair is now mirrored server-side at the same choke point that numbers documents, deliberately a mirror rather than a rename. And `/api/v1/me/permissions` finally reports create/update/delete alongside read, so the app can hide affordances a role cannot use instead of refusing at Save.

**Two bugs this pass found in its own work.** The Setup-menu rewrite broke `applySidebarPermissions()` — it counts `.menu-flyout > li` to decide whether a flyout is empty, and the new filter row and group headings are also `<li>`, so a role with no Master access would have kept seeing an empty Setup menu. And the Advanced toggle is a `.submenu-item` for styling, so the generic click-rebinding loop would also have fired on it and navigated to a doctype named `null`. Both caught before shipping; both are one-selector fixes documented at the site.

**What the screenshots caught.** 30.4.7's captures were meant to be documentation, but the first sidebar shot immediately disproved something two earlier reads of the markup had got wrong: the sidebar has **twelve** top-level entries, not eight module groups — `Reports`, `Manufacturing` and `PIM` are `:not(.has-flyout)` and so are invisible to any grep for flyout children. The audit had it wrong, item 30.3.3 had it wrong, and the first draft of the correction had it wrong. Fixed in USER_GUIDE and UAT_CHECKLIST. Playwright is resolved from wherever it already exists on the machine and is deliberately **not** a project dependency — a documentation tool must not be the thing that introduces a build step into a repo that has none.

**Verified**: `go build ./...`, `go vet ./...`, `node --check` clean; full `go test ./... -p 1` green across db/engines/internal-server, with 24 new subtests. Four migrations applied to the dev DB. Live over real HTTP on a throwaway port (:8152) against the dev DB: the trial-balance as-of bound, the Leave revocation refusal-then-success with the stored status checked at each step, the PO mirror storing both keys, and JSONTable validation rejecting both malformed JSON and a missing required cell by line number. **19/19 checks in a real headless browser** across an admin and a Store Manager session — the grouped/filterable Setup menu, the line editor serialising four typed cells to the exact shape the engine reads, the empty-picker affordance, and the permission gating hiding buttons for `Item` while still showing them for `Offer`. All live test data, the scratch token helper and the throwaway server were removed afterwards.

**Still open in Stage 30, deliberately**: 30.5.5 (`Stores` vs `Location` — the *documentation* half was a false claim and is fixed, but retiring or folding the doctype needs the user's call) and 30.5.8 (the consistency sweep, partly closed — the icon-only row buttons gained `aria-label`s via 30.5.7).

## 72. Stage 30.8 — The project brain map (2026-08-01, tooling + docs)

User request: a graphify-driven diagram of the entire project, in its own folder under `docs/`, that "may behave like a brain", is appended to as the system grows, and is easy to update. Built as `docs/brain/` — `BRAIN.md` (7 Mermaid diagrams + a card per region), `brain.html` (interactive, self-contained), `brain.map.json` (the only hand-edited file) — generated by a new stdlib-only `cmd/brainmap`. Item-by-item mechanics in micro_checklist.md Stage 30.8.

- **The design decision that makes it maintainable is the three-input split.** The map says which regions exist; the *working tree* says which files exist; the *graph* says what calls what. Only the first is hand-written, so the picture cannot drift away from the code the way a hand-drawn architecture diagram does — and because the second input is the tree rather than the graph, a new file that no region claims is named in the run output and in BRAIN.md §6 instead of being silently absent. "We will append if new is added" is therefore enforced by the tool, not by anyone remembering. Most new files need no edit at all: `engines/wms_cycle_count.go` is claimed by WMS's existing `engines/wms*.go` the moment it exists.
- **Measuring coverage against the tree rather than the graph turned out to matter more than expected.** graphify parses 246 of 345 files; it has no extractor for `.sql`, JSON, PowerShell or Markdown, and `tree_sitter_sql` is not installed, so **all 71 migration files contribute zero nodes**. Had coverage been measured against the graph it would have read 100% while a third of the repo — including the entire schema — was invisible. Persistence & Migrations now correctly shows 71 files against 15 symbols, and BRAIN.md says plainly why a region can be substantial and still look sparse.
- **Two rounds of the diagram being accurate but useless, both fixed by admitting what the graph cannot see.** First: the Cortex floated unconnected, because the SPA is JavaScript and the server is Go and nothing links them statically — so 8 `declared_links` now state those boundaries by hand, drawn as thick `==>` arrows, each carrying a note on why it cannot be extracted. Second: **99% of cross-region relationships are `INFERRED`** (graphify resolves calls within a file exactly and across files by name), so the original "dot the edge when mostly inferred" rule dotted every single edge and encoded nothing. Now dotted means *entirely* inferred and solid means at least one relationship was parsed from source, with the 99% figure stated outright in the legend. The map's shape is reliable in aggregate; any individual edge is a lead to grep, which is what `CLAUDE.md` already says about `[INFERRED]`.
- **A third diagram exists because the honest one was a hairball.** Every region talks to Persistence and the Error Catalog — true, and the point of them, but it means those two nodes dominate and hide everything else. §2b redraws the same map with the three universal hubs removed, which is the only view where module-to-module wiring (OMS→Inventory, PIM→Connectors, Manufacturing→WMS) is legible.
- **Verification was done by rendering, not by reading.** All 7 Mermaid blocks were parsed with the real mermaid@11 parser in a headless browser, and `brain.html` was rendered in headless Chromium in both light and dark. That caught two defects a read-through would not have: Go's nil slices serialise as `null` and broke every `.length` read on page load, and the lobe-band backgrounds were painting over the connection lines. Lobe colours were validated with the dataviz palette validator rather than picked by eye.
- **One Windows-specific finding worth carrying forward**, since it will bite anything else in this repo that writes files from a freshly built binary: **Controlled Folder Access is enabled on this machine**, so Defender refuses writes under `Documents\` from unrecognised executables — and reports the refusal as `ERROR_FILE_NOT_FOUND`, i.e. Go says "the system cannot find the file specified" about a directory that demonstrably exists. Isolated by testing writes inside and outside `Documents\` and from PowerShell versus Go. `update-brain.ps1` works around it (generate into `%TEMP%`, copy in with PowerShell, which Windows trusts); the generator detects the case and names the cause rather than leaving the next person chasing a phantom missing directory.

---

## 71. Stage 30.7 — Config sweep ("nothing hardcoded") + POS offer engine (2026-08-01, code)

Two requests the user framed as separate and which were built as such: every module's operational config surfaced on the Configuration page and *properly wired* ("if I change it should immediately change everywhere"), and offer configuration for POS, set up in the ERP and reflected at the till. Two scope questions were put to the user first — which offer families to support (answer: all four) and what to do about platform safety limits (answer: expose them, but do it properly) — and the build followed those answers. Item-by-item mechanics in micro_checklist.md Stage 30.7.

- **The framework was already right; it was simply barely populated.** Stage 28.1 had built exactly the correct thing — declare a setting once (module/label/type/default), read it via `GetSetting*` at the point of use, and a fully generic Configuration screen renders it with no frontend work. The gap was that only **7 settings** had ever been registered while ~30 operational constants were still literals in Go. This Stage took it to **36 across 12 modules**. Because every existing consumer already read per-use and `SetSetting` invalidates the cache on write, the user's "changes everywhere immediately" requirement needed no new plumbing at all — it needed the constants to actually be settings. That property was then demonstrated live rather than asserted: changing the list-page cap moved a live endpoint from 3 rows to 2 in the same process, no restart.
- **Two things had to be added to the framework before the sweep could be honest.** Tolerances and money thresholds (2%, ₹50, ₹21,000) had no representable type, so a float type was added — with `Min`/`Max` unified to `*float64` so one bound pair serves both numeric types rather than growing a parallel set of fields. And `tokenTTL()` already honored a `JWT_EXPIRY_HOURS` env var, which meant a naive setting would have rendered an editable control that a deployment silently overrode — a dead knob, worse than the constant. `SettingIsOverridden` distinguishes "an admin actually set this" from "this equals the default", making the precedence explicit: admin edit → env var → default.
- **Platform limits were exposed with teeth, not left in code.** Asked whether page-size caps, per-tenant concurrency and batch sizes should be editable, the user said do it properly but don't hardcode it. Each is now a setting carrying a registry `Min`/`Max` the validator enforces at save time, so a mistyped page cap is rejected with a specific message instead of exhausting memory. Verified live: `999999` against a max of `10000` was refused.
- **One of the user's own examples turned out not to exist, and another turned out to exist but be unreachable.** PO/GRN "edit days" had no time-based rule anywhere — only amendment-reason and post-receipt checks — so that is new enforcement, attached generically at `ValidateTransactionalRules`' existing choke point (a doctype → setting-key map) rather than bolted onto two doctypes by hand, and defaulted to 0/no-limit so no existing tenant changes behaviour. Conversely the Pine Labs and Unicommerce base URLs were *already* per-tenant DB config with working endpoints — they simply had **no screen at all**, so they were surfaced as an Integrations section on the Configuration page. Deliberately not copied into the settings registry: two sources of truth for one URL is precisely the drift this Stage exists to remove.
- **The offer engine is one doctype, four families, evaluated server-side.** `Offer` is a flat-schema Master, so create/list/edit come free from the generic doctype machinery and the only new engine code is the evaluator. `offer_type` selects among Percentage Off / Flat Off / Buy X Get Y / Bundle Price — the same shape Campaign's `trigger_type` already established — with thresholds, coupon codes, customer tiers, validity windows, caps, priority and stacking as orthogonal conditions. Two rules are deliberate and tested: cheapest units go free in BOGO while the *most expensive* are bundled first (so adding an item can never raise the bill), and the running total is floored at the bill so no stack can drive a sale negative.
- **The POS preview never sets the price.** The screen calls a preview endpoint so the cashier can see what applies, but `handleCheckout` re-runs the same evaluator against the tenant's own `Offer` rows. A tampered client cannot invent a discount, and an offline cart replayed later is priced against the rules as they stand when it lands. What was applied is persisted onto the cart for the receipt and audit trail, for the same reason the GST breakdown already is.
- **The live run earned its keep.** Testing over real HTTP (which required completing a full MFA/TOTP enrollment to get an HR/Admin session) caught a bug no unit test would have: the new doctype had been given a fresh `pos` module key, but POSCart and POSSession are mapped to `sales`, and the module gate 403s any doctype whose key no plan grants — every Offer request would have failed with SAAS-0191 on every existing tenant. Separately, a `TestWMSP2` failure was briefly and wrongly attributed to this Stage's WMS change; re-running showed it flaky under shared-DB fixture state and green 3/3 with the change in place, and the full suite is clean.
- **Verified**: `go build`/`go vet`/`node --check` clean; full `go test ./... -p 1` green; 21 new tests across two files. Live: 36 settings over the wire, immediate propagation demonstrated end-to-end, guardrails and float validation enforced, and an ERP-created offer applying at the POS on the next call with thresholds, coupon gating, priority and unmatched-code reporting all correct. All live fixtures and the scratch tooling were removed afterward.

For the current build tracker, see **[docs/micro_checklist.md](micro_checklist.md)**.

## 70. Stage 30.6 — Server-issued document numbers (2026-08-01, code)

User request, made from the Purchase Orders screen: *"PO number will be auto created. I will create po sequence. Same of GRN and all other transaction."* Two scope questions were put to the user before building (how wide, and what shape the numbers take); both were answered, and the work followed those answers. Item-by-item mechanics in micro_checklist.md Stage 30.6.

- **The numbering engine already existed; nothing was calling it.** `engines.GenerateSequence` — a row-locked `sequence_counters` table plus an admin-editable `prefix_configs` series, with a Prefix Configurations screen already shipped — has been in the tree since Stage 1 and already numbered Purchase Requisitions, Payslips, CSV imports and RFQ/PO-on-conversion. The 13 bespoke create screens simply never used it and asked the user to type a number instead. So this was a wiring job at one choke point, not a new subsystem: a doctype → series registry (`engines/document_numbering.go`) invoked from `handleGenericDoc`'s POST branch, right beside the Purchase Requisition call that already did exactly this, and ahead of `ValidateDocument` because the fields it fills are mandatory.
- **What was actually broken was silent data loss, not typing effort.** The typed number was sent as the document `id`, and the documents table upserts on `ON CONFLICT (id)`. Two makers who picked the same PO number did not get an error — the second save overwrote the first, invisibly. Live-reproduced before the fix and now pinned by a test that submits the same form twice and asserts two distinct documents.
- **Two traps found by reading the schema rather than assuming it.** `PurchaseOrder` has *two* mandatory number fields (`po_number` and `code`) from two overlapping historical registrations, so both must be filled or the save fails validation. `ASN`'s `po_number` field, despite the name, holds the *referenced PO's* number — numbering it would have detached every ASN from its purchase order. The registry is per-field for exactly this reason, and both cases are regression-tested.
- **`reset_frequency` was dead config for the entire life of the project.** It was read and then never used; the counter bucket was always whatever financial year the caller passed. Making it real meant deriving the visible period segment and the counter bucket *together* — a series that resets on a period it does not display would re-issue last year's numbers, and since the number is the id, that is a rejected save rather than a cosmetic wart. Same reasoning governs the new `include_store` toggle: turning the store segment off also collapses the per-store counters, or two stores would be handed the same visible number.
- **The generic record form is driven by the server, not a second list.** Rather than copy the doctype registry into JavaScript, `GetDocTypeMeta` now stamps an `auto_generated` flag onto the field metadata the form already fetches, computed from the same registry that assigns the number. Adding a doctype to the registry updates the generic form for free.
- **Scope held deliberately.** An explicit `id` in a request body is still honored as an upsert, because that is this API's existing contract (`ProductContent`'s composite-id save and Stage 29.8's transition test both rely on it) and numbering it would convert updates into duplicate documents. No create screen sends an id any longer, so the user-facing guarantee stands; only a deliberate API caller can still choose one. Engine-created doctypes (orders, shipments, returns, settlements) were left alone.
- **Verified**: `go build`/`go vet`/`node --check`/full `go test ./... -p 1` clean; 20 new tests across two files. Live over real HTTP on a throwaway port against the dev DB — two identical PO submissions returned sequential distinct numbers, reshaping a series to `TRF`/`-`/pad-4/NEVER/no-store took effect on the very next save, and the `auto_generated` flags were confirmed correct per doctype over the wire. All live fixtures, the temporary series edit and the scratch `cmd/` tooling were reverted afterward.

For the current build tracker, see **[docs/micro_checklist.md](micro_checklist.md)**.

## 69. Stage 30.1/30.2 — the layman audit's P0 block, closed (2026-07-31, code)

User asked to start building open checklist items, explicitly not phase by phase. Took the whole P0 block of Stage 30 (the 2026-07-30 layman audit, `docs/UX_MANUAL_AUDIT.md`) — seven items — plus two backend items from 30.5 that fell out of the same work. Item-by-item mechanics in micro_checklist.md Stage 30.

- **The flagship flow was blocked by one lookup.** `GetItemGSTInfo` matched an Item's internal `id` only, so the Code and Barcode a cashier actually holds both failed checkout with "item not found" — and the POS typeahead fills in the Code, making it certain rather than occasional. Fixed at a new shared `ResolveItemBySKU` (`code` → `barcode` → `id`, one query, deterministic priority) rather than at the one call site, which turned up two more places with the same defect in mirror image: channel-order SKU mapping matched only `code` (a barcode-sending channel was held as unmapped), and sticker printing matched only `id` (a code-keyed print run produced blank name/HSN on every label). Ordering matters and is tested: when one item's Code equals another's Barcode, the Code wins, or a collision quietly sells the wrong product at the wrong tax rate.
- **Master and transaction layers disagreed about tax.** HSN/GST were optional on Item but hard-rejected at both checkout and PO creation, so the app let a layman create a product that was silently unsellable *and* unpurchasable. Both are now mandatory in metadata (so the form marks them and `ValidateDocument` enforces them) and the master-data rule was tightened to demand exactly what the transaction layer has always demanded. Noted rather than guessed at: this makes a 0%/exempt item unsaveable, which matches the fact that checkout never could sell one — real exempt-goods support needs an explicit flag.
- **A GRN could close a PO while posting zero stock.** `location` was read by the stock-posting hook but was never a declared GRN field, so only the bespoke Workbench screen could supply it; every other route saved with HTTP 200 and moved no stock. Declared it (mandatory), defaulted it from the PO's warehouse before validation, and made a failed ledger post reverse the receipt to `Cancelled` — which is exactly the status the PO's received-quantity query already excludes — instead of a `log.Printf` under a 200.
- **Migration drift had no owner.** Five integration tables existed only in the create-from-scratch base schema, so no existing database ever got them. The deeper problem was three separate reimplementations of "apply pending migrations" (promote.ps1, deploy/migrate.sh, CI), all lexicographically ordered, none reachable from a normal workflow. Replaced by one runner compiled into the binary with the SQL embedded (`erp-server -migrate`), numeric-aware ordering, a transaction per file, the ledger table that already existed since Stage 14.6, and a startup warning naming the oldest unapplied file. The two scripts now delegate to it. It proved itself the same session: it caught a real unapplied migration another session had left in the tree.
- **Errors threw away the useful half.** The envelope sent only the catalog's generic headline, discarding both the engine's specific reason and the catalog's own `UserAction` — populated on all 302 rows and never once sent to a client. That is why a missing HSN read "contact administrator" to an administrator. Added `detail` + `user_action` at the response writer, so every error on every path improved at once; 5xx internals stay withheld exactly as before.
- **Loyalty points could be destroyed without buying anything.** "Redeem Points" burned the ledger entry on click and then told the cashier to type the discount into a line's price by hand. Points now ride on the cart as an intent, are burned by the server only when the sale actually completes, are reversed by a compensating entry if anything downstream fails, and are applied to the total automatically. GL splits the tender — revenue still credited in full (so GST needs no re-interpretation), cash for what was collected, and a new account for the points portion.

**Verified**: `go build`/`go vet` clean; six new test files, all green; a full serialized suite run. Live-verified on a throwaway server against the real dev database, replaying the audit's own reproductions: an HSN-less Item is now rejected naming the field, all three SKU identifiers resolve identically, a GRN with no `location` took stock 0 → 10, a sale completed by item Code, a 210 sale redeeming 50 points returned `amount_due: 160` with a balanced split posting, and both NULL-scan endpoints returned 200 where they had returned 503 and 500. All fixtures, the scratch token tool and the scratch server removed afterward.

**Two things worth carrying forward.** A near miss: the loyalty GL account was first written as `5300`, which is already *Loss on Asset Disposal* — the migration's `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` stopped the overwrite, but every redemption would have posted to asset disposal. List `gl_accounts` before claiming a code. And the whole test suite was unrunnable at session start: 33 test files hardcode port 5435 and the portable Postgres had been started on Postgres's own 5432 default, which `db.InitDB`'s `log.Fatalf` turns into a whole-package abort. The handover doc's 5435 was right and the audit's note was backwards; Postgres was restarted on 5435, and the connection string is now overridable via `TEST_DATABASE_URL` so a port mismatch can't cost a session again.

## 68. Stage 29.9 — Final SaaS module/menu naming pass (2026-07-29, code + docs)

User asked, ahead of a SaaS relaunch, whether reusing standard ERP nomenclature carries trademark/replication risk — confirmed no (generic functional terms like "Sales Order"/"Chart of Accounts" are industry-standard, not vendor IP; this repo's own Stage 21.11 ERPNext-style renames are the same category of naming). User then picked final names from a SAP/Microsoft/Oracle/NetSuite/Infor/Odoo/ERPNext comparison and asked for them applied everywhere. Item-by-item mechanics in micro_checklist.md Stage 29.9.

- **Renamed where unambiguous, held back where not.** Most of the list (Accounting→Financial Accounting, Buying→Procurement, Point of Sale→POS, HR & Assets→HRM, Purchase Orders→Purchase Order, Sales Invoices→Sales Invoice, Vendor Invoices→Vendor Invoice, Customers→Customer, Goods Receipt (GRN)→Goods Receipt, Transfers→Stock Transfer, Bin Master→Bin) applied cleanly across `public/index.html`, matching hardcoded page titles in `public/app.js`, and the breadcrumb references in `USER_SOP.md`/`USER_GUIDE.md`/`UAT_CHECKLIST.md`/`ADMIN_SOP.md`. Three items would have created a real UI defect if applied literally, so were flagged back to the user instead of guessed at: "Settings"→"Setup" would have collided with the already-existing, differently-scoped "Setup" master-data flyout; "Finance / GL"→"Chart of Accounts" would have mislabeled a screen that only had Trial Balance/Accounting Periods tabs; "Order Management"→"Sales Order" would have mislabeled a broader channel-order-to-invoice workbench as a single document screen.
- **User's answers, and what each produced**: keep admin module as "Settings" (no change); build a real Chart of Accounts tab rather than rename the page it didn't fit (new third `FINANCE_TABS` tab, `renderChartOfAccountsPanel` in `app.js`, reusing the existing `/api/v1/finance/trial-balance` endpoint's already-complete per-account rows — no new backend endpoint needed, since that query already `LEFT JOIN`s every `gl_accounts` row regardless of postings); keep "Order Management" (no change). Not building a new CRM module — none exists yet beyond a scoped POS loyalty feature (13.13d), and inventing one is scope beyond a naming pass.
- **Verified**: `node --check public/app.js` clean throughout. Live-verified the new Chart of Accounts tab end-to-end via a scratch Playwright script against the running dev server (`manager1` login, token injected into `localStorage` rather than driving the login form) — all three Finance tabs present in order, 25 real seeded GL accounts render correctly, zero console errors, and the "Finance / GL" page title stays accurate since it still covers all three tabs. Full repo grep confirmed no stray old labels remain outside the three intentionally-held items.

## 67. Stage 29.8 — the last two loophole items closed: status-transition map + JWT session staleness (2026-07-29, code)

Both remaining items in `ERP_LOOPHOLES_ANALYSIS.md` were blocked on a decision rather than on work — one marked `[needs design decision]`, the other deferred by standing policy. The user asked to be asked; they chose opt-in-strict enforcement scoped to transactional doctypes **and** masters, a middleware live-state re-check over a jti denylist, and multi-key signing rotation. Item mechanics in micro_checklist.md Stage 29.8.

- **The transition map extends what already existed rather than adding a second mechanism.** `StatusTransitionRule` (Stage 26.12.9) already had exactly the right shape and an admin screen; it was simply capped at four OMS entities and only ever read for order cancellation. Widening `entity` to free text (validated in code, so a doctype added tomorrow is governable without a migration) plus one `doctype_meta.strict_status_transitions` boolean was the whole schema change. Enforcement went on `ValidateTransactionalRules` — the single choke point every generic-doc write already passes — so coverage is by construction rather than by remembering to add a call. Rejections reuse `GLOBAL-0019`, which the error catalog already defined as "Invalid status transition"; no new code was invented for a scenario that was already named.
- **Opt-in strict is what makes it shippable.** A globally fail-closed map would have to be complete on day one or it takes production down, including the engine-driven paths that write statuses in SQL. Fail-open-unless-flagged means the 64 seeded doctypes are enforced and everything else behaves exactly as before, and any doctype can be un-flagged instantly if a matrix turns out wrong.
- **Generating beats listing, and testing proved why.** The masters' `Active`↔`Inactive` pairs and the approval-engine edges are derived from each doctype's own live `options` string. The explicit transactional matrices were first hand-written from `db/migration.sql` — and testing them against a **clone of the dev database** before touching anything showed four doctypes had since gained statuses (`PurchaseOrder`: `Pending Approval`/`Rejected`; `ProductionOrder`: `In Process`; `TransferOrder`: `Packed`; `CycleCountLine`: `Recount Requested`), with a real PurchaseOrder sitting in `Pending Approval` that strict mode would have frozen permanently. The same check found a `SalesInvoice` in status `Active` — not even one of its declared options — which no rule could ever free, since a rule can't name a `from_status` the schema doesn't know. That produced a deliberate escape: a write **leaving** an undeclared status is always allowed, because the destination is still constrained by `ValidateDocument`, so it can only move legacy debris back into a legal state, never into a made-up one. Neither bug would have been visible from reading the code.
- **Session staleness: the honest gap was bigger than "revocation".** `ParseToken` is pure HMAC and never touched the database, so every claim was frozen at issue time. Deactivating a user blocked new logins but left their existing token working for up to `JWT_EXPIRY_HOURS` (24h by default) — the QC report's "Deactivate Employee → login blocked ✅" was true and incomplete. A jti denylist, the obvious answer, would have fixed deactivation and done nothing at all for the role half: a demoted user's token was never revoked in the first place and keeps asserting the old role. Re-reading the user's row per request (30s cache, `AUTH_STATE_CACHE_SECONDS`) fixes both, needs no new table, no logout endpoint and no expiry sweeper, and makes the database authoritative for role and location too. A database failure returns a retryable 503 rather than a 401 — mistaking a replica hiccup for a mass deactivation would turn a blip into an outage.
- **Key rotation, without trusting the payload to pick the key.** `JWT_SECRET_<n>`: highest number signs, all configured keys verify, so rotation is add-key → wait one token TTL → delete-old-key with nobody logged out. Plain `JWT_SECRET` is unchanged and, with no numbered key set, the emitted token is byte-identical to before — the feature is inert until opted into. Verification tries every key rather than selecting by the token's own `kid`, deliberately: `kid` sits inside the payload and is attacker-controlled until the HMAC has already passed, so using it to choose the trust anchor means trusting unverified input to pick what verifies it. With a handful of keys the extra comparisons are microseconds. `kid` is still emitted so ops can tell which key signed a token; it is simply never load-bearing.
- **Two existing tests were wrong and were fixed, not accommodated.** Both minted tokens for user ids with no row in `users` — now correctly a dead session. One even documented itself as "indistinguishable at the JWT layer from a token a real successful login would have issued", which stopped being true; both now seed the row a real login would have required.

**Verified**: `go build ./...` + `go vet ./...` clean; 19 new engine subtests and 2 end-to-end wiring tests (a deactivated user's still-valid token → 401 `GLOBAL-0009`; an `RFQ` `Draft`→`Closed` → 422 `GLOBAL-0019` with the stored document provably unchanged, while the listed `Draft`→`Sent` still succeeds). Full suite green across 5 consecutive runs with strict mode live on all 64 flagged doctypes. One transient failure in an unrelated robotics test appeared once and did not reproduce in five subsequent runs — consistent with this repo's known shared-DB fixture-debris flakiness, not with anything in this change.

Then live-verified over real HTTP against a throwaway server instance (:8099, real dev DB, stopped afterwards): the blocked and allowed RFQ transitions behaved as above with the stored row confirmed unchanged on the rejection; an active token served 200, the *same* token returned 401 once the account was deactivated, and — after reactivating and demoting HR/Admin→Cashier — that same token was refused 403 on an HR/Admin endpoint while still claiming HR/Admin in its own payload, which is the concrete demonstration that the denylist approach would have left that half open. Key rotation was walked end to end as documented: with keys 1 and 2 both configured, tokens signed by either were accepted (`kid=1`/`kid=2` visible in the claims); after deleting `JWT_SECRET_1` and restarting, the key-1 token was rejected 401 and the key-2 token still served 200 — i.e. the retirement step is what actually retires, exactly as `deploy/erp.env.example` tells an operator.

---

## 66. Stage 29.7 — QC exhaustive-report follow-ups: gl_postings reporting index + production sslmode docs (2026-07-29, code + docs)

User asked for the two non-blocking observations in `QC_EXHAUSTIVE_REPORT.md` (O1 index, O2 sslmode) to be fixed, plus any other open items. Item-by-item mechanics in micro_checklist.md Stage 29.7.

- **O1 — the recommendation as written did not work, and measuring is what surfaced that.** QC asked for a composite `(account_code, created_at)` on `gl_postings` to stop the trial balance full-scanning at 10M+ rows. Built it, seeded a throwaway database with 1M postings, and the planner produced a *byte-identical* Seq Scan plan with the index present and absent. The reason is structural: these reports need `debit`/`credit` for every matching row, so a non-covering index buys a heap visit per row — strictly more expensive than the seq scan it would replace, which is why the planner correctly refused it. Shipping the literal recommendation would have added write cost to every GL posting and bought nothing. The index that ships is `(account_code, created_at) INCLUDE (debit, credit, cost_center)`; the payload is what flips the plans to index-only scans (`Heap Fetches: 0`). `cost_center` earns its 8MB because `GetCostCenterPL` groups by it; `department` is deliberately excluded — nothing groups by it, so it would be dead weight on every row.
- **The index alone is still inert — the date predicates had to change with it.** Nine gl_postings report queries wrote their range as `created_at::date BETWEEN $1 AND $2`. The cast wraps the indexed column in a function call, so the date could never enter an `Index Cond`; the queries scanned every posting for an account and threw most away with a filter. Rewritten to the equivalent half-open `created_at >= $1::date AND created_at < ($2::date + 1)` across `finance_reports_stage26.go`, `gl_cost_center.go`, `reports.go` and `report_definitions.go`, with the convention written down once at the top of `finance_reports_stage26.go` so the next query inherits it instead of re-introducing the cast. Index and rewrite are one change in two files' worth of edits — neither half is worth shipping alone. At 1M rows: P&L for a quarter 87ms/2,480 buffers → 5ms/162; cash flow 109ms/20,402 → 4ms/933; cost-centre P&L 95ms/20,359 → 9ms/158.
- **Equivalence was proved, not assumed.** Every rewritten predicate was run side by side with its original against the seeded data: zero differing rows on P&L and balance sheet, matching totals on cash flow, GST net balance, GST drill-down and the statutory export, and an explicit boundary case confirming a posting at `23:59:59.999` on the final day is still counted while `00:00:00` the next day is not — the one thing a half-open rewrite can plausibly get wrong.
- **A latent duplicate-index trap, found by testing the tenant path.** `ProvisionTenantSchema` clones with `LIKE ... INCLUDING ALL`, which copies indexes but **does not preserve their names** — a tenant provisioned after this migration carries the index under a server-generated name. A name-only `CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS` would therefore not recognise it and would build a second, redundant copy on that tenant's ledger, doubling the write cost of every posting. The backfill loop matches on index *definition* instead. (The pre-existing `gl_postings_idempotency_key_idx` clones the same way, so this is a general property of the provisioning path, not something this migration introduced.)
- **O2 — sslmode.** `deploy/erp.env.example` now states why the shipped `sslmode=disable` is valid *only* for a same-box loopback DB, what to use off-box (`require` minimum, `verify-full` + `sslrootcert=` preferred), and how to check what was actually negotiated via `pg_stat_ssl`. It also records a driver-specific gotcha worth knowing: lib/pq's default when `sslmode` is omitted is `require`, **not** psql/libpq's `prefer` — verified in `lib/pq@v1.12.3/ssl.go`, where `mode == ""` shares a branch with `SSLModeRequire`. `deploy/README.md` A2 gained a matching callout at the step where the reader decides where Postgres lives, since that is where the decision is actually made.
- **What O1 does not fix, stated plainly.** `GetTrialBalance` aggregates the entire ledger with no date filter, so it must touch every row by definition and no index can help; bounding it to a period or as-of date is the real fix and is a product decision, now logged as 29.7.4. `GetStatutoryGLExport` filters on date alone with no `account_code`, so an account-leading index cannot seek for it — left to seq-scan deliberately, since it is an async background CSV export nobody waits on, and a second index would tax every posting write to speed it up (29.7.5).

**Verified**: `go build ./...` + `go vet ./engines/...` clean; `go test ./... -p 1` fully green, including the finance GL-totals test that has historically been flaky. Migration applied to two throwaway databases and to the dev DB across both its tenant schemas — applies clean, re-runs are no-ops, a schema without `gl_postings` is skipped, and a tenant provisioned post-migration inherits the index without a re-run duplicating it.
